DeLaM : A Dependent Layered Modal Type Theory for Meta-programming
Jason Z. S. Hu, Brigitte Pientka

Abstract
. We scale layered modal type theory to dependent types, introducing DeLaM , dependent layered modal type theory. This type theory is novel in that we have one uniform type theory in which we can not only compose and execute code, but also intensionally analyze the code of types and terms. The latter in particular allows us to write tactics as meta-programs and use regular libraries when writing tactics. DeLaM provides a sound foundation for proof assistants to support type-safe tactic mechanism.



1.
Introduction

Hu and Pientka (2024a)
develop a layered modal type theory which supports pattern
matching on code. A critical idea lying in this system is
the layering principle
. The layering principle begins with a core language, e.g. simply typed

\lambda
-calculus (STLC). Then we extend the core language with a layer of the
\square
modality which supports
meta-programming and intensional analysis. Though only two layers are demonstrated by
Hu and Pientka (2024a)
, in principle, this
extension can be iterated indefinite number of times, forming an arbitrary
n
-layered
modal type theory.

In another dimension, instead of adding more and more layers, we could also increase
the expressive power of the core language. One interesting candidate for a core language is Martin-Löf type theory (MLTT). MLTT is the foundation for many type-theory-based proof assistants, including Coq,
Agda and Lean. Treating MLTT as the core language and applying the layering principle to it could
yield a dependently typed system that allows to meta-program and intensionally analyze
code of itself without forgoing the consistency of the overall system. This feature gives a solid foundation for proof assistants to support truly type-safe
meta-programming. Due to the layering principle, libraries written for bare MLTT can also be used during
meta-programming. For example, we can use the same data structures for natural numbers and lists for
both programs and meta-programs. Meanwhile in reality, e.g. in Coq, we have at least four unexchangeable notions
natural numbers: the natural numbers defined inductively in Gallina, Ltac’s natural
numbers, failure levels and hint database’s search levels. Therefore, we can foresee that the layering principle also has the additional benefit
in engineering.

In this technical report, we first extend the previous layered modal type theory with
context variables. They are necessary to enable recursion on code. We justify the decidability of conversion checking following

Abel
et al. (2017)
’s reducibility proof. We then scale the setup all the way to MLTT, introducing
DeLaM
,

De
pendent
La
yered
M
odal type theory. We then scale the reducibility proof to
DeLaM
and therefore justify its
decidability of conversion checking. A corollary is the consistency of
DeLaM
, hence showing that this type theory can
be used as a foundation for proof assistants.

2.
Supporting Context Variables

Hu and Pientka (2024a)
present a layered modal type theory which supports pattern
matching on code and establish a normalization proof via a presheaf model. However, this form of intentional analysis is not in the most desired form: we cannot
perform recursion on the structure of code. This limitations comes from two aspects:

•

STLC lacks a generic notion of types. When we do recursion on, for example, a
\lambda
expression, the type of the

\lambda
and the type of the body necessarily differ. Therefore, we are not able to formulate a recursion principle without type variables
as in System F, or a variable of type
Set
in dependent type theory. This problem naturally goes away if we employ a stronger core type system, so it is
not our primary concern.

•

However, System F or dependent types does not give us a way to capture local
contexts using a variable. Consider again the
\lambda
case, even if we have type variables, the recursion on
the body is in an extended local context, so for the recursion to work, we must be
able to capture
context variables
, which capture local contexts as global
variables.

In this section, we focus on context variables. We develop the syntactic theory for our
2
-layered contextual model type theory with
context variables, and show its consistency via a reducibility predicate argument.

2.1.
Well-formedness of Contexts and Types

With context variables, the type theory becomes “slightly” dependently typed, in
that both global and local contexts, and types can depend on context variables, so
their well-formedness requires dedicated judgments. The syntax of contexts and types is:

(Layer,
i\in[0,1]
)

\displaystyle i

(Local variables)

\displaystyle x,y

(Global variables)

\displaystyle u

(Context variables)

\displaystyle g

(Types,
Typ
)

\displaystyle S,T

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\ \texttt{Nat}\;|\;\square(\Gamma\vdash T)\;|\;S\longrightarrow T\;|\;(g:\textsf{Ctx})\Rightarrow T

(Global bindings)

\displaystyle B

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\ u:(\Gamma\vdash T)\;|\;g:\textsf{Ctx}

(Global contexts)

\displaystyle\Phi,\Psi

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\ \cdot\;|\;\Phi,B

(Local contexts)

\displaystyle\Gamma,\Delta

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\ \cdot\;|\;g\;|\;\Gamma,x:T

Their well-formedness judgments are:

\frac{}{\vdash\cdot}\par\frac{\vdash\Psi}{\vdash\Psi,g:\textsf{Ctx}}\par\frac{\Psi\vdash_{0}\Gamma\quad\Psi\vdash_{0}T}{\vdash\Psi,u:(\Gamma\vdash T)}\par\frac{\vdash\Psi}{\Psi\vdash_{i}\cdot}\par\frac{\vdash\Psi\quad g:\textsf{Ctx}\in\Psi}{\Psi\vdash_{i}g}\par\frac{\Psi\vdash_{i}\Gamma\quad\Psi\vdash_{i}T}{\Psi\vdash_{i}\Gamma,x:T}\par\frac{\vdash\Psi}{\Psi\vdash_{i}\texttt{Nat}}\par\frac{\Psi\vdash_{i}S\quad\Psi\vdash_{i}T}{\Psi\vdash_{i}S\longrightarrow T}\par\frac{\Psi\vdash_{0}\Delta\quad\Psi\vdash_{0}T}{\Psi\vdash_{1}\square(\Delta\vdash T)}\par\frac{\Psi,g:\textsf{Ctx}\vdash_{1}T}{\Psi\vdash_{1}(g:\textsf{Ctx})\Rightarrow T}

\vdash\Psi
states the well-formedness of a global context
\Psi
. There are two kinds of bindings in a global context: either
x:(\Gamma\vdash T)
as in
previous modal type theory, or
g:\textsf{Ctx}
which is a context variable representing a local
context. Eventually,
g
will be substituted by a concrete and well-formed local context. Due to the introduction of context variables, the system clearly becomes
dependently typed.
\Psi\vdash_{i}\Gamma
states the well-formedness of a local context
\Gamma
at
layer
i
. Since we are dealing with a
2
-layered system now, we know
i\in[0,1]
. The base case of a local context can either be an empty local context, or be
a well-scoped context variable. The layer
i
is propagated to the well-formedness judgment of types
\Psi\vdash_{i}T
,
which states that
T
is well-formed in
\Psi
at layer
i
. Notice that the well-formedness of
T
does not depend on any local context. This judgment essentially combines
T\ \texttt{core}
and
T\ \texttt{type}

predicates in
Hu and Pientka (2024a)
, with context variables taken into consideration. Context variables is introduced by a special meta-function type
(g:\textsf{Ctx})\Rightarrow T
, which pushes a context variable
g
to the global context. This type can be seen as a “meta-function space” in which we define macros or
meta-programs, so intuitively, this type and its terms can only live at layer
1
. We prove the presupposition lemma of these three judgments:

Lemma 2.1 (Presupposition).

•

If
\Psi\vdash_{i}\Gamma
, then
\vdash\Psi
.

•

If
\Psi\vdash_{i}T
, then
\vdash\Psi
.

Lemma 2.2 (Lifting).

•

If
\Psi\vdash_{0}\Gamma
, then
\Psi\vdash_{1}\Gamma
.

•

If
\Psi\vdash_{0}T
, then
\Psi\vdash_{1}T
.

2.2.
Weakenings

Similar to previous layered modal type theories, we need two notions of weakenings: a global one and
a local one. In this section, due to context variables, we change the definition of weakenings
based on their counterparts in
Hu and Pientka (2024a)
:

(Global weakenings)

\displaystyle\gamma

\displaystyle:=\textsf{id}\;|\;q(\gamma)\;|\;p(\gamma)

(Local weakenings)

\displaystyle\tau

\displaystyle:=\textsf{id}\;|\;q(\tau)\;|\;p(\tau)

We will use a global weakening
p(\textsf{id})
in the typing rule of meta-functions in

\Gamma[p(\textsf{id})]
to account for the insertion of
g:\textsf{Ctx}
to the global context

\Psi
. In the following, we examine the properties of weakenings. First, we define the composition of weakenings. We only define the one for global weakenings and the one for local weakenings is
completely identical:

\displaystyle\textsf{id}\circ\gamma^{\prime}

\displaystyle:=\gamma^{\prime}

\displaystyle\gamma\circ\textsf{id}

\displaystyle:=\gamma

\displaystyle p(\gamma)\circ q(\gamma^{\prime})

\displaystyle:=p(\gamma\circ\gamma^{\prime})

\displaystyle q(\gamma)\circ q(\gamma^{\prime})

\displaystyle:=q(\gamma\circ\gamma^{\prime})

\displaystyle\gamma\circ p(\gamma^{\prime})

\displaystyle:=p(\gamma\circ\gamma^{\prime})

Lemma 2.3 (Associativity).

•

(\gamma\circ\gamma^{\prime})\circ\gamma^{\prime\prime}=\gamma\circ(\gamma^{\prime}\circ\gamma^{\prime\prime})

•

(\tau\circ\tau^{\prime})\circ\tau^{\prime\prime}=\tau\circ(\tau^{\prime}\circ\tau^{\prime\prime})

Then we apply global weakenings to types and contexts:

\displaystyle\texttt{Nat}[\gamma]

\displaystyle:=\texttt{Nat}

\displaystyle S\longrightarrow T[\gamma]

\displaystyle:=(S[\gamma])\longrightarrow(T[\gamma])

\displaystyle\square(\Gamma\vdash T)[\gamma]

\displaystyle:=\square(\Gamma[\gamma]\vdash T[\gamma])

\displaystyle(g:\textsf{Ctx})\Rightarrow T[\gamma]

\displaystyle:=(g:\textsf{Ctx})\Rightarrow(T[q(\gamma)])

\displaystyle\cdot[\gamma]

\displaystyle:=\cdot

(properly weakened depending on the name representation)

\displaystyle g[\gamma]

\displaystyle:=g

\displaystyle\Gamma,x:T[\gamma]

\displaystyle:=(\Gamma[\gamma]),x:(T[\gamma])

This definition of global weakenings admits the following lemma:

Lemma 2.4 (Algebra of global weakenings).

•

T[\textsf{id}]=T

•

\Gamma[\textsf{id}]=\Gamma

•

T[\gamma][\gamma^{\prime}]=T[\gamma\circ\gamma^{\prime}]

•

\Gamma[\gamma][\gamma^{\prime}]=\Gamma[\gamma\circ\gamma^{\prime}]

The well-formedness of weakenings is given by the following rules:

\frac{\vdash\Psi}{\textsf{id}:\Psi\Longrightarrow_{g}\Psi}\par\frac{\gamma:\Psi\Longrightarrow_{g}\Phi\quad\Psi\vdash B}{p(\gamma):\Psi,B\Longrightarrow_{g}\Phi}\par\frac{\gamma:\Psi\Longrightarrow_{g}\Phi\quad\Phi\vdash B\quad\Psi\vdash B[\gamma]}{q(\gamma):\Psi,B[\gamma]\Longrightarrow_{g}\Phi,B}\\
\par\frac{\Psi\vdash_{i}\Gamma}{\textsf{id}:\Psi;\Gamma\Longrightarrow_{i}\Gamma}\par\frac{\tau:\Psi;\Gamma\Longrightarrow_{i}\Delta\quad\Psi\vdash_{i}T}{p(\tau):\Psi;\Gamma,x:T\Longrightarrow_{i}\Delta}\par\frac{\tau:\Psi;\Gamma\Longrightarrow_{i}\Delta\quad\Psi\vdash_{i}T}{q(\tau):\Psi;\Gamma,x:T\Longrightarrow_{i}\Delta,x:T}

where the well-formedness of global bindings
\Psi\vdash B
is given as follows:

\frac{\vdash\Psi}{\Psi\vdash g:\textsf{Ctx}}\par\frac{\Psi\vdash_{0}\Gamma\quad\Psi\vdash_{0}T}{\Psi\vdash u:(\Gamma\vdash T)}

The identity case for local weakenings is slightly more complex because we must take
the context variables into consideration.

Then we can prove the following global weakening lemma:

Lemma 2.5 (Global weakenings).

•

If
\Phi\vdash_{i}\Gamma
and
\gamma:\Psi\Longrightarrow_{g}\Phi
, then
\Psi\vdash_{i}\Gamma[\gamma]
.

•

If
\Phi\vdash_{i}T
and
\gamma:\Psi\Longrightarrow_{g}\Phi
, then
\Psi\vdash_{i}T[\gamma]
.

Proof.

Mutual induction on
\Phi\vdash_{i}\Gamma
and
\Phi\vdash_{i}T
.
∎

The action of global weakenings on a type at layer
0
is no-op:

Lemma 2.6.

If
\Psi\vdash_{0}T
, then
T[\gamma]=T
.

Global weakenings do not really affect local weakenings:

Lemma 2.7.

If
\gamma:\Psi\Longrightarrow_{g}\Phi
and
\tau:\Phi;\Gamma\Longrightarrow_{i}\Delta
, then
\tau:\Psi;\Gamma[\gamma]\Longrightarrow_{i}\Delta[\gamma]
.

Proof.

Induction on
\tau:\Phi;\Gamma\Longrightarrow_{i}\Delta
.
∎

The actions of local weakenings only affect terms so they will be looked into in the
next section, after we consider the syntax of the type theory.

2.3.
Syntax and Typing

In this section, we define the syntax of the type theory with context variables. To isolate concerns, we use
letbox
for elimination, instead of pattern matching. Nevertheless, pattern matching on code should work with proper adjustments to our
development:

(Natural numbers,
\mathbb{N}
)

\displaystyle m

(Local substitutions)

\displaystyle\delta

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\cdot_{g?}^{m}\;|\;\textsf{wk}_{g}^{m}\;|\;\delta,t/x

(Terms,
Exp
)

\displaystyle s,t

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\ x\;|\;u^{\delta}

(natural numbers)

\displaystyle\;|\;\textsf{zero}\;|\;\textsf{succ}~t

(box)

\displaystyle\;|\;\texttt{box}\ t\;|\;\texttt{letbox}\ {u}\shortleftarrow s\ \texttt{in}\ t

(functions)

\displaystyle\;|\;\lambda x.t\;|\;s\ t

(meta-functions)

\displaystyle\;|\;\Lambda g.t\;|\;t~\$~\Gamma

Similar to before, we have natural numbers as our base type. To construct and eliminate meta-functions, we have
\Lambda g.t
and
t~\$~\Gamma

respectively. Since we have contextual types, each global variable must be associated with a local
substitution. In a local substitution, due to how a local context is structured, there are also two
base cases: it can either be an empty local substitution
\cdot_{g?}^{m}
, or a weakening
\textsf{wk}^{m}_{g}
of a
context variable
g
. The number
m
associated with both cases are the number of local weakening
p
’s. Effectively,
m
equals to the length of the codomain local context in the typing
judgment, as we will specify later in this section. This number can be fetched from a local substitution by the following function:

\displaystyle\widehat{\cdot_{g?}^{m}}

\displaystyle:=m

\displaystyle\widehat{\textsf{wk}_{g}^{m}}

\displaystyle:=m

\displaystyle\widehat{\delta,t/x}

\displaystyle:=\widehat{\delta}

In addition,
\cdot
is optionally associated with a context variable
g
. If there is such a
g
, it is the base case of the codomain local context. These information are necessary in order to define the local and global substitution
operations. We rely on the following function to return the context variable inside of a local
substitution, if it exists:

\displaystyle:=g?

\displaystyle:=g

\displaystyle:=\widecheck{\delta}

Next, we define the action of global weakenings on terms and local substitutions:

\displaystyle x[\gamma]

\displaystyle:=x

(with
u
properly weakened depending on name representation)

\displaystyle u^{\delta}[\gamma]

\displaystyle:=u^{\delta[\gamma]}

\displaystyle\textsf{zero}[\gamma]

\displaystyle:=\textsf{zero}

\displaystyle\textsf{succ}~t[\gamma]

\displaystyle:=\textsf{succ}~(t[\gamma])

\displaystyle\texttt{box}\ t[\gamma]

\displaystyle:=\texttt{box}\ (t[\gamma])

\displaystyle\texttt{letbox}\ {u}\shortleftarrow s\ \texttt{in}\ t[\gamma]

\displaystyle:=\texttt{letbox}\ {u}\shortleftarrow s[\gamma]\ \texttt{in}\ (t[q(\gamma)])

\displaystyle\lambda x.t[\gamma]

\displaystyle:=\lambda x.{(t[\gamma])}

\displaystyle t~s[\gamma]

\displaystyle:=(t[\gamma])~(s[\gamma])

\displaystyle\Lambda g.t[\gamma]

\displaystyle:=\Lambda g.{(t[q(\gamma)])}

\displaystyle t~\$~\Gamma[\gamma]

\displaystyle:=(t[\gamma])~\$~(\Gamma[\gamma])

(with
g
properly weakened)

\displaystyle\textsf{wk}_{g}^{m}[\gamma]

\displaystyle:=\textsf{wk}_{g}^{m}

(with
g
properly weakened if exists)

\displaystyle\cdot_{g?}^{m}[\gamma]

\displaystyle:=\cdot_{g?}^{m}

\displaystyle\delta,t/x[\gamma]

\displaystyle:=(\delta[\gamma]),(t[\gamma])/x

The application of global weakenings satisfy the following lemma:

Lemma 2.8 (Algebra of global weakenings).

•

t[\textsf{id}]=t

•

\delta[\textsf{id}]=\delta

•

t[\gamma][\gamma^{\prime}]=t[\gamma\circ\gamma^{\prime}]

•

\delta[\gamma][\gamma^{\prime}]=\delta[\gamma\circ\gamma^{\prime}]

Applying local weakenings on terms and local substitutions is defined as follows:

(properly weakened)

\displaystyle x[\tau]

\displaystyle:=x

\displaystyle u^{\delta}[\tau]

\displaystyle:=u^{\delta[\tau]}

\displaystyle\textsf{zero}[\tau]

\displaystyle:=\textsf{zero}

\displaystyle\textsf{succ}~t[\tau]

\displaystyle:=\textsf{succ}~(t[\tau])

\displaystyle\texttt{box}\ t[\tau]

\displaystyle:=\texttt{box}\ t

\displaystyle\texttt{letbox}\ {u}\shortleftarrow s\ \texttt{in}\ t[\tau]

\displaystyle:=\texttt{letbox}\ {u}\shortleftarrow s[\tau]\ \texttt{in}\ (t[\tau])

\displaystyle\lambda x.t[\tau]

\displaystyle:=\lambda x.{(t[q(\tau)])}

\displaystyle t~s[\tau]

\displaystyle:=(t[\tau])~(s[\tau])

\displaystyle\Lambda g.t[\tau]

\displaystyle:=\Lambda g.{(t[\tau])}

\displaystyle t~\$~\Gamma[\tau]

\displaystyle:=(t[\tau])~\$~\Gamma

(where
m^{\prime}
is the number of
p
constructor in
\tau
)

\displaystyle\textsf{wk}_{g}^{m}[\tau]

\displaystyle:=\textsf{wk}_{g}^{m+m^{\prime}}

(where
m^{\prime}
is the number of
p
constructor in
\tau
)

\displaystyle\cdot_{g?}^{m}[\tau]

\displaystyle:=\cdot_{g?}^{m+m^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\delta,t/x[\tau]

\displaystyle:=(\delta[\tau]),(t[\tau])/x

The application of local weakenings satisfy the following lemma:

Lemma 2.9 (Algebra of local weakenings).

•

t[\gamma][\tau]=t[\tau][\gamma]

•

\delta[\gamma][\tau]=\delta[\tau][\gamma]

•

t[\tau][\tau^{\prime}]=t[\tau\circ\tau^{\prime}]

•

\delta[\tau][\tau^{\prime}]=\delta[\tau\circ\tau^{\prime}]

•

t[\delta][\tau]=t[\delta[\tau]]

•

(\delta\circ\delta^{\prime})[\tau]=\delta\circ(\delta^{\prime}[\tau])

Then the weakenings of dual-contexts are defined as just tuples of global and local
weakenings:

\displaystyle\frac{\gamma:\Psi\Longrightarrow_{g}\Phi\quad\tau:\Psi;\Gamma\Longrightarrow_{i}\Delta[\gamma]}{\gamma;\tau:\Psi;\Gamma\Longrightarrow_{i}\Phi;\Delta}

\displaystyle t[\gamma;\tau]:=t[\gamma][\tau]

\displaystyle\delta[\gamma;\tau]:=\delta[\gamma][\tau]

To disambiguate, when we apply a local weakening literal, we usually pair it with an
identity global weakening. For example,
t[\textsf{id};p(\textsf{id})]

locally
weakens
t
with a local weakening
p(\textsf{id})
. When we write
t[p(\textsf{id})]
, we mean that
t
is
globally
weakened by
p(\textsf{id})
. The correctness lemma for these operations can only be proved after defining the typing
rules. In order to define the typing rules, we must first give the definition of global
substitutions of types and local contexts:

(Global substitutions)

\displaystyle\sigma

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\cdot\;|\;\sigma,t/u\;|\;\sigma,\Gamma/g

Global substitutions can obviously be globally weakened by iteratively applying a
global weakening to the terms and local contexts within. The global substitution operation of types and local contexts is defined as follows:

\displaystyle\texttt{Nat}[\sigma]

\displaystyle:=\texttt{Nat}

\displaystyle S\longrightarrow T[\sigma]

\displaystyle:=(S[\sigma])\longrightarrow(T[\sigma])

\displaystyle\square(\Gamma\vdash T)[\sigma]

\displaystyle:=\square(\Gamma[\sigma]\vdash T[\sigma])

\displaystyle(g:\textsf{Ctx})\Rightarrow T[\sigma]

\displaystyle:=(g:\textsf{Ctx})\Rightarrow(T[\sigma[p(\textsf{id})],g/g])

\displaystyle\cdot[\sigma]

\displaystyle:=\cdot

(lookup
g
in
\sigma
; undefined if
g
is not bound or result is not a
local context)

\displaystyle g[\sigma]

\displaystyle:=\sigma(g)

\displaystyle\Gamma,x:T[\sigma]

\displaystyle:=(\Gamma[\sigma]),x:(T[\sigma])

For consistency of notations, we often write
q(\sigma)
for
\sigma[p(\textsf{id})],g/g
or

\sigma[p(\textsf{id})],u^{\textsf{id}}/u
. This notation relates similar operations of weakenings and substitutions. The global substitutions on types satisfy the following lemma:

Lemma 2.10 (Algebra of Global Substitutions).

•

T[\gamma][\gamma^{\prime}]=T[\gamma\circ\gamma]

•

\sigma[\gamma][\gamma^{\prime}]=\sigma[\gamma\circ\gamma]

•

T[\sigma][\gamma]=T[\sigma[\gamma]]

•

\Gamma[\sigma][\gamma]=\Gamma[\sigma[\gamma]]

•

t[\sigma][\gamma]=t[\sigma[\gamma]]

•

\delta[\sigma][\gamma]=\delta[\sigma[\gamma]]

Next, we give the application operation of local substitutions on terms and
composition of local substitutions:

(lookup of
x
in
\delta
)

\displaystyle x[\delta]

\displaystyle:=\delta(x)

\displaystyle u^{\delta^{\prime}}[\delta]

\displaystyle:=u^{\delta^{\prime}\circ\delta}

\displaystyle\textsf{zero}[\delta]

\displaystyle:=\textsf{zero}

\displaystyle\textsf{succ}~t[\delta]

\displaystyle:=\textsf{succ}~(t[\delta])

\displaystyle\lambda x.t[\delta]

\displaystyle:=\lambda x.(t[\delta[\textsf{id};p(\textsf{id})],x/x])

\displaystyle t~s[\delta]

\displaystyle:=(t[\delta])~(s[\delta])

\displaystyle\texttt{box}\ t[\delta]

\displaystyle:=\texttt{box}\ t

\displaystyle\texttt{letbox}\ {u}\shortleftarrow s\ \texttt{in}\ t[\delta]

\displaystyle:=\texttt{letbox}\ {u}\shortleftarrow s[\delta]\ \texttt{in}\ (t[\delta[p(\textsf{id})]])

\displaystyle\Lambda g.t[\delta]

\displaystyle:=\Lambda g.(t[\delta[(p(\textsf{id}))]])

\displaystyle t~\$~\Gamma[\delta]

\displaystyle:=(t[\delta])~\$~\Gamma

\displaystyle\textsf{wk}_{g}^{m}\circ\delta

\displaystyle:=\textsf{wk}_{g}^{\widehat{\delta}}

\displaystyle\cdot^{m}\circ\delta

\displaystyle:=\cdot_{\widecheck{\delta}}^{\widehat{\delta}}

\displaystyle\cdot_{g}^{m}\circ\delta

\displaystyle:=\cdot_{g}^{\widehat{\delta}}

\displaystyle(\delta^{\prime},t/x)\circ\delta

\displaystyle:=(\delta^{\prime}\circ\delta),t[\delta]/x

Similarly, we might also write
q(\delta)
for
\delta[\textsf{id};p(\textsf{id})],x/x
. Notice that in the definition of composition, we make use of the
\widehat{\delta}

function to fetch the number of weakenings. This number is used in the application operation of global substitutions given below
in the application of global substitutions to specify the number of local
p

weakenings when a context variable is substituted by a concrete context. In the composition of
\cdot^{m}
, we use
\widecheck{\delta}
to query whether

\delta
’s codomain context starts from a context variable. If it does, then we use that context variable in the result of the composition. Local substitutions and global weakenings interact in the following way:

Lemma 2.11 (Algebra of Local Substitutions).

•

t[\delta][\gamma]=(t[\gamma])[\delta[\gamma]]

•

(\delta\circ\delta^{\prime})[\gamma]=(\delta[\gamma])\circ(\delta^{\prime}[\gamma])

Now, we define the application of global substitutions to terms and local substitutions:

\displaystyle x[\sigma]

\displaystyle:=x

(lookup of
u
in
\sigma
)

\displaystyle u^{\delta}[\sigma]

\displaystyle:=\sigma(u)[\delta[\sigma]]

\displaystyle\textsf{zero}[\sigma]

\displaystyle:=\textsf{zero}

\displaystyle\textsf{succ}~t[\sigma]

\displaystyle:=\textsf{succ}~(t[\sigma])

\displaystyle\lambda x.t[\sigma]

\displaystyle:=\lambda x.(t[\sigma])

\displaystyle t~s[\sigma]

\displaystyle:=(t[\sigma])~(s[\sigma])

\displaystyle\texttt{box}\ t[\sigma]

\displaystyle:=\texttt{box}\ (t[\sigma])

\displaystyle\texttt{letbox}\ {u}\shortleftarrow s\ \texttt{in}\ t[\sigma]

\displaystyle:=\texttt{letbox}\ {u}\shortleftarrow s[\sigma]\ \texttt{in}\ (t[\sigma[p(\textsf{id})],u^{\textsf{id}}/u])

\displaystyle\Lambda g.t[\sigma]

\displaystyle:=\Lambda g.(t[\sigma[(p(\textsf{id}))],g/g])

\displaystyle t~\$~\Gamma[\sigma]

\displaystyle:=(t[\sigma])~\$~(\Gamma[\sigma])

(defined only when
\sigma(g)
is a local context)

\displaystyle\textsf{wk}_{g}^{m}[\sigma]

\displaystyle:=\textsf{id}_{\sigma(g)}[\textsf{id};p^{m}(\textsf{id})]

\displaystyle\cdot^{m}[\sigma]

\displaystyle:=\cdot^{m}

(if
\sigma(g)=\Gamma
and
\Gamma
ends with a
\cdot
)

\displaystyle\cdot_{g}^{m}[\sigma]

\displaystyle:=\cdot^{|\Gamma|+m}

(if
\sigma(g)=\Gamma
and
\Gamma
ends with a
g^{\prime}
)

\displaystyle\cdot_{g}^{m}[\sigma]

\displaystyle:=\cdot_{g^{\prime}}^{|\Gamma|+m}

\displaystyle(\delta,t/x)[\sigma]

\displaystyle:=(\delta[\sigma]),t[\sigma]/x

In the definition of global substitution operation, we make use of the local identity
substitution, which is defined through local weakening substitutions as below:

\displaystyle\textsf{wk}^{m}_{\cdot}

\displaystyle:=\cdot^{m}

\displaystyle\textsf{wk}^{m}_{g}

\displaystyle:=\textsf{wk}_{g}^{m}

\displaystyle\textsf{wk}^{m}_{\Gamma,x:T}

\displaystyle:=\textsf{wk}^{1+m}_{\Gamma},x/x

The local identity substitution is defined as a special case of the local weakening
substitutions by setting
m
to be
0
:

\displaystyle\textsf{id}_{\Gamma}:=\textsf{wk}^{0}_{\Gamma}

Though we make heavy use of symbol overloading, but hopefully the exact meanings of
symbols should be disambiguated by the surrounding textual contexts. Usually, the symbols are designed such that their behaviors remain the same for their
ambiguous readings (e.g. various uses of
id
and
wk
).

At last, we need to define the global identity substitution before giving the typing
rules. The global identity substitution is defined in the same principle; it is a special
case of the global weakening substitutions:

\displaystyle\textsf{wk}^{m}_{\cdot}

\displaystyle:=\cdot

\displaystyle\textsf{wk}^{m}_{\Psi,g:\textsf{Ctx}}

\displaystyle:=\textsf{wk}_{\Psi}^{1+m},g/g

\displaystyle\textsf{wk}^{m}_{\Psi,u:(\Gamma\vdash T)}

\displaystyle:=\textsf{wk}^{1+m}_{\Psi},u^{\textsf{id}_{\Gamma}[p^{1+m}(\textsf{id})]}/u

Notice that in the cons case for
u:(\Gamma\vdash T)
, the local identity substitution

\textsf{id}_{\Gamma}
must be weakened by
p^{1+m}(\textsf{id})
, because its global typing
environment is weakened by
\textsf{wk}^{1+m}_{\Psi}
, which takes the same effect. This weakening is necessary to make the typing to go through. Then identity is just a special case:

\displaystyle\textsf{id}_{\Psi}:=\textsf{wk}^{0}_{\Psi}

We sometimes omit the subscript for different
id
, as we know their effects on
types, local contexts, terms and local substitutions are just
identity
.

The composition of global substitutions is defined intuitively:

\displaystyle\cdot\circ\sigma^{\prime}

\displaystyle:=\cdot

\displaystyle(\sigma,t/u)\circ\sigma^{\prime}

\displaystyle:=(\sigma\circ\sigma^{\prime}),t[\sigma^{\prime}]/u

\displaystyle(\sigma,\Gamma/g)\circ\sigma^{\prime}

\displaystyle:=(\sigma\circ\sigma^{\prime}),\Gamma[\sigma^{\prime}]/g

Essentially, composition just iteratively applies
\sigma^{\prime}
to all terms and contexts
in the first global substitution. Notice that composition can only be defined, after the applications of global
substitutions to both terms and local contexts are defined. This definition does lead to some complication when we tries to prove the global
substitution lemma of terms and local substitutions in the next section.

At last, the following gives the typing rules of terms and local substitutions.

\frac{\Psi\vdash_{i}\Gamma\quad x:T\in\Gamma}{\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{i}x:T}\par\frac{\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{i}\delta:\Delta\quad u:(\Delta\vdash T)\in\Psi}{\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{i}u^{\delta}:T}\par\frac{\Psi\vdash_{i}\Gamma}{\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{i}\textsf{zero}:\texttt{Nat}}\par\frac{\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{i}t:\texttt{Nat}}{\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{i}\textsf{succ}~t:\texttt{Nat}}\par\frac{\Psi;\Gamma,x:S\vdash_{i}t:T}{\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{i}\lambda x.t:S\longrightarrow T}\par\frac{\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{i}t:S\longrightarrow T\quad\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{i}s:S}{\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{i}t\ s:T}\par\frac{\Psi\vdash_{1}\Gamma\quad\Psi;\Delta\vdash_{0}t:T}{\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{1}\texttt{box}\ t:\square(\Delta\vdash T)}\par\frac{\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{1}s:\square(\Delta\vdash T)\quad\hbox{\pagecolor{light-gray}$\Psi\vdash_{0}\Delta$}\quad\hbox{\pagecolor{light-gray}$\Psi\vdash_{0}T$}\quad\Psi\vdash_{1}T^{\prime}\quad\Psi,u:(\Delta\vdash T);\Gamma[p(\textsf{id})]\vdash_{1}t:T^{\prime}[p(\textsf{id})]}{\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{1}\texttt{letbox}\ {u}\shortleftarrow s\ \texttt{in}\ t:T^{\prime}}\par\frac{\Psi\vdash_{1}\Gamma\quad\Psi,g:\textsf{Ctx};\Gamma[p(\textsf{id})]\vdash_{1}t:T}{\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{1}\Lambda g.t:(g:\textsf{Ctx})\Rightarrow T}\par\frac{\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{1}t:(g:\textsf{Ctx})\Rightarrow T\quad\Psi\vdash_{0}\Delta}{\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{1}t~\$~\Delta:T[\textsf{id}_{\Psi},\Delta/g]}\par\frac{\Psi\vdash_{i}\Gamma\quad\text{$\Gamma$ ends with $\cdot$}\quad|\Gamma|=m}{\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{i}\cdot^{m}:\cdot}\par\frac{\Psi\vdash_{i}\Gamma\quad g:\textsf{Ctx}\in\Psi\quad\text{$\Gamma$ ends with $g$}\quad|\Gamma|=m}{\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{i}\cdot_{g}^{m}:\cdot}\par\frac{\Psi\vdash_{i}\Gamma\quad g:\textsf{Ctx}\in\Psi\quad\text{$\Gamma$ ends with $g$}\quad|\Gamma|=m}{\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{i}\textsf{wk}_{g}^{m}:g}\par\frac{\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{i}\delta:\Delta\quad\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{i}t:T}{\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{i}\delta,t/x:\Delta,x:T}

In the typing rules, there are premises highlighted by
shades
. These shaded premises are necessary to establish the theorem of
presupposition

or
syntactic validity
. After establishing presupposition, these premises can be derived from other premises
and thus technically can be omitted afterwards. Moreover, in the rule for global variables, the lookup of a global context
\Psi
must
consider the effect of global weakenings as follows:

\frac{}{u:B[p(\textsf{id})]\in\Psi,u:B}\par\frac{u:B\in\Psi}{u:B[p(\textsf{id})]\in\Psi,u^{\prime}:B^{\prime}}

The typing rules also support lifting:

Lemma 2.12 (Lifting).

•

If
\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{0}t:T
, then
\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{1}t:T
.

•

If
\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{0}\delta:\Delta
, then
\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{1}\delta:\Delta
.

This lemma ensures that terms at layer
0
are
included
in layer
1
.

Typing rules for global substitutions are defined as follows:

\frac{\vdash\Psi}{\Psi\vdash\cdot:\cdot}\par\frac{\Psi\vdash\sigma:\Phi\quad\Psi\vdash_{0}\Gamma\quad\Psi\vdash_{0}T\quad\Psi;\Gamma[\sigma]\vdash_{0}t:T[\sigma]}{\Psi\vdash\sigma,t/u:\Phi,u:(\Gamma\vdash T)}\par\frac{\Psi\vdash\sigma:\Phi\quad\Psi\vdash_{0}\Gamma}{\Psi\vdash\sigma,\Gamma/g:\Phi,g:\textsf{Ctx}}

In the next section, we establish a set of syntactic properties as a basic sanity
check of the definitions, which are also useful in later section proving normalization.

2.4.
Syntactic Properties of 2-layered Modal Type Theory with Context Variables

In this section, we list syntactic properties that eventually leads to the
substitution lemma of terms and local substitutions for global substitutions. This lemma is our “benchmark” to ensure that the rules for the system make sense. During the process, we must establish other necessary syntactic properties. We elaborate the proofs an important and selected few. Other proofs in this section have been mechanized in Agda.

Lemma 2.13.

•

If
n
is the length of
\sigma^{\prime}
, then
t[q^{n}(p(id))][\sigma,t/u,\sigma^{\prime}]=t[q^{n}(p(id))][\sigma,\Gamma/g,\sigma^{\prime}]=t[\sigma,\sigma^{\prime}]
.

•

If
n
is the length of
\sigma^{\prime}
, then
\delta[q^{n}(p(id))][\sigma,t/u,\sigma^{\prime}]=\delta[q^{n}(p(id))][\sigma,\Gamma/g,\sigma^{\prime}]=\delta[\sigma,\sigma^{\prime}]
.

This lemma allows to skip a binding in the middle of a global substitution according
to a global weakening.

A similar lemma holds for local substitutions:

Lemma 2.14.

•

If
n
is the length of
\delta^{\prime}
, then
t[q^{n}(p(id))][\delta,t/x,\delta^{\prime}]=t[\delta,\delta^{\prime}]
.

•

If
n
is the length of
\delta^{\prime}
, then
\delta[q^{n}(p(id))]\circ(\delta,t/x,\delta^{\prime})=\delta\circ(\delta,\delta^{\prime})
.

Lemma 2.15 (Composition of Global Substitutions).

•

T[\sigma][\sigma^{\prime}]=T[\sigma\circ\sigma^{\prime}]

•

\Gamma[\sigma][\sigma^{\prime}]=\Gamma[\sigma\circ\sigma^{\prime}]

Lemma 2.16 (Composition and Associativity of Local Substitutions).

•

t[\delta][\delta^{\prime}]=t[\delta\circ\delta^{\prime}]

•

(\delta\circ\delta^{\prime})\circ\delta^{\prime\prime}=\delta\circ(\delta^{\prime}\circ\delta^{\prime\prime})

Lemma 2.17 (Typing of Local Weakening Substitutions).

If
\Psi\vdash_{i}\Delta,\Gamma
, then
\Psi;\Delta,\Gamma\vdash_{i}\textsf{wk}^{|\Gamma|}_{\Delta}:\Delta
.

The corollary is the well-typedness of local identity substitution:

Corollary 2.18.

If
\Psi\vdash_{i}\Gamma
, then
\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{i}\textsf{id}_{\Gamma}:\Gamma
.

The next few questions require well-formedness or typing judgments to work:

Lemma 2.19.

•

If
\Psi\vdash_{i}T
, then
T[\textsf{wk}^{n}_{\Psi}]=T[p^{n}(\textsf{id})]
.

•

If
\Psi\vdash_{i}\Gamma
, then
\Gamma[\textsf{wk}^{n}_{\Psi}]=\Gamma[p^{n}(\textsf{id})]
.

This lemma proves that the global weakening substitutions behaves exactly like global
weakenings.

Lemma 2.20 (Naturality).

•

If
\Psi,g:\textsf{Ctx}\vdash_{i}T
and
\gamma:\Phi\Longrightarrow_{g}\Psi
, then

T[\textsf{id}_{\Psi},\Gamma/g][\gamma]=T[q(\gamma)][\textsf{id}_{\Phi},\Gamma[\gamma]/g]
.

•

If
\Psi,g:\textsf{Ctx}\vdash_{i}\Delta
and
\gamma:\Phi\Longrightarrow_{g}\Psi
, then

\Delta[\textsf{id}_{\Psi},\Gamma/g][\gamma]=\Delta[q(\gamma)][\textsf{id}_{\Phi},\Gamma[\gamma]/g]
.

The naturality lemma finds correspondence in the characterization of a presheaf
category of a type theory in general, which instructs how
q
weakenings can be used
to swap a global weakening and a global substitution.

Lemma 2.21 (Local Identity).

•

If
\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{i}t:T
, then
t[\textsf{id}_{\Gamma}]=t
.

•

If
\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{i}\delta:\Delta
, then
\delta\circ\textsf{id}_{\Gamma}=\delta
.

This lemma shows that the local identity substitution has no effect on terms and that
the right identity property of local substitutions.

Next, we establish the global weakening lemma for typing rules:

Lemma 2.22 (Global weakenings).

•

If
\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{i}t:T
and
\gamma:\Psi^{\prime}\Longrightarrow_{g}\Psi
, then

\Psi^{\prime};\Gamma[\gamma]\vdash_{i}t[\gamma]:T[\gamma]
.

•

If
\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{i}\delta:\Delta
and
\gamma:\Psi^{\prime}\Longrightarrow_{g}\Psi
, then

\Psi^{\prime};\Gamma[\gamma]\vdash_{i}\delta[\gamma]:\Delta[\gamma]
.

Proof.

Mutual induction on
\Phi;\Gamma\vdash_{i}t:T
and
\Phi;\Gamma\vdash_{i}\delta:\Delta
. We only consider a few interesting cases:

Case

\frac{\Phi;\Gamma\vdash_{1}s:\square(\Delta\vdash T)\quad\Phi\vdash_{1}T^{\prime}\quad\Phi,u:(\Delta\vdash T);\Gamma[p(\textsf{id})]\vdash_{1}t:T^{\prime}[p(\textsf{id})]}{\Phi;\Gamma\vdash_{1}\texttt{letbox}\ {u}\shortleftarrow s\ \texttt{in}\ t:T^{\prime}}

(by IH)

\displaystyle\Psi;\Gamma[\gamma]\vdash_{1}s[\gamma]:\square(\Delta\vdash T)[\gamma]

(by IH)

\displaystyle\Psi,u:(\Delta[\gamma]\vdash T[\gamma]);\Gamma[p(\textsf{id})][q(\gamma)]\vdash_{1}t[q(\gamma)]:T^{\prime}[p(\textsf{id})][q(\gamma)]

(by computation)

\displaystyle\Psi,u:(\Delta[\gamma]\vdash T[\gamma]);\Gamma[p(\gamma)]\vdash_{1}t[q(\gamma)]:T^{\prime}[p(\gamma)]

\displaystyle\Psi,u:(\Delta[\gamma]\vdash T[\gamma]);\Gamma[\gamma][p(\textsf{id})]\vdash_{1}t[q(\gamma)]:T^{\prime}[\gamma][p(\textsf{id})]

(by constructor)

\displaystyle\Psi;\Gamma[\gamma]\vdash_{1}\texttt{letbox}\ {u}\shortleftarrow s\ \texttt{in}\ t[\gamma]:T^{\prime}[\gamma]

Case

\frac{\Phi,g:\textsf{Ctx};\Gamma[p(\textsf{id})]\vdash_{1}t:T}{\Phi;\Gamma\vdash_{1}\Lambda g.t:(g:\textsf{Ctx})\Rightarrow T}

(by typing rules)

\displaystyle q(\gamma):\Psi^{\prime},g:\textsf{Ctx}\Longrightarrow_{g}\Psi,g:\textsf{Ctx}

(by IH)

\displaystyle\Psi^{\prime},g:\textsf{Ctx};\Gamma[p(\textsf{id})][q(\gamma)]\vdash_{1}t[q(\gamma)]:T[q(\gamma)]

(by algebraic law)

\displaystyle\Psi^{\prime},g:\textsf{Ctx};\Gamma[p(\textsf{id})\circ q(\gamma)]\vdash_{1}t[q(\gamma)]:T[q(\gamma)]

\displaystyle\Psi^{\prime},g:\textsf{Ctx};\Gamma[p(\gamma)]\vdash_{1}t[q(\gamma)]:T[q(\gamma)]

\displaystyle\Psi^{\prime},g:\textsf{Ctx};\Gamma[\gamma][p(\textsf{id})]\vdash_{1}t[q(\gamma)]:T[q(\gamma)]

(by typing rule)

\displaystyle\Psi^{\prime};\Gamma[\gamma]\vdash_{1}\Lambda g.(t[q(\gamma)]):(g:\textsf{Ctx})\Rightarrow(T[q(\gamma)])

Case

\frac{\Phi;\Gamma\vdash_{1}t:(g:\textsf{Ctx})\Rightarrow T\quad\Phi\vdash_{0}\Delta}{\Phi;\Gamma\vdash_{1}t~\$~\Delta:T[\textsf{id}_{\Phi},\Delta/g]}

(by IH)

\displaystyle\Psi;\Gamma[\gamma]\vdash_{1}t[\gamma]:(g:\textsf{Ctx})\Rightarrow T[\gamma]

(by
Lemma

2.5
)

\displaystyle\Psi\vdash_{0}\Delta[\gamma]

(by constructor)

\displaystyle\Psi;\Gamma[\gamma]\vdash_{1}(t[\gamma])~\$~(\Delta[\gamma]):T[q(\gamma)][\textsf{id}_{\Psi},\Delta[\gamma]/g]

(by naturality)

\displaystyle\Psi;\Gamma[\gamma]\vdash_{1}(t[\gamma])~\$~(\Delta[\gamma]):T[\textsf{id}_{\Phi},\Delta/g][\gamma]

∎

Lemma 2.23 (Global Weakening).

If
\Psi\vdash\sigma:\Phi
and
\gamma:\Psi^{\prime}\Longrightarrow_{g}\Psi
, then
\Psi^{\prime}\vdash\sigma[\gamma]:\Phi
.

Lemma 2.24 (Global Weakening Substitutions).

If
\vdash\Psi,\Phi
, then
\Psi,\Phi\vdash\textsf{wk}^{|\Phi|}_{\Psi}:\Psi
.

Proof.

This lemma is actually requires a bit preliminaries to establish and so this is the
earliest point where this lemma can be proven. From
\vdash\Psi,\Phi
, we know
\vdash\Psi
, which we do induction on. We consider only one case:

\frac{\Psi\vdash_{0}\Gamma\quad\Psi\vdash_{0}T}{\vdash\Psi,u:(\Gamma\vdash T)}

(by definition)

\displaystyle\textsf{wk}^{|\Phi|}_{\Psi,u:(\Gamma\vdash T)}=\textsf{wk}^{1+|\Phi|}_{\Psi},u^{\textsf{id}_{\Gamma}[p^{1+|\Phi|}(\textsf{id})]}/u

(by IH)

\displaystyle\Psi,u:(\Gamma\vdash T),\Phi\vdash\textsf{wk}^{1+|\Phi|}_{\Psi}:\Psi

At last, we must prove
\Psi,u:(\Gamma\vdash T),\Phi;\Gamma[\textsf{wk}^{1+|\Phi|}_{\Psi}]\vdash_{0}u^{\textsf{id}_{\Gamma}[p^{1+|\Phi|}(\textsf{id})]}:T[\textsf{wk}^{1+|\Phi|}_{\Psi}]
. But we know that this goal is the same as the following due to

Lemma

2.19
:

\displaystyle\Psi,u:(\Gamma\vdash T),\Phi;\Gamma[p^{1+|\Phi|}(\textsf{id})]\vdash_{0}u^{\textsf{id}_{\Gamma}[p^{1+|\Phi|}(\textsf{id})]}:T[p^{1+|\Phi|}(\textsf{id})]

It remains to prove that the substitution is well-typed:

\displaystyle\Psi,u:(\Gamma\vdash T),\Phi;\Gamma[p^{1+|\Phi|}(\textsf{id})]\vdash_{0}\textsf{id}_{\Gamma}[p^{1+|\Phi|}(\textsf{id})]:\Gamma[p^{1+|\Phi|}(\textsf{id})]

This goal is immediate due to
Lemmas

2.21
and
2.22
.
∎

Corollary 2.25.

If
\vdash\Psi
, then
\Psi\vdash\textsf{id}_{\Psi}:\Psi
.

Finally, we can establish the presupposition of terms and local substitutions:

Lemma 2.26 (Presupposition).

•

If
\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{i}t:T
, then
\Psi\vdash_{i}\Gamma
and
\Psi\vdash_{i}T
.

•

If
\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{i}\delta:\Delta
, then
\Psi\vdash_{i}\Gamma
and
\Psi\vdash_{i}\Delta
.

Proof.

We do a mutual induction.
∎

Next, we need a similar lemma to
Lemma

2.19
but for terms and local
substitutions:

Lemma 2.27.

•

If
\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{i}t:T
, then
t[\textsf{wk}^{n}_{\Psi}]=t[p^{n}(\textsf{id})]
.

•

If
\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{i}\delta:\Delta
, then
\delta[\textsf{wk}^{n}_{\Psi}]=\delta[p^{n}(\textsf{id})]
.

Proof.

The proof of this lemma requires an intrigued generalization in order to handle
extensions of global contexts due to
letbox
and
\Lambda
. Details of the generalization are technical and too elaborate to put in this
technical report, and thus we choose to leave them in the Agda mechanization for
readers’ reference.
∎

Next, we should verify the identity rules of composition of global substitutions. Notice that by applying
Lemmas

2.19
and
2.27
, we obtain the
right identity immediately:

Lemma 2.28.

If
\Psi\vdash\sigma:\Phi
, then
\sigma\circ\textsf{id}_{\Psi}=\sigma
.

The left identity, on the other hand, requires certain generalization which
must incorporate global weakenings. We again leave the details in the Agda mechanization:

Lemma 2.29.

If
\Psi\vdash\sigma:\Phi
, then
\textsf{id}_{\Phi}\circ\sigma=\sigma
.

Another useful equation is that global substitutions and local weakenings commute:

Lemma 2.30 (Commutativity of Global Substitutions and Local Weakenings).

•

If
\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{i}t:T
and
\tau:\Psi;\Delta\Longrightarrow_{i}\Gamma
, then

t[\tau][\sigma]=t[\sigma][\tau]
.

•

If
\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{i}\delta:\Gamma^{\prime}
and
\tau:\Psi;\Delta\Longrightarrow_{i}\Gamma
, then

\delta[\tau][\sigma]=\delta[\sigma][\tau]
.

Next, we move on to the global substitution lemma for terms and local substitutions. Prior to that, we must first show the local weakening lemma and local substitution lemma:

Lemma 2.31 (Local Weakenings).

•

If
\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{i}t:T
and
\tau:\Psi;\Delta\Longrightarrow_{i}\Gamma
, then

\Psi;\Delta\vdash_{i}t[\tau]:T
.

•

If
\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{i}\delta:\Delta^{\prime}
and
\tau:\Psi;\Delta\Longrightarrow_{i}\Gamma
, then

\Psi;\Delta\vdash_{i}\delta[\tau]:\Delta^{\prime}
.

Lemma 2.32 (Local Substitutions).

•

If
\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{i}t:T
and
\Psi;\Delta\vdash_{i}\delta:\Gamma
, then

\Psi;\Delta\vdash_{i}t[\delta]:T
.

•

If
\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{i}\delta:\Delta
and
\Psi;\Gamma^{\prime}\vdash_{i}\delta^{\prime}:\Gamma
, then

\Psi;\Gamma^{\prime}\vdash_{i}\delta\circ\delta^{\prime}:\Delta
.

Proof.

We do a mutual induction. Notice that in this lemma, it is somewhat more cumbersome to establish the proof for
local substitutions. When
\delta=\cdot^{m}
, then we must reason about the properties of

\widecheck{\delta^{\prime}}
. If
\widecheck{\delta^{\prime}}=g
for some
g
, then we must show that both
\Gamma
and

\Gamma^{\prime}
start with this
g
. The details are given in the Agda mechanization.
∎

Finally, we give the global substitution lemma:

Lemma 2.33 (Global Substitutions).

•

If
\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{i}t:T
and
\Psi^{\prime}\vdash\sigma:\Psi
, then

\Psi^{\prime};\Gamma[\sigma]\vdash_{i}t[\sigma]:T[\sigma]
.

•

If
\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{i}\delta:\Delta
and
\Psi^{\prime}\vdash\sigma:\Psi
, then

\Psi^{\prime};\Gamma[\sigma]\vdash_{i}\delta[\sigma]:\Delta[\sigma]
.

Proof.

We do a mutual induction. We consider a few interesting cases:

Case

\frac{\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{1}s:\square(\Delta\vdash T)\quad\Psi\vdash_{1}T^{\prime}\quad\Psi,u:(\Delta\vdash T);\Gamma[p(\textsf{id})]\vdash_{1}t:T^{\prime}[p(\textsf{id})]}{\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{1}\texttt{letbox}\ {u}\shortleftarrow s\ \texttt{in}\ t:T^{\prime}}

(by IH)

\displaystyle\Psi^{\prime};\Gamma[\sigma]\vdash_{1}s[\sigma]:\square(\Delta\vdash T)[\sigma]=\square(\Delta[\sigma]\vdash T[\sigma])

(by typing rule)

\displaystyle\Psi^{\prime},u:(\Delta[\sigma]\vdash T[\sigma])\vdash\sigma[p(\textsf{id})],u^{\textsf{id}_{\Delta[\sigma[p(\textsf{id})]]}}/u:\Psi,u:(\Delta\vdash T)

(by IH)

\displaystyle\Psi^{\prime},u:(\Delta[\sigma]\vdash T[\sigma]);\Gamma[p(\textsf{id})][\sigma[p(\textsf{id})],u^{\textsf{id}_{\Delta[\sigma[p(\textsf{id})]]}}/u]\vdash_{1}t[\sigma[p(\textsf{id})],u^{\textsf{id}_{\Delta[\sigma[p(\textsf{id})]]}}/u]:T^{\prime}[p(\textsf{id})][\sigma[p(\textsf{id})],u^{\textsf{id}_{\Delta[\sigma[p(\textsf{id})]]}}/u]

(by
Lemma

2.13
)

\displaystyle\Psi^{\prime},u:(\Delta[\sigma]\vdash T[\sigma]);\Gamma[\sigma[p(\textsf{id})]]\vdash_{1}t[\sigma[p(\textsf{id})],u^{\textsf{id}_{\Delta[\sigma[p(\textsf{id})]]}}/u]:T^{\prime}[\sigma[p(\textsf{id})]]

(by algebraic laws)

\displaystyle\Psi^{\prime},u:(\Delta[\sigma]\vdash T[\sigma]);\Gamma[\sigma][p(\textsf{id})]\vdash_{1}t[\sigma[p(\textsf{id})],u^{\textsf{id}_{\Delta[\sigma][p(\textsf{id})]}}/u]:T^{\prime}[\sigma][p(\textsf{id})]

(by typing rule)

\displaystyle\Psi^{\prime};\Gamma[\sigma]\vdash_{1}\texttt{letbox}\ {u}\shortleftarrow s\ \texttt{in}\ t:T^{\prime}[\sigma]

Case

\frac{\Psi\vdash_{1}\Gamma\quad\Psi,g:\textsf{Ctx};\Gamma[p(\textsf{id})]\vdash_{1}t:T}{\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{1}\Lambda g.t:(g:\textsf{Ctx})\Rightarrow T}

Basically we do the same as above, but instead of providing a global substitution
of terms, we provide a global substitution of a local context.

Case

\frac{\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{1}t:(g:\textsf{Ctx})\Rightarrow T\quad\Psi\vdash_{0}\Delta}{\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{1}t~\$~\Delta:T[\textsf{id}_{\Psi},\Delta/g]}

(by IH)

\displaystyle\Psi^{\prime};\Gamma[\sigma]\vdash_{1}t[\sigma]:(g:\textsf{Ctx})\Rightarrow T[\sigma]=(g:\textsf{Ctx})\Rightarrow(T[\sigma[p(\textsf{id})],g/g])

(by IH)

\displaystyle\Psi^{\prime}\vdash_{0}\Delta[\sigma]

(by typing rule)

\displaystyle\Psi^{\prime};\Gamma[\sigma]\vdash_{1}t~\$~\Delta[\sigma]:T[\sigma[p(\textsf{id})],g/g][\textsf{id}_{\Psi^{\prime}},\Delta[\sigma]/g]

Finally we must show the following equation:

\displaystyle T[\sigma[p(\textsf{id})],g/g][\textsf{id}_{\Psi^{\prime}},\Delta[\sigma]/g]=T[\textsf{id}_{\Psi},\Delta/g][\sigma]

We reason as follows:

\displaystyle T[\sigma[p(\textsf{id})],g/g][\textsf{id}_{\Psi^{\prime}},\Delta[\sigma]/g]

\displaystyle=T[(\sigma[p(\textsf{id})],g/g)\circ(\textsf{id}_{\Psi^{\prime}},\Delta[\sigma]/g)]

\displaystyle=T[(\sigma[p(\textsf{id})]\circ(\textsf{id}_{\Psi^{\prime}},\Delta[\sigma]/g)),\Delta[\sigma]]

(by
Lemma

2.13
)

\displaystyle=T[(\sigma\circ\textsf{id}_{\Psi^{\prime}}),\Delta[\sigma]]

(by right identity)

\displaystyle=T[\sigma,\Delta[\sigma]]

On the right hand side,

\displaystyle T[\textsf{id}_{\Psi},\Delta/g][\sigma]

\displaystyle=T[(\textsf{id}_{\Psi}\circ\sigma),\Delta[\sigma]/g]

(by left identity)

\displaystyle=T[\sigma,\Delta[\sigma]/g]

Thus both sides agree.

Case

\frac{\Psi\vdash_{i}\Gamma\quad g:\textsf{Ctx}\in\Psi\quad\text{$\Gamma$ ends with $g$}\quad|\Gamma|=m}{\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{i}\cdot_{g}^{m}:\cdot}

In this case, we must look up
g
in
\sigma
, and branch depending on

\sigma(g)
.

Subcase

If
\sigma(g)=g^{\prime},\Delta
meaning that
\sigma(g)
ends with a context
variable
g^{\prime}
, then we must construct a typing judgment for
\cdot_{g^{\prime}}^{m^{\prime}}

for some
m^{\prime}
. In this case, say if
\Gamma=g,\Gamma^{\prime}
, then
\Gamma[\sigma]=g^{\prime},\Delta,(\Gamma^{\prime}[\sigma])
. Therefore,
m^{\prime}=|\Delta|+|\Gamma^{\prime}[\sigma]|=|\Delta|+m
.

Subcase

If
\sigma(g)=\cdot,\Delta
, then we proceed similarly except that we
must construct a typing judgment for
\cdot^{m^{\prime}}
instead.

∎

Lemma 2.34 (Distributivity of Global Substitutions).

•

If
\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{i}t:T
,
\Psi;\Delta\vdash_{i}\delta:\Gamma
and

\Phi\vdash\sigma:\Psi
, then

t[\delta][\sigma]=(t[\sigma][\delta[\sigma]])
.

•

If
\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{i}\delta:\Delta
,
\Psi;\Gamma^{\prime}\vdash_{i}\delta^{\prime}:\Gamma

and
\Phi\vdash\sigma:\Psi
, then

(\delta\circ\delta^{\prime})[\sigma]=(\delta[\sigma])\circ(\delta^{\prime}[\sigma])
.

Proof.

We do a mutual induction on
\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{i}t:T
and
\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{i}\delta:\Delta
. The difficulty is coming from the base cases of local substitutions. We must incorporate the shape of
\delta^{\prime}
depending on the cases of
\delta
. We refer the readers to the Agda mechanization for the detailed proof and how we do
case analysis on the base cases.

∎

2.5.
Equivalence Rules

In this section, we describe the equivalence rules. They follow closely to the equivalence rules by
Hu and Pientka (2024a, Sec. 4)
. We only show the rules for the newly added constructs.

\frac{\Psi\vdash_{1}\Gamma\quad\Psi,g:\textsf{Ctx};\Gamma[p(\textsf{id})]\vdash_{1}t\approx t^{\prime}:T}{\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{1}\Lambda g.t\approx\Lambda g.t^{\prime}:(g:\textsf{Ctx})\Rightarrow T}\par\frac{\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{1}t\approx t^{\prime}:(g:\textsf{Ctx})\Rightarrow T\quad\Psi\vdash_{0}\Delta}{\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{1}t~\$~\Delta\approx t^{\prime}~\$~\Delta:T[\textsf{id}_{\Psi},\Delta/g]}\par\frac{\Psi\vdash_{1}\Gamma\quad\Psi,g:\textsf{Ctx};\Gamma[p(\textsf{id})]\vdash_{1}t:T\quad\Psi\vdash_{0}\Delta}{\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{1}(\Lambda g.t)~\$~\Delta\approx t[\textsf{id}_{\Psi},\Delta/g]:T[\textsf{id}_{\Psi},\Delta/g]}\par\frac{\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{1}t:(g:\textsf{Ctx})\Rightarrow T}{\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{1}t\approx\Lambda g.(t[p(\textsf{id})])~\$~g:(g:\textsf{Ctx})\Rightarrow T}

In the rules above, we specify the congruence for meta-abstraction
\Lambda
and the
meta-application. Moreover, they also have
\beta
and
\eta
rules in the expected ways.

We also have a equivalence judgment for local substitutions

\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{1}\delta\approx\delta^{\prime}:\Delta

The only rules are the congruence rules determined by all possible constructors. Both equivalence judgments for terms and local substitutions must be mutually
defined.

Note that there is no need to define the equivalence for global substitutions. Effectively, the equivalence for global substitutions is defined as the equality. This is because all terms stored in a global substitution are at layer
0
, and thus
they do not have meaningful dynamics. Therefore, equivalent global substitutions must also be equal.

We first establish the presupposition lemma for equivalence:

Lemma 2.35 (Presupposition).

•

If
\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{1}t\approx t^{\prime}:T
, then
\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{1}t:T
and
\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{1}t^{\prime}:T
.

•

If
\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{1}\delta\approx\delta^{\prime}:\Delta
, then
\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{1}\delta:\Delta
and
\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{1}\delta^{\prime}:\Delta
.

Proof.

We perform a mutual induction. For the
\beta
and
\eta
rules, we apply substitution lemmas proved in the
previous section.
∎

Lemma 2.36 (Local Weakenings).

•

If
\Psi;\Delta\vdash_{1}t\approx t^{\prime}:T
and
\tau:\Psi;\Gamma\Longrightarrow_{1}\Delta
,
then
\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{1}t[\tau]\approx t^{\prime}[\tau]:T
.

•

If
\Psi;\Delta\vdash_{1}\delta\approx\delta^{\prime}:\Delta^{\prime}
and

\tau:\Psi;\Gamma\Longrightarrow_{1}\Delta
, then

\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{1}\delta[\tau]\approx\delta^{\prime}[\tau]:\Delta^{\prime}
.

Proof.

We follow the local weakening property for typing above.
∎

A counterpart is w.r.t to global weakenings:

Lemma 2.37 (Global Weakenings).

•

If
\Phi;\Gamma\vdash_{1}t\approx t^{\prime}:T
and
\gamma:\Psi\Longrightarrow_{g}\Phi
,
then
\Psi;\Gamma[\gamma]\vdash_{1}t[\gamma]\approx t^{\prime}[\gamma]:T[\gamma]
.

•

If
\Phi;\Gamma\vdash_{1}\delta\approx\delta^{\prime}:\Delta
and
\gamma:\Psi\Longrightarrow_{g}\Phi
,
then
\Psi;\Gamma[\gamma]\vdash_{1}\delta[\gamma]\approx\delta^{\prime}[\gamma]:\Delta[\gamma]
.

Proof.

Similarly, we follow the global weakening property for typing above.
∎

Lemma 2.38 (Congruence of Local Substitutions).

•

If
\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{1}t:T
and
\Psi;\Delta\vdash_{1}\delta\approx\delta^{\prime}:\Gamma
, then

\Psi;\Delta\vdash_{1}t[\delta]\approx t[\delta^{\prime}]:T
.

•

If
\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{1}\delta:\Delta^{\prime}
and

\Psi;\Delta\vdash_{1}\delta^{\prime}\approx\delta^{\prime\prime}:\Gamma
, then

\Psi;\Delta\vdash_{1}\delta\circ\delta^{\prime}\approx\delta\circ\delta^{\prime\prime}:\Delta^{\prime}
.

Proof.

We do a mutual induction on
\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{1}t:T
and
\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{1}\delta:\Delta^{\prime}
. We only consider a few interesting cases.

Case

\frac{\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{1}s:\square(\Delta^{\prime}\vdash T)\quad\Psi\vdash_{1}T^{\prime}\quad\Psi,u:(\Delta^{\prime}\vdash T);\Gamma[p(\textsf{id})]\vdash_{1}t:T^{\prime}[p(\textsf{id})]}{\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{1}\texttt{letbox}\ {u}\shortleftarrow s\ \texttt{in}\ t:T^{\prime}}

In this case, we must use the global weakening lemma above to derive

\Psi;\Delta[p(\textsf{id})]\vdash_{1}\delta[p(\textsf{id})]\approx\delta^{\prime}[p(\textsf{id})]:\Gamma[p(\textsf{id})]

and then we apply IH.

Case

For all base cases of local substitutions, we realize that given

\Psi;\Delta\vdash_{1}\delta\approx\delta^{\prime}:\Gamma
,
we have

–

\widecheck{\delta}=\widecheck{\delta^{\prime}}
, and

–

\widehat{\delta}=\widehat{\delta^{\prime}}
.

because they characterize
\Delta
, so the exact
\delta
and
\delta^{\prime}
are
irrelevant.

The target goal follows immediate.

∎

Lemma 2.39 (Local Substitutions).

•

If
\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{1}t\approx t^{\prime}:T
and
\Psi;\Delta\vdash_{1}\delta\approx\delta^{\prime}:\Gamma
, then

\Psi;\Delta\vdash_{1}t[\delta]\approx t^{\prime}[\delta^{\prime}]:T
.

•

If
\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{1}\delta^{\prime\prime}\approx\delta^{\prime\prime\prime}:\Delta^{\prime}
and

\Psi;\Delta\vdash_{1}\delta\approx\delta^{\prime}:\Gamma
, then

\Psi;\Delta\vdash_{1}\delta^{\prime\prime}\circ\delta\approx\delta^{\prime\prime\prime}\circ\delta^{\prime}:\Delta^{\prime}
.

Proof.

We proceed by a mutual induction on
\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{1}t\approx t^{\prime}:T
and

\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{1}\delta^{\prime\prime}\approx\delta^{\prime\prime\prime}:\Delta^{\prime}
. We only look into the
\beta
and
\eta
rule for meta-functions, because we cannot
apply IH:

Case

\frac{\Psi\vdash_{1}\Gamma\quad\Psi,g:\textsf{Ctx};\Gamma[p(\textsf{id})]\vdash_{1}t:T\quad\Psi\vdash_{0}\Delta^{\prime}}{\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{1}(\Lambda g.t)~\$~\Delta^{\prime}\approx t[\textsf{id}_{\Psi},\Delta^{\prime}/g]:T[\textsf{id}_{\Psi},\Delta^{\prime}/g]}

(by local substitution lemma)

\displaystyle\Psi,g:\textsf{Ctx};\Delta[p(\textsf{id})]\vdash_{1}t[\delta[p(\textsf{id})]]\approx t[\delta^{\prime}[p(\textsf{id})]]:T

\displaystyle\Psi;\Delta\vdash_{1}(\Lambda g.(t[\delta[p(\textsf{id})]]))~\$~\Delta^{\prime}\approx t[\delta^{\prime}[p(\textsf{id})]][\textsf{id}_{\Psi},\Delta^{\prime}/g]:T[\textsf{id}_{\Psi},\Delta^{\prime}/g]

Now we have to align up the right hand side. The target right hand side is

t[\textsf{id}_{\Psi},\Delta^{\prime}/g][\delta^{\prime}]

We reason as follows

(by distributivity of global substitutions)

\displaystyle t[\delta^{\prime}[p(\textsf{id})]][\textsf{id}_{\Psi},\Delta^{\prime}/g]

\displaystyle=t[\textsf{id}_{\Psi},\Delta^{\prime}/g][\delta^{\prime}[p(\textsf{id})][\textsf{id}_{\Psi},\Delta^{\prime}/g]]

(by
Lemma

2.13
)

\displaystyle=t[\textsf{id}_{\Psi},\Delta^{\prime}/g][\delta^{\prime}]

and we have the target goal.

Case

\frac{\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{1}t:(g:\textsf{Ctx})\Rightarrow T}{\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{1}t\approx\Lambda g.(t[p(\textsf{id})])~\$~g:(g:\textsf{Ctx})\Rightarrow T}

We apply the local weakening lemma:

\displaystyle\Psi;\Delta\vdash_{1}t[\delta]\approx t[\delta^{\prime}]:T

On the right hand side, we have

\displaystyle\Lambda g.(t[p(\textsf{id})])~\$~g[\delta^{\prime}]

\displaystyle=\Lambda g.(t[p(\textsf{id})][\delta^{\prime}[p(\textsf{id})]])~\$~g

(by algebraic rule)

\displaystyle=\Lambda g.(t[\delta^{\prime}][p(\textsf{id})])~\$~g

∎

Next, we consider the global substitution lemma:

Lemma 2.40 (Global Substitutions).

•

If
\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{1}t\approx t^{\prime}:T
and
\Phi\vdash\sigma:\Psi
, then

\Phi;\Gamma[\sigma]\vdash_{1}t[\sigma]\approx t^{\prime}[\sigma]:T[\sigma]
.

•

If
\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{1}\delta\approx\delta^{\prime}:\Delta
and
\Phi\vdash\sigma:\Psi
, then

\Phi;\Gamma[\sigma]\vdash_{1}\delta[\sigma]\approx\delta^{\prime}[\sigma]:\Delta[\sigma]
.

Proof.

As we have noted above, there is no need for an equivalence judgment between two
global substitutions. Therefore we directly apply the same
\sigma
on both sides. Let us consider the
\beta
and
\eta
rules for meta-functions:

Case

\frac{\Psi\vdash_{1}\Gamma\quad\Psi,g:\textsf{Ctx};\Gamma[p(\textsf{id})]\vdash_{1}t:T\quad\Psi\vdash_{0}\Delta^{\prime}}{\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{1}(\Lambda g.t)~\$~\Delta^{\prime}\approx t[\textsf{id}_{\Psi},\Delta^{\prime}/g]:T[\textsf{id}_{\Psi},\Delta^{\prime}/g]}

\displaystyle\Phi,g:\textsf{Ctx}\vdash\sigma[p(\textsf{id})],g/g:\Psi,g:\textsf{Ctx}

(by global substitution lemma)

\displaystyle\Phi,g:\textsf{Ctx};\Gamma[p(\textsf{id})][\sigma[p(\textsf{id})],g/g]\vdash_{1}t[\sigma[p(\textsf{id})],g/g]:T[\sigma[p(\textsf{id})],g/g]

\displaystyle\Phi,g:\textsf{Ctx};\Gamma[\sigma][p(\textsf{id})]\vdash_{1}t[\sigma[p(\textsf{id})],g/g]:T[\sigma[p(\textsf{id})],g/g]

\displaystyle\Phi\vdash_{0}\Delta^{\prime}[\sigma]

\displaystyle\Phi;\Gamma[\sigma]\vdash_{1}(\Lambda g.(t[\sigma[p(\textsf{id})],g/g]))~\$~(\Delta^{\prime}[\sigma])\approx t[\sigma[p(\textsf{id})],g/g][\textsf{id}_{\Psi},\Delta^{\prime}[\sigma]/g]:T[\sigma[p(\textsf{id})],g/g][\textsf{id}_{\Psi},\Delta^{\prime}[\sigma]/g]

We now reason a few equations:

\displaystyle(\Lambda g.(t[\sigma[p(\textsf{id})],g/g]))~\$~(\Delta^{\prime}[\sigma])=(\Lambda g.t)~\$~\Delta^{\prime}[\sigma]

and

\displaystyle t[\sigma[p(\textsf{id})],g/g][\textsf{id}_{\Psi},\Delta^{\prime}[\sigma]/g]

\displaystyle=t[(\sigma[p(\textsf{id})],g/g)\circ(\textsf{id}_{\Psi},\Delta^{\prime}[\sigma]/g)]

\displaystyle=t[\sigma[p(\textsf{id})]\circ(\textsf{id}_{\Psi},\Delta^{\prime}[\sigma]/g),\Delta^{\prime}[\sigma]/g]

\displaystyle=t[\sigma\circ\textsf{id}_{\Psi},\Delta^{\prime}[\sigma]/g]

\displaystyle=t[\textsf{id}_{\Phi}\circ\sigma,\Delta^{\prime}[\sigma]/g]

\displaystyle=t[\textsf{id}_{\Phi},\Delta^{\prime}/g][\sigma]

Similarly, we have

\displaystyle T[\sigma[p(\textsf{id})],g/g][\textsf{id}_{\Psi},\Delta^{\prime}[\sigma]/g]=T[\textsf{id}_{\Phi},\Delta^{\prime}/g][\sigma]

This concludes the goal.

Case

\frac{\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{1}t:(g:\textsf{Ctx})\Rightarrow T}{\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{1}t\approx\Lambda g.(t[p(\textsf{id})])~\$~g:(g:\textsf{Ctx})\Rightarrow T}

\displaystyle\Phi;\Gamma[\sigma]\vdash_{1}t[\sigma]:(g:\textsf{Ctx})\Rightarrow T[\sigma]

\displaystyle\Phi;\Gamma[\sigma]\vdash_{1}t[\sigma]:(g:\textsf{Ctx})\Rightarrow(T[\sigma[p(\textsf{id})],g/g])

\displaystyle\Phi;\Gamma[\sigma]\vdash_{1}t[\sigma]\approx\Lambda g.t[\sigma][p(\textsf{id})]~\$~g:(g:\textsf{Ctx})\Rightarrow(T[\sigma[p(\textsf{id})],g/g])

We reason the right hand side

\displaystyle\Lambda g.(t[p(\textsf{id})])~\$~g[\sigma]

\displaystyle=\Lambda g.(t[p(\textsf{id})][\sigma[p(\textsf{id})],g/g])~\$~g

\displaystyle=\Lambda g.(t[\sigma[p(\textsf{id})]])~\$~g

\displaystyle=\Lambda g.(t[\sigma][p(\textsf{id})])~\$~g

and hence establish the goal.

∎

2.6.
Weak Head Reduction

To compute the decide whether two terms are equivalence based on the typing rules
above, we take the approach of reducibility candidates. This approach requires a reduction strategy, and then we use a type directed
convertibility checking to determine whether two normal forms after reduction are
equivalent. In this approach, it is sufficient to have a term reduced to weak head normal forms,
specified as below:

(Weak head normal form (
Nf
))

\displaystyle w

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\ v\;|\;\textsf{zero}\;|\;\textsf{succ}~t\;|\;\texttt{box}\ t\;|\;\lambda x.t\;|\;\Lambda g.t

(Neutral form
(
Ne
))

\displaystyle v

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\ x\;|\;u^{\delta}\;|\;v\ t\;|\;\texttt{letbox}\ {u}\shortleftarrow v\ \texttt{in}\ t\;|\;v~\$~\Gamma

Basically the one-step weak head reduction simply takes the
\beta
rules from the
equivalence relations and adds head reduction:

Head reductions:

\frac{\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{1}t\rightsquigarrow t^{\prime}:S\longrightarrow T\quad\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{1}s:S}{\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{1}t\ s\rightsquigarrow t^{\prime}\ s:T}\par\frac{\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{1}s\rightsquigarrow s^{\prime}:\square T\quad\Psi,u:T;\Gamma[p(\textsf{id})]\vdash_{1}t:T^{\prime}[p(\textsf{id})]}{\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{1}\texttt{letbox}\ {u}\shortleftarrow s\ \texttt{in}\ t\rightsquigarrow\texttt{letbox}\ {u}\shortleftarrow s^{\prime}\ \texttt{in}\ t:T^{\prime}}\par\frac{\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{1}t\rightsquigarrow t^{\prime}:(g:\textsf{Ctx})\Rightarrow T\quad\Psi\vdash_{0}\Delta}{\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{1}t~\$~\Delta\rightsquigarrow t^{\prime}~\$~\Delta:T[\textsf{id}_{\Psi},\Delta/g]}

\beta
reductions:

\frac{\Psi;\Gamma,x:S\vdash_{1}t:T\quad\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{1}s:S}{\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{1}(\lambda x.t)\ s\rightsquigarrow t[\textsf{id}_{\Gamma},s/x]:T}\par\frac{\Psi\vdash_{1}\Gamma\quad\Psi,g:\textsf{Ctx};\Gamma[p(\textsf{id})]\vdash_{1}t:T\quad\Psi\vdash_{0}\Delta}{\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{1}(\Lambda g.t)~\$~\Delta\rightsquigarrow t[\textsf{id}_{\Psi},\Delta/g]:T[\textsf{id}_{\Psi},\Delta/g]}\par\frac{\Psi;\cdot\vdash_{0}s:T\quad\Psi\vdash_{1}\Gamma\quad\Psi\vdash_{1}T^{\prime}\quad\Psi,u:T;\Gamma[p(\textsf{id})]\vdash_{1}t:T^{\prime}[p(\textsf{id})]}{\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{1}\texttt{letbox}\ {u}\shortleftarrow\texttt{box}\ s\ \texttt{in}\ t\rightsquigarrow t[\textsf{id}_{\Psi},s/u]:T^{\prime}}

Notice that weak head reduction only occurs at layer
1
, so we do not need to rewrite
down the layer explicitly. This is because
\beta
reduction only occurs at layer
1
and all terms at layer
0

are identified by their exact syntactic structure. We define the reflexive transitive closure
\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{1}t\rightsquigarrow^{\ast}t^{\prime}:T
of weak head reduction
in the usual way. In an implementation, we would repeatedly do one-step weak head reduction, until it
reaches a normal form. To compare two terms of the same type, we would first compute the weak head normal
forms of both sides, and then based on their type, we perform a type-directed
convertibility check. This check also performs
\eta
expansion when necessary, so the resulting algorithm
is complete w.r.t. the equivalence relation.

Notice that the weak head reduction relation is a subrelation of the equivalence
above. Therefore, several theorems are simply carried over. We omit the proofs and only state the theorems below:

Lemma 2.41 (Local Weakenings).

If
\Psi;\Delta\vdash_{1}t\rightsquigarrow t^{\prime}:T
and
\tau:\Psi;\Gamma\Longrightarrow_{1}\Delta
,
then
\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{1}t[\tau]\rightsquigarrow t^{\prime}[\tau]:T
.

Lemma 2.42 (Global Weakenings).

If
\Phi;\Gamma\vdash_{1}t\rightsquigarrow t^{\prime}:T
and
\gamma:\Psi\Longrightarrow_{g}\Phi
,
then
\Psi;\Gamma[\gamma]\vdash_{1}t[\gamma]\rightsquigarrow t^{\prime}[\gamma]:T[\gamma]
.

Theorem 2.43 (Preservation).

If
\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{1}t\rightsquigarrow t^{\prime}:T
, then
\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{1}t:T
and
\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{1}t^{\prime}:T
.

Lemma 2.44 (Local Substitutions).

If
\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{1}t\rightsquigarrow t^{\prime}:T
and
\Psi;\Delta\vdash_{1}\delta:\Gamma
, then

\Psi;\Delta\vdash_{1}t[\delta]\rightsquigarrow t^{\prime}[\delta]:T
.

Lemma 2.45 (Globale Substitutions).

If
\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{1}t\rightsquigarrow t^{\prime}:T
and
\Phi\vdash\sigma:\Psi
, then

\Phi;\Gamma[\sigma]\vdash_{1}t[\sigma]\rightsquigarrow t^{\prime}[\sigma]:T[\sigma]
.

Lemma 2.46 (Uniqueness).

If
\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{1}t\rightsquigarrow t^{\prime}:T
and
\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{1}t\rightsquigarrow t^{\prime\prime}:T
, then
t^{\prime}=t^{\prime\prime}
.

Proof.

Induction on
\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{1}t\rightsquigarrow t^{\prime}:T
and analyze
\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{1}t\rightsquigarrow t^{\prime\prime}:T
.
∎

These conclusions can be generalized to its reflexive transitive closure.

In the next section, we will first look into the logical relations which establish
normalization property, and then we show that this checking strategy indeed is
complete.

3.
Normalization and Convertibility

In the previous section, we have developed the syntactic theory of context
variables and the weak head reduction relation. In this section, our goal is to show the termination of the weak head reduction,
i.e. normalization, develop a type-directed convertibility checking algorithm, and
show that this algorithm is sound and complete w.r.t. the equivalence of terms. In the first step to normalization, we establish the reducibility predicate. This predicate is defined inductively and then recursively in order to describe the
semantic well-formedness of types and terms. Then from the reducibility predicate, we prove the semantic soundness of the model and
prove the normalization property as a corollary. Next, we give the convertibility algorithm as a judgment. This judgment will be shown equivalent to the equivalence of terms, which wraps up
our discussion on simple types.

3.1.
Generic Equivalence

We follow
Abel
et al. (2017)
to define a modular generic equivalence to ease
subsequent proof constructions. Since we do not have type-level computation for this almost simply typed theory, we
only have to be concerned about two kinds of equivalence over terms:

\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{1}t\simeq t^{\prime}:T
describes a generic equivalence between two terms, and

\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{1}v\sim v^{\prime}:T
describes a generic equivalence between two neutral terms. Furthermore,
\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{1}t\simeq t^{\prime}:T
is generalized to
\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{1}\delta\simeq\delta^{\prime}:\Delta

by recursion on
\Delta
, which denotes a generic equivalence between local
substitutions and the base cases are just congruence. The definition is

\frac{\Psi\vdash_{1}\Gamma\quad\text{$\Gamma$ ends with $\cdot$}\quad|\Gamma|=m}{\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{1}\cdot^{m}\simeq\cdot^{m}:\cdot}\par\frac{\Psi\vdash_{1}\Gamma\quad g:\textsf{Ctx}\in\Psi\quad\text{$\Gamma$ ends with $g$}\quad|\Gamma|=m}{\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{1}\cdot_{g}^{m}\simeq\cdot_{g}^{m}:\cdot}\par\frac{\Psi\vdash_{1}\Gamma\quad g:\textsf{Ctx}\in\Psi\quad\text{$\Gamma$ ends with $g$}\quad|\Gamma|=m}{\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{1}\textsf{wk}_{g}^{m}\simeq\textsf{wk}_{g}^{m}:g}\par\frac{\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{1}\delta\simeq\delta^{\prime}:\Delta\quad\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{1}t\simeq t^{\prime}:T}{\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{1}\delta,t/x\simeq\delta^{\prime},t^{\prime}/x:\Delta,x:T}

The generic equivalence will be instantiated twice, once for each layer. Following the layering principle, the laws for layer
0
are subsumed by those for
layer
1
. The reason why we also need an instantiation for layer
0
is that code from layer
0

can be lifted by
letbox
and become a program, so that its computation is
recovered. Therefore, a logical relation is needed to capture its computational behavior.

These two relations must satisfy the following laws. Law statements for
\square
and meta-functions only apply when the generic
equivalence is indexed by layer
1
.

Law 3.1 (Subsumption).

•

If
\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{1}v\sim v^{\prime}:T
, then
\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{1}v\simeq v^{\prime}:T
.

•

If
\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{1}t\simeq t^{\prime}:T
, then
\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{1}t\approx t^{\prime}:T
.

From the subsumption of generic equivalence of terms, we have the subsumption of
generic equivalence of local substitutions as a lemma:

Lemma 3.1 (Subsumption).

If
\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{1}\delta\simeq\delta^{\prime}:\Delta
, then
\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{1}\delta\approx\delta^{\prime}:\Delta
.

Law 3.2 (PER).

Both relations are PERs.

Law 3.3 (Monotonicity).

Given
\gamma;\tau:\Psi;\Gamma\Longrightarrow\Phi;\Delta
, if

\Phi;\Delta\vdash_{1}t\simeq t^{\prime}:T
, or
\Phi;\Delta\vdash_{1}v\sim v^{\prime}:T
, then

\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{1}t[\gamma;\tau]\simeq t^{\prime}[\gamma;\tau]:T[\gamma]
, or

\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{1}v[\gamma;\tau]\sim v^{\prime}[\gamma;\tau]:T[\gamma]
, respectively.

As a lemma, we have monotonicity generalized to local substitutions:

Lemma 3.2 (Monotonicity).

Given
\gamma;\tau:\Psi;\Gamma\Longrightarrow\Phi;\Delta
, if

\Phi;\Delta\vdash_{1}\delta\simeq\delta^{\prime}:\Delta^{\prime}
, then

\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{1}\delta[\gamma;\tau]\simeq\delta^{\prime}[\gamma;\tau]:\Delta^{\prime}[\gamma]
.

Law 3.4 (Weak head closure).

If
\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{1}t\rightsquigarrow^{\ast}w:T
,
\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{1}t^{\prime}\rightsquigarrow^{\ast}w^{\prime}:T
and
\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{1}w\simeq w^{\prime}:T
, then
\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{1}t\simeq t^{\prime}:T
.

Law 3.5 (Congruence).

•

If
\Psi\vdash_{1}\Gamma
, then
\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{1}\textsf{zero}\simeq\textsf{zero}:\texttt{Nat}
.

•

If
\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{1}t\simeq t^{\prime}:\texttt{Nat}
, then
\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{1}\textsf{succ}~t\simeq\textsf{succ}~t^{\prime}:\texttt{Nat}
.

•

If
\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{1}t:S\longrightarrow T
,
\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{1}t^{\prime}:S\longrightarrow T
and

\Psi;\Gamma,x:S\vdash_{1}t[\textsf{id};p(\textsf{id})]~x\simeq t^{\prime}[\textsf{id};p(\textsf{id})]~x:T
, then

\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{1}t\simeq t^{\prime}:S\longrightarrow T
.

•

If
\Psi\vdash_{1}\Gamma
and
\Psi;\Delta\vdash_{0}t:T
,
then
\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{1}\texttt{box}\ t\simeq\texttt{box}\ t:T
.

•

If
\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{1}t:(g:\textsf{Ctx})\Rightarrow T
,
\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{1}t^{\prime}:(g:\textsf{Ctx})\Rightarrow T
and

\Psi,g:\textsf{Ctx};\Gamma\vdash_{1}t[p(\textsf{id})]~\$~g\simeq t^{\prime}[p(\textsf{id})]~\$~g:T
, then

\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{1}t\simeq t^{\prime}:(g:\textsf{Ctx})\Rightarrow T
.

Law 3.6 (Congruence of neutrals).

•

If
\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{1}x:T
, then
\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{1}x\sim x:T
.

•

If
u:(\Delta\vdash T)\in\Psi
and
\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{1}\delta\simeq\delta^{\prime}:\Delta
, then

\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{1}u^{\delta}\sim u^{\delta^{\prime}}:T
.

•

If
\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{1}v\sim v^{\prime}:S\longrightarrow T
and
\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{1}t\simeq t^{\prime}:S
, then

\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{1}v~t\sim v~t^{\prime}:T
.

•

If
\Psi\vdash_{1}T^{\prime}
,
\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{1}v\sim v^{\prime}:\square(\Delta\vdash T)
and

\Psi,u:(\Delta\vdash T);\Gamma\vdash_{1}t\simeq t^{\prime}:T^{\prime}[p(\textsf{id})]
, then

\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{1}\texttt{letbox}\ {u}\shortleftarrow v\ \texttt{in}\ t\sim\texttt{letbox}\ {u}\shortleftarrow v^{\prime}\ \texttt{in}\ t^{\prime}:T^{\prime}
.

•

If
\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{1}v\sim v^{\prime}:(g:\textsf{Ctx})\Rightarrow T
and
\Psi\vdash_{0}\Delta
,
then
\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{1}v~\$~\Delta\sim v^{\prime}~\$~\Delta:T[\textsf{id}_{\Psi},\Delta/g]

From the congruence of local variables, we have that local weakening substitutions,
specifically, local identity substitutions, are reflexive in the generic equivalence:

Lemma 3.3 (Reflexivity of Local Weakening Substitutions).

If
\Psi\vdash_{1}\Delta,\Gamma
, then

\Psi;\Delta,\Gamma\vdash_{1}\textsf{wk}^{|\Gamma|}_{\Delta}\simeq\textsf{wk}^{|\Gamma|}_{\Delta}:\Delta
.

Lemma 3.4 (Reflexivity of Local Identity Substitutions).

If
\Psi\vdash_{1}\Gamma
, then

\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{1}\textsf{id}_{\Gamma}\simeq\textsf{id}_{\Gamma}:\Gamma
.

This further implies

Lemma 3.5 (Congruence of Global Variables).

If
\vdash\Psi
and
u:(\Gamma\vdash T)\in\Psi
, then

\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{1}u^{\textsf{id}_{\Gamma}}\sim u^{\textsf{id}_{\Gamma}}:T
.

We give the first instantiation of both relations as follows:

•

\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{1}t\simeq t^{\prime}:T:=\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{1}t\approx t^{\prime}:T
, and

•

\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{1}v\sim v^{\prime}:T:=\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{1}v\approx v^{\prime}:T
.

The laws are instantiated to appropriate rules. Later, we will instantiate the relations to algorithmic equivalence, showing that the
algorithmic rules are complete, following
Abel
et al. (2017)
. Before that, let us first define the reducibility predicates parameterized by the
generic equivalence relations for terms.

3.2.
Reducibility Predicates

Following
Abel
et al. (2017)
, we first give the semantic well-formedness
of types. The predicates do not need to be defined recursive-inductively, because unlike
dependent types, there is no type-level computation here in our system.

\frac{\vdash\Psi}{\Psi\mathrel{\raisebox{0.01pt}{\rule{0.45pt}{6.88889pt}}{\hskip 1.00006pt\vDash}}_{i}\texttt{Nat}}\par\frac{\forall\gamma:\Phi\Longrightarrow_{g}\Psi~.~\Phi\mathrel{\raisebox{0.01pt}{\rule{0.45pt}{6.88889pt}}{\hskip 1.00006pt\vDash}}_{i}S[\gamma]\quad\forall\gamma:\Phi\Longrightarrow_{g}\Psi~.~\Phi\mathrel{\raisebox{0.01pt}{\rule{0.45pt}{6.88889pt}}{\hskip 1.00006pt\vDash}}_{i}T[\gamma]}{\Psi\mathrel{\raisebox{0.01pt}{\rule{0.45pt}{6.88889pt}}{\hskip 1.00006pt\vDash}}_{i}S\longrightarrow T}\par\frac{\Psi\vdash_{0}\Delta\quad\forall\gamma:\Phi\Longrightarrow_{g}\Psi~.~\Phi\mathrel{\raisebox{0.01pt}{\rule{0.45pt}{6.88889pt}}{\hskip 1.00006pt\vDash}}_{0}T[\gamma]}{\Psi\mathrel{\raisebox{0.01pt}{\rule{0.45pt}{6.88889pt}}{\hskip 1.00006pt\vDash}}_{1}\square(\Delta\vdash T)}\qquad\frac{\Psi,g:\textsf{Ctx}\vdash_{1}T\quad\forall\gamma:\Phi\Longrightarrow_{g}\Psi\text{ and }\Phi\vdash_{0}\Gamma~.~\Phi\mathrel{\raisebox{0.01pt}{\rule{0.45pt}{6.88889pt}}{\hskip 1.00006pt\vDash}}_{1}T[q(\gamma)][\textsf{id}_{\Phi},\Gamma/g]}{\Psi\mathrel{\raisebox{0.01pt}{\rule{0.45pt}{6.88889pt}}{\hskip 1.00006pt\vDash}}_{1}(g:\textsf{Ctx})\Rightarrow T}

Compared to the syntactic well-formedness judgment in
Sec.

2.1
, the
semantic counterpart has extra universal quantifications over global weakenings. These universal quantifications are necessary when we give semantics to terms. Moreover, the semantic well-formedness of types is monotonic w.r.t. global weakenings:

Lemma 3.6 (Monotonicity).

If
\gamma:\Phi\Longrightarrow_{g}\Psi
and
\Psi\mathrel{\raisebox{0.01pt}{\rule{0.45pt}{6.88889pt}}{\hskip 1.00006pt\vDash}}_{i}T
, then
\Phi\mathrel{\raisebox{0.01pt}{\rule{0.45pt}{6.88889pt}}{\hskip 1.00006pt\vDash}}_{i}T[\gamma]
.

Proof.

Induction. ∎

The lifting lemma also has a semantic counterpart:

Lemma 3.7 (Lifting).

If
\Psi\mathrel{\raisebox{0.01pt}{\rule{0.45pt}{6.88889pt}}{\hskip 1.00006pt\vDash}}_{0}T
, then
\Psi\mathrel{\raisebox{0.01pt}{\rule{0.45pt}{6.88889pt}}{\hskip 1.00006pt\vDash}}_{1}T
.

Proof.

Induction.
∎

Lemma 3.8 (Escape).

If
\Psi\mathrel{\raisebox{0.01pt}{\rule{0.45pt}{6.88889pt}}{\hskip 1.00006pt\vDash}}_{i}T
, then
\Psi\vdash_{i}T
.

Proof.

We do induction on
\Psi\mathrel{\raisebox{0.01pt}{\rule{0.45pt}{6.88889pt}}{\hskip 1.00006pt\vDash}}_{i}T
. In the meta-function case, we instantiate the global weakening to

p(\textsf{id}):\Psi,g:\textsf{Ctx}\Longrightarrow_{g}\Psi
and
\Gamma
to
g
. Apply IH again to obtain the goal.
∎

Now we move on to defining the reducibility for terms
\Psi;\Gamma\mathrel{\raisebox{0.01pt}{\rule{0.45pt}{6.88889pt}}{\hskip 1.00006pt\vDash}}_{1}^{i}{t}\approx t^{\prime}:T
and
local substitutions
\Psi;\Gamma\mathrel{\raisebox{0.01pt}{\rule{0.45pt}{6.88889pt}}{\hskip 1.00006pt\vDash}}_{1}^{i}{\delta}\approx\delta^{\prime}:\Delta
. Both relations are defined first by recursion on the layer
i
. Then, the reducibility predicate for terms
\Psi;\Gamma\mathrel{\raisebox{0.01pt}{\rule{0.45pt}{6.88889pt}}{\hskip 1.00006pt\vDash}}_{1}^{i}{t}\approx t^{\prime}:T
is defined by recursion on

\Psi\mathrel{\raisebox{0.01pt}{\rule{0.45pt}{6.88889pt}}{\hskip 1.00006pt\vDash}}_{i}T
. The reducibility predicate for local substitutions
\Psi;\Gamma\mathrel{\raisebox{0.01pt}{\rule{0.45pt}{6.88889pt}}{\hskip 1.00006pt\vDash}}_{1}^{i}{\delta}\approx\delta^{\prime}:\Delta
is defined inductively. The predicates are defined in this way because the layer-1 predicate for terms refers
to the predicates at layer
0
, as in our presheaf models by
Hu and Pientka (2024a)
. The definition that we present here does not focus too much on the
layered nature to save space and be more modular. However, we imagine that if this model must be put into a proof assistant, then
attention must be paid to ensure the well-foundness. The predicate for terms is a Kripke model, as it is indexed by both global and local contexts. We first define the semantic natural numbers:

\frac{\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{1}t\rightsquigarrow^{\ast}w:\texttt{Nat}\quad\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{1}t^{\prime}\rightsquigarrow^{\ast}w^{\prime}:\texttt{Nat}\quad\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{1}w\simeq w^{\prime}:\texttt{Nat}\quad\Psi;\Gamma\mathrel{\raisebox{0.01pt}{\rule{0.45pt}{6.88889pt}}{\hskip 1.00006pt\vDash}}^{\textsf{Nf}}w\approx w^{\prime}:\texttt{Nat}}{\Psi;\Gamma\mathrel{\raisebox{0.01pt}{\rule{0.45pt}{6.88889pt}}{\hskip 1.00006pt\vDash}}_{1}^{i}{t}\approx t^{\prime}:\texttt{Nat}}\par\frac{}{\Psi;\Gamma\mathrel{\raisebox{0.01pt}{\rule{0.45pt}{6.88889pt}}{\hskip 1.00006pt\vDash}}^{\textsf{Nf}}\textsf{zero}\approx\textsf{zero}:\texttt{Nat}}\par\frac{\Psi;\Gamma\mathrel{\raisebox{0.01pt}{\rule{0.45pt}{6.88889pt}}{\hskip 1.00006pt\vDash}}t\approx t^{\prime}:\texttt{Nat}}{\Psi;\Gamma\mathrel{\raisebox{0.01pt}{\rule{0.45pt}{6.88889pt}}{\hskip 1.00006pt\vDash}}^{\textsf{Nf}}{\textsf{succ}~t}\approx\textsf{succ}~t^{\prime}:\texttt{Nat}}\par\frac{\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{1}v\sim v^{\prime}:\texttt{Nat}}{\Psi;\Gamma\mathrel{\raisebox{0.01pt}{\rule{0.45pt}{6.88889pt}}{\hskip 1.00006pt\vDash}}^{\textsf{Nf}}{v}\approx v^{\prime}:\texttt{Nat}}

Then we let
\Psi;\Gamma\mathrel{\raisebox{0.01pt}{\rule{0.45pt}{6.88889pt}}{\hskip 1.00006pt\vDash}}_{1}^{i}{t}\approx t^{\prime}:\texttt{Nat}:=\Psi;\Gamma\mathrel{\raisebox{0.01pt}{\rule{0.45pt}{6.88889pt}}{\hskip 1.00006pt\vDash}}t\approx t^{\prime}:\texttt{Nat}
.

Next, we define the case for function.
\Psi;\Gamma\mathrel{\raisebox{0.01pt}{\rule{0.45pt}{6.88889pt}}{\hskip 1.00006pt\vDash}}_{1}^{i}{t}\approx t^{\prime}:S\longrightarrow T
holds iff

•

\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{1}t\rightsquigarrow^{\ast}w:S\longrightarrow T
, and

•

\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{1}t^{\prime}\rightsquigarrow^{\ast}w^{\prime}:S\longrightarrow T
, and

•

\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{1}w\simeq w^{\prime}:S\longrightarrow T
, and

•

for any
\gamma;\tau:\Phi;\Delta\Longrightarrow\Psi;\Gamma
, and given

\Phi;\Delta\mathrel{\raisebox{0.01pt}{\rule{0.45pt}{6.88889pt}}{\hskip 1.00006pt\vDash}}_{1}^{i}{s}\approx s^{\prime}:S[\gamma]
, then we have

\Phi;\Delta\mathrel{\raisebox{0.01pt}{\rule{0.45pt}{6.88889pt}}{\hskip 1.00006pt\vDash}}_{1}^{i}{w[\gamma;\tau]~s}\approx w^{\prime}[\gamma;\tau]~s^{\prime}:T[\gamma]
.

It means that
t
reduces to some weak head normal form, and the result of applying
this weak head normal form to a reducible term remains reducible.

Next, we define the reducibility for
\square(\Delta\vdash T)
,

\frac{\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{1}t\rightsquigarrow^{\ast}w:\square(\Delta\vdash T)\quad\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{1}t^{\prime}\rightsquigarrow^{\ast}w^{\prime}:\square(\Delta\vdash T)\quad\quad\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{1}w\simeq w^{\prime}:\square(\Delta\vdash T)\quad\Psi;\Gamma\mathrel{\raisebox{0.01pt}{\rule{0.45pt}{6.88889pt}}{\hskip 1.00006pt\vDash}}_{1}^{1}{{}^{\textsf{Nf}}w}\approx w^{\prime}:\square(\Delta\vdash T)}{\Psi;\Gamma\mathrel{\raisebox{0.01pt}{\rule{0.45pt}{6.88889pt}}{\hskip 1.00006pt\vDash}}_{1}^{1}{t}\approx t^{\prime}:\square(\Delta\vdash T)}\\
\frac{\Psi;\Delta\vdash_{0}t:T\quad\forall~\Psi;\Delta^{\prime}\mathrel{\raisebox{0.01pt}{\rule{0.45pt}{6.88889pt}}{\hskip 1.00006pt\vDash}}_{1}^{0}{\delta}\approx\delta^{\prime}:\Delta~.~\Psi;\Delta^{\prime}\mathrel{\raisebox{0.01pt}{\rule{0.45pt}{6.88889pt}}{\hskip 1.00006pt\vDash}}_{1}^{0}{t[\delta]}\approx t[\delta^{\prime}]:T}{\Psi;\Gamma\mathrel{\raisebox{0.01pt}{\rule{0.45pt}{6.88889pt}}{\hskip 1.00006pt\vDash}}_{1}^{1}{{}^{\textsf{Nf}}{\texttt{box}\ t}}\approx\texttt{box}\ t:\square(\Delta\vdash T)}\par\frac{\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{1}v\sim v^{\prime}:\square(\Delta\vdash T)}{\Psi;\Gamma\mathrel{\raisebox{0.01pt}{\rule{0.45pt}{6.88889pt}}{\hskip 1.00006pt\vDash}}_{1}^{1}{{}^{\textsf{Nf}}{v}}\approx v^{\prime}:\square(\Delta\vdash T)}

Similar to our presheaf models, we must refer to
\Psi;\Delta\mathrel{\raisebox{0.01pt}{\rule{0.45pt}{6.88889pt}}{\hskip 1.00006pt\vDash}}_{1}^{0}{\delta}\approx\delta^{\prime}:\Gamma
when giving semantics for terms of type
\square(\Delta\vdash T)
,
so the predicates at layer
0
must be finished before defining the predicate at layer

1
. To support pattern matching on code, instead of this universal quantification, we must
use an inductively defined layer-
0
model instead as done in

Hu and Pientka (2024a, Sec. 5.4.1)
.

Next, we define the case for meta-functions.
\Psi;\Gamma\mathrel{\raisebox{0.01pt}{\rule{0.45pt}{6.88889pt}}{\hskip 1.00006pt\vDash}}_{1}^{1}{t}\approx t^{\prime}:(g:\textsf{Ctx})\Rightarrow T
holds iff

•

\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{1}t\rightsquigarrow^{\ast}w:(g:\textsf{Ctx})\Rightarrow T
, and

•

\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{1}t^{\prime}\rightsquigarrow^{\ast}w^{\prime}:(g:\textsf{Ctx})\Rightarrow T
, and

•

\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{1}w\simeq w^{\prime}:(g:\textsf{Ctx})\Rightarrow T
, and

•

for any
\gamma;\tau:\Phi;\Delta\Longrightarrow\Psi;\Gamma
, and given

\Phi\vdash_{0}\Delta^{\prime}
, then we have

\Phi;\Delta\mathrel{\raisebox{0.01pt}{\rule{0.45pt}{6.88889pt}}{\hskip 1.00006pt\vDash}}_{1}^{1}{w[\gamma;\tau]~\$~\Delta^{\prime}}\approx w^{\prime}[\gamma;\tau]~\$~\Delta^{\prime}:T[q(\gamma)][\textsf{id}_{\Phi},\Delta^{\prime}/g]
.

We generalize the reducibility for terms to local contexts and local substitutions by
doing an inductive-recursive definition:

\frac{\vdash\Psi}{\Psi\mathrel{\raisebox{0.01pt}{\rule{0.45pt}{6.88889pt}}{\hskip 1.00006pt\vDash}}_{i}\cdot}

Then
\Psi;\Gamma\mathrel{\raisebox{0.01pt}{\rule{0.45pt}{6.88889pt}}{\hskip 1.00006pt\vDash}}_{1}^{i}{\delta}\approx\delta^{\prime}:\cdot
holds by considering the cases for
\Gamma
,

•

if
\Gamma
ends with
\cdot
, then
\delta=\delta^{\prime}=\cdot^{|\Gamma|}
;

•

if
\Gamma
ends with
g
, then
g:\textsf{Ctx}\in\Psi
and
\delta=\delta^{\prime}=\cdot^{|\Gamma|}_{g}
.

\frac{\vdash\Psi\quad g:\textsf{Ctx}\in\Psi}{\Psi\mathrel{\raisebox{0.01pt}{\rule{0.45pt}{6.88889pt}}{\hskip 1.00006pt\vDash}}_{i}g}

Then
\Psi;\Gamma\mathrel{\raisebox{0.01pt}{\rule{0.45pt}{6.88889pt}}{\hskip 1.00006pt\vDash}}_{1}^{i}{\delta}\approx\delta^{\prime}:g
holds iff
\Gamma
ends with
g
and
\delta=\delta^{\prime}=\textsf{wk}_{g}^{|\Gamma|}
.
