\frac{\Psi\mathrel{\raisebox{0.01pt}{\rule{0.45pt}{6.88889pt}}{\hskip 1.00006pt\vDash}}_{i}\Delta\quad\Psi\mathrel{\raisebox{0.01pt}{\rule{0.45pt}{6.88889pt}}{\hskip 1.00006pt\vDash}}_{i}T}{\Psi\mathrel{\raisebox{0.01pt}{\rule{0.45pt}{6.88889pt}}{\hskip 1.00006pt\vDash}}_{i}\Delta,x:T}

Then
\Psi;\Gamma\mathrel{\raisebox{0.01pt}{\rule{0.45pt}{6.88889pt}}{\hskip 1.00006pt\vDash}}_{1}^{i}{\delta}\approx\delta^{\prime}:\Delta,x:T
holds iff

•

\Psi;\Gamma\mathrel{\raisebox{0.01pt}{\rule{0.45pt}{6.88889pt}}{\hskip 1.00006pt\vDash}}_{1}^{i}{\delta}\approx\delta^{\prime}:\Delta
,

•

\Psi;\Gamma\mathrel{\raisebox{0.01pt}{\rule{0.45pt}{6.88889pt}}{\hskip 1.00006pt\vDash}}_{1}^{i}{t}\approx t^{\prime}:T
.

Note that one should consider the rules above really give two predicates, one for
layer
0
and one for layer
1
. In this technical report, however, we do not really type out the replication.

At this point, we have finished defining reducibility predicates for all types. We further let
\Psi;\Gamma\mathrel{\raisebox{0.01pt}{\rule{0.45pt}{6.88889pt}}{\hskip 1.00006pt\vDash}}_{i}t:T
be
\Psi;\Gamma\mathrel{\raisebox{0.01pt}{\rule{0.45pt}{6.88889pt}}{\hskip 1.00006pt\vDash}}_{1}^{i}{t}\approx t:T
. This predicate basically means that
t
can be reduced to some weak head normal form.

By the definition of the predicates, we have the following lemmas:

Lemma 3.9.

If
\Psi;\Gamma\mathrel{\raisebox{0.01pt}{\rule{0.45pt}{6.88889pt}}{\hskip 1.00006pt\vDash}}_{1}^{i}{t}\approx t^{\prime}:T
, then
\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{1}s\rightsquigarrow^{\ast}t:T
, and
\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{1}s^{\prime}\rightsquigarrow^{\ast}t^{\prime}:T
, and

\Psi;\Gamma\mathrel{\raisebox{0.01pt}{\rule{0.45pt}{6.88889pt}}{\hskip 1.00006pt\vDash}}_{1}^{i}{s}\approx s^{\prime}:T
.

Proof.

By induction on
\Psi\mathrel{\raisebox{0.01pt}{\rule{0.45pt}{6.88889pt}}{\hskip 1.00006pt\vDash}}_{i}T
.
∎

Lemma 3.10.

If
\Psi;\Gamma\mathrel{\raisebox{0.01pt}{\rule{0.45pt}{6.88889pt}}{\hskip 1.00006pt\vDash}}_{1}^{i}{t}\approx t^{\prime}:T
, then
\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{1}t\rightsquigarrow^{\ast}w:T
, and
\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{1}t^{\prime}\rightsquigarrow^{\ast}w^{\prime}:T
, and

\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{1}w\simeq w^{\prime}:T
.

Proof.

By induction on
\Psi\mathrel{\raisebox{0.01pt}{\rule{0.45pt}{6.88889pt}}{\hskip 1.00006pt\vDash}}_{i}T
and transitivity of multi-step weak head
reduction.
∎

Corollary 3.11.

If
\Psi;\Gamma\mathrel{\raisebox{0.01pt}{\rule{0.45pt}{6.88889pt}}{\hskip 1.00006pt\vDash}}_{1}^{i}{t}\approx t^{\prime}:T
, then
\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{1}t\simeq t^{\prime}:T
.

Lemma 3.12.

If
\Psi;\Gamma\mathrel{\raisebox{0.01pt}{\rule{0.45pt}{6.88889pt}}{\hskip 1.00006pt\vDash}}_{1}^{i}{\delta}\approx\delta^{\prime}:\Delta
, then

\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{1}\delta\simeq\delta^{\prime}:\Delta
.

Proof.

Generalization of
Corollary

3.11
.
∎

Lemma 3.13 (Reducibility of Neutrals).

If
\Psi\mathrel{\raisebox{0.01pt}{\rule{0.45pt}{6.88889pt}}{\hskip 1.00006pt\vDash}}_{i}T
,
\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{i}v:T
and
\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{1}v\sim v^{\prime}:T
, then
\Psi;\Gamma\mathrel{\raisebox{0.01pt}{\rule{0.45pt}{6.88889pt}}{\hskip 1.00006pt\vDash}}_{1}^{i}{v}\approx v^{\prime}:T
.

Proof.

Induction by
\Psi\mathrel{\raisebox{0.01pt}{\rule{0.45pt}{6.88889pt}}{\hskip 1.00006pt\vDash}}_{i}T
and apply IHs.
∎

Lemma 3.14 (Reducibility of Weakenings).

If
\Psi\mathrel{\raisebox{0.01pt}{\rule{0.45pt}{6.88889pt}}{\hskip 1.00006pt\vDash}}_{i}\Gamma,\Delta
, then
\Psi;\Gamma,\Delta\mathrel{\raisebox{0.01pt}{\rule{0.45pt}{6.88889pt}}{\hskip 1.00006pt\vDash}}_{1}^{i}{\textsf{wk}^{|\Delta|}_{\Gamma}}\approx\textsf{wk}^{|\Delta|}_{\Gamma}:\Gamma
.

Proof.

A direct consequence of
Lemma

3.13
.
∎

Corollary 3.15 (Reducibility of Identity).

If
\Psi\mathrel{\raisebox{0.01pt}{\rule{0.45pt}{6.88889pt}}{\hskip 1.00006pt\vDash}}_{i}\Gamma
, then
\Psi;\Gamma\mathrel{\raisebox{0.01pt}{\rule{0.45pt}{6.88889pt}}{\hskip 1.00006pt\vDash}}_{1}^{i}{\textsf{id}_{\Gamma}}\approx\textsf{id}_{\Gamma}:\Gamma
.

Lemma 3.16.

If
\Psi\mathrel{\raisebox{0.01pt}{\rule{0.45pt}{6.88889pt}}{\hskip 1.00006pt\vDash}}_{0}T
, then
\Psi;\Gamma\mathrel{\raisebox{0.01pt}{\rule{0.45pt}{6.88889pt}}{\hskip 1.00006pt\vDash}}_{1}^{0}{t}\approx t^{\prime}:T
iff
\Psi;\Gamma\mathrel{\raisebox{0.01pt}{\rule{0.45pt}{6.88889pt}}{\hskip 1.00006pt\vDash}}_{1}^{1}{t}\approx t^{\prime}:T
.

Proof.

We only consider the cases for
Nat
and functions and apply IHs directly.
∎

This is derived from the fact that the predicate for
Nat
is invariant at
different layers.

We verify several important properties for the reducibility predicates:

Lemma 3.17 (Escape).

If
\Psi;\Gamma\mathrel{\raisebox{0.01pt}{\rule{0.45pt}{6.88889pt}}{\hskip 1.00006pt\vDash}}_{1}^{i}{t}\approx t^{\prime}:T
, then
\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{1}t\approx t^{\prime}:T
.

Proof.

We do induction on
\Psi\mathrel{\raisebox{0.01pt}{\rule{0.45pt}{6.88889pt}}{\hskip 1.00006pt\vDash}}_{i}T
. Notice that generic equivalence eventually implies syntactic equivalence by the
subsumption law.
∎

Lemma 3.18 (Escape).

If
\Psi;\Gamma\mathrel{\raisebox{0.01pt}{\rule{0.45pt}{6.88889pt}}{\hskip 1.00006pt\vDash}}_{1}^{i}{\delta}\approx\delta^{\prime}:\Delta
, then
\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{1}\delta\approx\delta^{\prime}:\Delta
.

Lemma 3.19 (Monotonicity).

If
\Psi\mathrel{\raisebox{0.01pt}{\rule{0.45pt}{6.88889pt}}{\hskip 1.00006pt\vDash}}_{i}T
and
\Psi;\Gamma\mathrel{\raisebox{0.01pt}{\rule{0.45pt}{6.88889pt}}{\hskip 1.00006pt\vDash}}_{1}^{i}{t}\approx t^{\prime}:T
, given
\gamma;\tau:\Phi;\Delta\Longrightarrow\Psi;\Gamma
, then
\Phi;\Delta\mathrel{\raisebox{0.01pt}{\rule{0.45pt}{6.88889pt}}{\hskip 1.00006pt\vDash}}_{1}^{i}{t[\gamma;\tau]}\approx t^{\prime}[\gamma;\tau]:T[\gamma]
.

Proof.

We do induction on
\Psi\mathrel{\raisebox{0.01pt}{\rule{0.45pt}{6.88889pt}}{\hskip 1.00006pt\vDash}}_{i}T
.

Case

T=\texttt{Nat}
, then we have the goal by the monotonicity of multi-step weak head
reduction and the generic equivalence. We further do induction on
\Psi;\Gamma\mathrel{\raisebox{0.01pt}{\rule{0.45pt}{6.88889pt}}{\hskip 1.00006pt\vDash}}^{\textsf{Nf}}{w}\approx w^{\prime}:\texttt{Nat}
.

Case

T=S\longrightarrow T^{\prime}
, then we assume another
\gamma^{\prime};\tau^{\prime}:\Phi^{\prime};\Delta^{\prime}\Longrightarrow\Phi;\Delta
and
\Phi^{\prime};\Delta^{\prime}\mathrel{\raisebox{0.01pt}{\rule{0.45pt}{6.88889pt}}{\hskip 1.00006pt\vDash}}_{1}^{i}{s}\approx s^{\prime}:S[\gamma\circ\gamma^{\prime}]
, we must show
\Phi^{\prime};\Delta^{\prime}\mathrel{\raisebox{0.01pt}{\rule{0.45pt}{6.88889pt}}{\hskip 1.00006pt\vDash}}_{1}^{i}{t[\gamma;\tau][\gamma^{\prime};\tau^{\prime}]~s}\approx t^{\prime}[\gamma;\tau][\gamma^{\prime};\tau^{\prime}]~s^{\prime}:S[\gamma\circ\gamma^{\prime}]
. But this is immediate due to the composition of weakenings.

Case

T=\square(\Delta\vdash T^{\prime})
, then it is also immediate after a case analysis of

\Psi;\Gamma\mathrel{\raisebox{0.01pt}{\rule{0.45pt}{6.88889pt}}{\hskip 1.00006pt\vDash}}^{\textsf{Nf}}{w}\approx w^{\prime}:\square(\Delta\vdash T^{\prime})
. We apply the monotonicity of multi-step weak head
reduction and the generic equivalence appropriately.

Case

T=(g:\textsf{Ctx})\Rightarrow T^{\prime}
, then this case is very similar to the function
case. We assume another
\gamma^{\prime};\tau^{\prime}:\Phi^{\prime};\Delta^{\prime}\Longrightarrow\Phi;\Delta
and
\Phi^{\prime}\vdash_{0}\Gamma^{\prime}
. Our goal is to show

\Phi^{\prime};\Delta^{\prime}\mathrel{\raisebox{0.01pt}{\rule{0.45pt}{6.88889pt}}{\hskip 1.00006pt\vDash}}_{1}^{1}{w[\gamma;\tau][\gamma^{\prime};\tau^{\prime}]~\$~\Gamma^{\prime}}\approx w^{\prime}[\gamma;\tau][\gamma^{\prime};\tau^{\prime}]~\$~\Gamma^{\prime}:T[q(\gamma\circ\gamma^{\prime})][\textsf{id}_{\Phi^{\prime}},\Gamma^{\prime}/g]

This again has been given by the composition of weakenings and notice that

q(\gamma\circ\gamma^{\prime})=q(\gamma)\circ q(\gamma^{\prime})
.

∎

Lemma 3.20 (Monotonicity).

If
\Psi;\Gamma\mathrel{\raisebox{0.01pt}{\rule{0.45pt}{6.88889pt}}{\hskip 1.00006pt\vDash}}_{1}^{i}{\delta}\approx\delta^{\prime}:\Delta^{\prime}
, given
\gamma;\tau:\Phi;\Delta\Longrightarrow\Psi;\Gamma
, then
\Phi;\Delta\mathrel{\raisebox{0.01pt}{\rule{0.45pt}{6.88889pt}}{\hskip 1.00006pt\vDash}}_{1}^{i}{\delta[\gamma;\tau]}\approx\delta^{\prime}[\gamma;\tau]:\Delta^{\prime}[\gamma]
.

Lemma 3.21 (PER).

The
\Psi;\Gamma\mathrel{\raisebox{0.01pt}{\rule{0.45pt}{6.88889pt}}{\hskip 1.00006pt\vDash}}_{1}^{i}{t}\approx t^{\prime}:T
relation satisfies symmetry and transitivity.

Proof.

Transitivity relies on the uniqueness of weak head reduction.
∎

Lemma 3.22 (PER).

The
\Psi;\Gamma\mathrel{\raisebox{0.01pt}{\rule{0.45pt}{6.88889pt}}{\hskip 1.00006pt\vDash}}_{1}^{i}{\delta}\approx\delta^{\prime}:\Delta
relation satisfies symmetry and transitivity.

The following lemma is the semantic counterpart for the layering principle. It ensures that terms inhabiting types in STLC have the same behaviors at both layers.

Lemma 3.23 (Layering Restriction).

•

If
\Psi\mathrel{\raisebox{0.01pt}{\rule{0.45pt}{6.88889pt}}{\hskip 1.00006pt\vDash}}_{0}T
, then
\Psi;\Gamma\mathrel{\raisebox{0.01pt}{\rule{0.45pt}{6.88889pt}}{\hskip 1.00006pt\vDash}}_{1}^{0}{t}\approx t^{\prime}:T
is equivalent to
\Psi;\Gamma\mathrel{\raisebox{0.01pt}{\rule{0.45pt}{6.88889pt}}{\hskip 1.00006pt\vDash}}_{1}^{1}{t}\approx t^{\prime}:T
.

•

If
\Psi\mathrel{\raisebox{0.01pt}{\rule{0.45pt}{6.88889pt}}{\hskip 1.00006pt\vDash}}_{0}\Delta
, then
\Psi;\Gamma\mathrel{\raisebox{0.01pt}{\rule{0.45pt}{6.88889pt}}{\hskip 1.00006pt\vDash}}_{1}^{0}{\delta}\approx\delta^{\prime}:\Delta
is
equivalent to
\Psi;\Gamma\mathrel{\raisebox{0.01pt}{\rule{0.45pt}{6.88889pt}}{\hskip 1.00006pt\vDash}}_{1}^{1}{\delta}\approx\delta^{\prime}:\Delta
.

This lemma is particularly useful to help treat global variables the same at both layers.

Our goal is then to show the following theorem:

Theorem 3.24 (Completeness).

•

If
\Psi\vdash_{i}T
, then
\Psi\mathrel{\raisebox{0.01pt}{\rule{0.45pt}{6.88889pt}}{\hskip 1.00006pt\vDash}}_{i}T
.

•

If
\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{i}t\approx t^{\prime}:T
, then
\Psi;\Gamma\mathrel{\raisebox{0.01pt}{\rule{0.45pt}{6.88889pt}}{\hskip 1.00006pt\vDash}}_{1}^{i}{t}\approx t^{\prime}:T
.

•

If
\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{i}\delta\approx\delta^{\prime}:\Delta
, then
\Psi;\Gamma\mathrel{\raisebox{0.01pt}{\rule{0.45pt}{6.88889pt}}{\hskip 1.00006pt\vDash}}_{1}^{i}{\delta}\approx\delta^{\prime}:\Delta
.

If the
\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{1}t\simeq t^{\prime}:T
is the algorithmic convertibility checking algorithm, then
we show that syntactic equivalence implies algorithmic equivalence. In other words, algorithmic convertibility is complete w.r.t. syntactic equivalence. However, to arrive at that solution, we must first show that the completeness theorem
above holds w.r.t. the generic equivalence relations. Following
Abel
et al. (2017)
, however, we still have one step missing to
conclude this goal. We must define a set of validity judgments to handle the meta-function case in the
semantic well-formedness of types.

3.3.
Validity Judgments

According to
Abel
et al. (2017)
, validity judgments are introduced to
characterize the reducible terms that are also closed under substitutions. In the same spirit, we also need the validity judgments to handle the case for
meta-functions:

\frac{\Psi,g:\textsf{Ctx}\vdash_{1}T\quad\forall\gamma:\Phi\Longrightarrow_{g}\Psi\text{ and }\Phi\vdash_{0}\Gamma~.~\Phi\mathrel{\raisebox{0.01pt}{\rule{0.45pt}{6.88889pt}}{\hskip 1.00006pt\vDash}}_{1}T[q(\gamma)][\textsf{id}_{\Phi},\Gamma/g]}{\Psi\mathrel{\raisebox{0.01pt}{\rule{0.45pt}{6.88889pt}}{\hskip 1.00006pt\vDash}}_{1}(g:\textsf{Ctx})\Rightarrow T}

When we attempt to prove the following statement from the completeness theorem:

If
\Psi\vdash_{i}T
, then
\Psi\mathrel{\raisebox{0.01pt}{\rule{0.45pt}{6.88889pt}}{\hskip 1.00006pt\vDash}}_{1}T
.

This case breaks down, because the IH only provides

\Psi,g:\textsf{Ctx}\mathrel{\raisebox{0.01pt}{\rule{0.45pt}{6.88889pt}}{\hskip 1.00006pt\vDash}}_{1}T

and by monotonicity, we derive

\Phi,g:\textsf{Ctx}\mathrel{\raisebox{0.01pt}{\rule{0.45pt}{6.88889pt}}{\hskip 1.00006pt\vDash}}_{1}T[q(\gamma)]

but then we are stuck. We have no way to prove that the semantic well-formedness of types is closed under
substitutions. Following
Abel
et al. (2017)
, we define validity judgments which further
restrict reducibility predicates to subsets that are closed under substitutions. Since types are only affected by the global contexts, the validity judgments are
defined by induction-recursion on global contexts:

\frac{}{\vDash^{v}\cdot}

The validity equivalence for global substitutions

\Phi\vDash\sigma:\cdot
is defined as
\vdash\Phi
and
\sigma=\cdot
.

\frac{\vDash^{v}\Psi}{\vDash^{v}\Psi,g:\textsf{Ctx}}

\Phi\vDash\sigma:\Psi,g:\textsf{Ctx}
is defined as

•

\sigma=\sigma_{1},\Gamma/g
,

•

\Phi\vdash_{0}\Gamma
, and

•

\Phi\vDash\sigma_{1}:\Psi
.

\frac{\vDash^{v}\Psi\quad\Psi\Vdash_{0}\Gamma\quad\Psi\Vdash_{0}T}{\vDash^{v}\Psi,u:(\Gamma\vdash T)}

\Phi\vDash\sigma:\Psi,u:(\Gamma\vdash T)
is defined as

•

\sigma=\sigma_{1},t/u
,

•

\Phi\vDash\sigma_{1}:\Psi
, and

•

for all
\Phi;\Delta\mathrel{\raisebox{0.01pt}{\rule{0.45pt}{6.88889pt}}{\hskip 1.00006pt\vDash}}_{1}^{0}{\delta}\approx\delta^{\prime}:\Gamma[\sigma_{1}]
, we have

\Phi;\Delta\mathrel{\raisebox{0.01pt}{\rule{0.45pt}{6.88889pt}}{\hskip 1.00006pt\vDash}}_{1}^{0}{t[\delta]}\approx t[\delta^{\prime}]:T[\sigma_{1}]
.

The validity of types
\Psi\Vdash_{i}T
is defined as:
\Psi\mathrel{\raisebox{0.01pt}{\rule{0.45pt}{6.88889pt}}{\hskip 1.00006pt\vDash}}_{i}T
and given

\Phi\vDash\sigma:\Psi
, we have
\Phi\mathrel{\raisebox{0.01pt}{\rule{0.45pt}{6.88889pt}}{\hskip 1.00006pt\vDash}}_{i}T[\sigma]
. The validity of local contexts
\Psi\Vdash_{i}\Gamma
is defined by applying the validity
of types pointwise. The judgments are simplified because there is no need to have an equivalence judgment
between global substitutions. In a global substitution, there are only two kinds of components: code of STLC and
local contexts. The former is determined to be identified by syntax. Local contexts are also identified by syntax because we are dealing with simple
types.

With these definitions ready, we put a universal quantification on top of the
reducibility predicates, which specifies the reducible terms that are closed under
valid global substitutions:

•

\Psi;\Gamma\Vdash_{i}t\approx t^{\prime}:T
iff for any
\Phi\vDash\sigma:\Psi
and

\Phi;\Delta\mathrel{\raisebox{0.01pt}{\rule{0.45pt}{6.88889pt}}{\hskip 1.00006pt\vDash}}_{1}^{i}{\delta}\approx\delta^{\prime}:\Gamma[\sigma]
, we have

\Phi;\Delta\mathrel{\raisebox{0.01pt}{\rule{0.45pt}{6.88889pt}}{\hskip 1.00006pt\vDash}}_{1}^{i}{t[\sigma][\delta]}\approx t^{\prime}[\sigma][\delta]:T[\sigma]
.

•

\Psi;\Gamma\Vdash_{i}\delta\approx\delta^{\prime}:\Delta
iff for any

\Phi\vDash\sigma:\Psi
and

\Phi;\Delta^{\prime}\mathrel{\raisebox{0.01pt}{\rule{0.45pt}{6.88889pt}}{\hskip 1.00006pt\vDash}}_{1}^{i}{\delta^{\prime\prime}}\approx\delta^{\prime\prime\prime}:\Gamma[\sigma]
, we have

\Phi;\Delta^{\prime}\mathrel{\raisebox{0.01pt}{\rule{0.45pt}{6.88889pt}}{\hskip 1.00006pt\vDash}}_{1}^{i}{\delta[\sigma]\circ\delta^{\prime\prime}}\approx\delta^{\prime}[\sigma]\circ\delta^{\prime\prime}:\Delta[\sigma]
.

Now we work out several lemmas:

Lemma 3.25 (Escape).

If
\Phi\vDash\sigma:\Psi
, then
\Phi\vdash\sigma:\Psi
.

Proof.

We do induction on
\vDash^{v}\Psi
. In the case for extension of code, we apply the escape lemma for semantically
well-formed types and reducible terms.
∎

Lemma 3.26 (Monotonicity).

If
\Phi\vDash\sigma:\Psi
and
\gamma:\Phi^{\prime}\Longrightarrow_{g}\Phi
, then

\Phi^{\prime}\vDash\sigma[\gamma]:\Psi
.

Proof.

We do induction on
\vDash^{v}\Psi
. Use monotonicity of reducible terms and the algebra of global substitutions. ∎

Lemma 3.27 (Validity of Global Weakening Substitutions).

If
\vdash\Psi,\Phi
and
\vDash^{v}\Psi
, then
\Psi,\Phi\vDash\textsf{wk}^{|\Phi|}_{\Psi}:\Psi
.

Proof.

We do induction on
\vDash^{v}\Psi
. The most interesting case is the extension case for code. If
\Psi=\Psi^{\prime},u:(\Gamma\vdash T)
, then our goal is given
\Psi^{\prime},u:(\Gamma\vdash T),\Phi;\Delta\mathrel{\raisebox{0.01pt}{\rule{0.45pt}{6.88889pt}}{\hskip 1.00006pt\vDash}}_{1}^{0}{\delta}\approx\delta^{\prime}:\Gamma[\textsf{wk}^{1+|\Phi|}_{\Psi^{\prime}}]
, to prove

\Psi^{\prime},u:(\Gamma\vdash T),\Phi;\Delta\mathrel{\raisebox{0.01pt}{\rule{0.45pt}{6.88889pt}}{\hskip 1.00006pt\vDash}}_{1}^{0}{u^{\textsf{id}_{\Gamma}[p^{1+|\Phi|}(\textsf{id})]}[\delta]}\approx u^{\textsf{id}_{\Gamma}[p^{1+|\Phi|}(\textsf{id})]}[\delta^{\prime}]:T[\textsf{wk}^{1+|\Phi|}_{\Psi^{\prime}}]

In the last section, we have established that
[\textsf{wk}^{1+|\Phi|}_{\Psi^{\prime}}]
has the
same effect as
[p^{1+|\Phi|}(\textsf{id})]
, so this goal becomes

\Psi^{\prime},u:(\Gamma\vdash T),\Phi;\Delta\mathrel{\raisebox{0.01pt}{\rule{0.45pt}{6.88889pt}}{\hskip 1.00006pt\vDash}}_{1}^{0}{u^{\textsf{id}_{\Gamma}[p^{1+|\Phi|}(\textsf{id})]}[\delta]}\approx u^{\textsf{id}_{\Gamma}[p^{1+|\Phi|}(\textsf{id})]}[\delta^{\prime}]:T[p^{1+|\Phi|}(\textsf{id})]

By computation, the goal further becomes:

\Psi^{\prime},u:(\Gamma\vdash T),\Phi;\Delta\mathrel{\raisebox{0.01pt}{\rule{0.45pt}{6.88889pt}}{\hskip 1.00006pt\vDash}}_{1}^{0}{u^{\delta}}\approx u^{\delta^{\prime}}:T[p^{1+|\Phi|}(\textsf{id})]

But this is immediate due to
Lemma

3.12
, the reducibility of
neutrals and congruence of the generic equivalence

\Psi^{\prime},u:(\Gamma\vdash T),\Phi;\Delta\vdash_{1}u^{\delta}\sim u^{\delta^{\prime}}:T[p^{1+|\Phi|}(\textsf{id})]

∎

In particular, it proves that the identity is valid:

Corollary 3.28.

If
\vdash\Psi
, then
\Psi\vDash\textsf{id}:\Psi
.

Theorem 3.29 (Fundamental).

•

If
\vdash\Psi
, then
\vDash^{v}\Psi
.

•

If
\Psi\vdash_{i}T
, then
\Psi\Vdash_{i}T
.

•

If
\Psi\vdash_{i}\Gamma
, then
\Psi\Vdash_{i}\Gamma
.

Proof.

We do induction. The cases for global contexts are simple.

Case

\frac{\vdash\Psi}{\Psi\vdash_{i}\texttt{Nat}}

Assuming
\Phi\vDash\sigma:\Psi
, by escape, we have

\Phi\vdash\sigma:\Psi
, and then
\vdash\Phi
by presupposition. We then conclude the goal.

Case

\frac{\Psi\vdash_{i}S\quad\Psi\vdash_{i}T}{\Psi\vdash_{i}S\longrightarrow T}

We assume
\Phi\vDash\sigma:\Psi
. We now should prove
\Phi\mathrel{\raisebox{0.01pt}{\rule{0.45pt}{6.88889pt}}{\hskip 1.00006pt\vDash}}_{i}(S\longrightarrow T)[\sigma]
. This can be concluded from
\Phi\mathrel{\raisebox{0.01pt}{\rule{0.45pt}{6.88889pt}}{\hskip 1.00006pt\vDash}}_{i}S[\sigma]
and

\Phi\mathrel{\raisebox{0.01pt}{\rule{0.45pt}{6.88889pt}}{\hskip 1.00006pt\vDash}}_{i}T[\sigma]
. They are immediate from
\Psi\Vdash_{i}S
and
\Psi\Vdash_{i}T
by IH.

Case

\frac{\Psi\vdash_{0}\Delta\quad\Psi\vdash_{0}T}{\Psi\vdash_{1}\square(\Delta\vdash T)}

We assume
\Phi\vDash\sigma:\Psi
. We now should prove
\Phi\mathrel{\raisebox{0.01pt}{\rule{0.45pt}{6.88889pt}}{\hskip 1.00006pt\vDash}}_{i}(\square(\Delta\vdash T))[\sigma]
. We further assume
\gamma:\Phi^{\prime}\Longrightarrow_{g}\Phi
. The goal can be concluded from
\Phi^{\prime}\mathrel{\raisebox{0.01pt}{\rule{0.45pt}{6.88889pt}}{\hskip 1.00006pt\vDash}}_{1}T[\sigma][\gamma]
. Since
T[\sigma][\gamma]=T[\sigma[\gamma]]
and
\Psi\Vdash_{0}T
from IH,
we only need
\Phi^{\prime}\vDash\sigma[\gamma]:\Psi
, which we get from
monotonicity.

Case

\frac{\Psi,g:\textsf{Ctx}\vdash_{1}T}{\Psi\vdash_{1}(g:\textsf{Ctx})\Rightarrow T}

We assume
\Phi\vDash\sigma:\Psi
. We now should prove
\Phi\mathrel{\raisebox{0.01pt}{\rule{0.45pt}{6.88889pt}}{\hskip 1.00006pt\vDash}}_{i}((g:\textsf{Ctx})\Rightarrow T)[\sigma]
. We further assume
\gamma:\Phi^{\prime}\Longrightarrow_{g}\Phi
and
\Phi^{\prime}\vdash_{0}\Gamma
. The goal can be concluded from
\Phi^{\prime}\mathrel{\raisebox{0.01pt}{\rule{0.45pt}{6.88889pt}}{\hskip 1.00006pt\vDash}}_{1}T[q(\sigma)][q(\gamma)][\textsf{id},\Gamma/g]
. We compute:

\displaystyle T[q(\sigma)][q(\gamma)][\textsf{id},\Gamma/g]

\displaystyle=T[q(\sigma)[q(\gamma)]][\textsf{id},\Gamma/g]

\displaystyle=T[q(\sigma[\gamma])][\textsf{id},\Gamma/g]

\displaystyle=T[\sigma[\gamma][p(\textsf{id})],g/g][\textsf{id},\Gamma/g]

\displaystyle=T[(\sigma[\gamma][p(\textsf{id})]\circ(\textsf{id},\Gamma/g)),g[\textsf{id},\Gamma/g]/g]

\displaystyle=T[\sigma[\gamma],\Gamma/g]

Therefore the goal becomes to prove
\Phi^{\prime}\mathrel{\raisebox{0.01pt}{\rule{0.45pt}{6.88889pt}}{\hskip 1.00006pt\vDash}}_{1}T[\sigma[\gamma],\Gamma/g]
. By IH, we have
\Psi,g:\textsf{Ctx}\Vdash_{1}T
. We simply need
\Phi^{\prime}\vDash\sigma[\gamma],\Gamma/g:\Psi,g:\textsf{Ctx}
. This is immediate from monotonicity and definition.

∎

Theorem 3.30 (Fundamental).

•

If
\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{i}t:T
, then
\Psi;\Gamma\Vdash_{i}t:T
.

•

If
\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{i}\delta:\Delta
, then
\Psi;\Gamma\Vdash_{i}\delta:\Delta
.

•

If
\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{i}t\approx t^{\prime}:T
, then
\Psi;\Gamma\Vdash_{i}t\approx t^{\prime}:T
.

•

If
\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{i}\delta\approx\delta^{\prime}:\Delta
, then
\Psi;\Gamma\Vdash_{i}\delta\approx\delta^{\prime}:\Delta
.

Proof.

We again do mutual induction. We focus on modal cases.

Case

\frac{\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{i}\delta\approx\delta^{\prime}:\Delta\quad u:(\Delta\vdash T)\in\Psi}{\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{i}u^{\delta}\approx u^{\delta^{\prime}}:T}

(by IH)

\displaystyle H_{0}:

\displaystyle\Psi;\Gamma\Vdash_{i}\delta\approx\delta^{\prime}:\Delta

(by assumption)

\displaystyle H_{1}:

\displaystyle\Phi\vDash\sigma:\Psi

(by assumption)

\displaystyle\Phi;\Delta^{\prime}\mathrel{\raisebox{0.01pt}{\rule{0.45pt}{6.88889pt}}{\hskip 1.00006pt\vDash}}_{1}^{i}{\delta^{\prime\prime}}\approx\delta^{\prime\prime\prime}:\Gamma[\sigma]

(by
H_{0}
)

\displaystyle H_{2}:

\displaystyle\Phi;\Delta^{\prime}\mathrel{\raisebox{0.01pt}{\rule{0.45pt}{6.88889pt}}{\hskip 1.00006pt\vDash}}_{1}^{i}{\delta[\sigma]\circ\delta^{\prime\prime}}\approx\delta^{\prime}[\sigma]\circ\delta^{\prime\prime\prime}:\Delta[\sigma]

(by layering restriction and
\Delta
is well-formed at layer
0
)

\displaystyle H_{3}:

\displaystyle\Phi;\Delta^{\prime}\mathrel{\raisebox{0.01pt}{\rule{0.45pt}{6.88889pt}}{\hskip 1.00006pt\vDash}}_{1}^{0}{\delta[\sigma]\circ\delta^{\prime\prime}}\approx\delta^{\prime}[\sigma]\circ\delta^{\prime\prime\prime}:\Delta[\sigma]

(by
H_{1}
and
H_{3}
)

\displaystyle\Phi;\Delta^{\prime}\mathrel{\raisebox{0.01pt}{\rule{0.45pt}{6.88889pt}}{\hskip 1.00006pt\vDash}}_{1}^{i}{\sigma(u)[\delta[\sigma]\circ\delta^{\prime\prime}]}\approx\sigma(u)[\delta^{\prime}[\sigma]\circ\delta^{\prime\prime\prime}]:T[\sigma[p^{1+u}(\textsf{id})]]

Case

\frac{\Psi\vdash_{1}\Gamma\quad\Psi;\Delta\vdash_{0}t\approx t^{\prime}:T}{\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{1}\texttt{box}\ t\approx{\texttt{box}\ t^{\prime}}:\square(\Delta\vdash T)}

(by IH)

\displaystyle H_{0}:

\displaystyle\Psi;\Delta\Vdash_{0}t\approx t^{\prime}:T

(by assumption)

\displaystyle H_{1}:

\displaystyle\Phi\vDash\sigma:\Psi

(by assumption)

\displaystyle H_{2}:

\displaystyle\Phi;\Delta^{\prime}\mathrel{\raisebox{0.01pt}{\rule{0.45pt}{6.88889pt}}{\hskip 1.00006pt\vDash}}_{1}^{1}{\delta}\approx\delta^{\prime}:\Gamma[\sigma]

(by
H_{2}
, escape and presupposition)

\displaystyle\Phi\vdash_{1}\Delta^{\prime}

(by assumption)

\displaystyle H_{3}:

\displaystyle\Phi;\Delta^{\prime\prime}\mathrel{\raisebox{0.01pt}{\rule{0.45pt}{6.88889pt}}{\hskip 1.00006pt\vDash}}_{1}^{0}{\delta^{\prime\prime}}\approx\delta^{\prime\prime\prime}:\Delta[\sigma]

(by static code)

\displaystyle t=t^{\prime}

(by
H_{0}
,
H_{1}
and
H_{3}
)

\displaystyle\Phi;\Delta^{\prime\prime}\mathrel{\raisebox{0.01pt}{\rule{0.45pt}{6.88889pt}}{\hskip 1.00006pt\vDash}}_{1}^{0}{t[\sigma][\delta^{\prime\prime}]}\approx t^{\prime}[\sigma][\delta^{\prime\prime\prime}]:T[\sigma]

(by definition)

\displaystyle\Phi;\Delta[\sigma]\mathrel{\raisebox{0.01pt}{\rule{0.45pt}{6.88889pt}}{\hskip 1.00006pt\vDash}}_{1}^{1}{{}^{\textsf{Nf}}\texttt{box}\ t[\sigma]}\approx\texttt{box}\ t^{\prime}[\sigma]:T[\sigma]

\displaystyle\Phi;\Delta^{\prime}\vdash_{1}\texttt{box}\ t[\sigma][\delta]\approx{\texttt{box}\ t^{\prime}[\sigma][\delta^{\prime}]}:\square(\Delta\vdash T)[\sigma]

In the last step, notice that local substitutions do not propagate into
box
.

Case

\frac{\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{1}s\approx s^{\prime}:\square(\Delta\vdash T)\quad\Psi\vdash_{0}\Delta\quad\Psi\vdash_{0}T\quad\Psi\vdash_{1}T^{\prime}\quad\Psi,u:(\Delta\vdash T);\Gamma[p(\textsf{id})]\vdash_{1}t\approx t^{\prime}:T^{\prime}[p(\textsf{id})]}{\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{1}\texttt{letbox}\ {u}\shortleftarrow s\ \texttt{in}\ t\approx\texttt{letbox}\ {u}\shortleftarrow s^{\prime}\ \texttt{in}\ t^{\prime}:T^{\prime}}

(by IH)

\displaystyle H_{0}:

\displaystyle\Psi;\Gamma\Vdash_{1}s\approx s^{\prime}:\square(\Delta\vdash T)

(by IH)

\displaystyle H_{1}:

\displaystyle\Psi,u:(\Delta\vdash T);\Gamma[p(\textsf{id})]\Vdash_{1}t\approx t^{\prime}:T^{\prime}[p(\textsf{id})]

(by assumption)

\displaystyle H_{2}:

\displaystyle\Phi\vDash\sigma:\Psi

(by assumption)

\displaystyle H_{3}:

\displaystyle\Phi;\Delta^{\prime}\mathrel{\raisebox{0.01pt}{\rule{0.45pt}{6.88889pt}}{\hskip 1.00006pt\vDash}}_{1}^{1}{\delta}\approx\delta^{\prime}:\Gamma[\sigma]

(by
H_{0}
,
H_{2}
and
H_{3}
)

\displaystyle\Phi;\Delta^{\prime}\mathrel{\raisebox{0.01pt}{\rule{0.45pt}{6.88889pt}}{\hskip 1.00006pt\vDash}}_{1}^{0}{s[\sigma][\delta]}\approx s^{\prime}[\sigma][\delta^{\prime}]:\square(\Delta\vdash T)[\sigma]

Now, we consider
H_{3}
, where we know for some
w
and
w^{\prime}

\displaystyle\Phi;\Delta^{\prime}\vdash_{1}s[\sigma][\delta]\rightsquigarrow^{\ast}w:\square(\Delta[\sigma]\vdash(T[\sigma]))

\displaystyle\Phi;\Delta^{\prime}\vdash_{1}s^{\prime}[\sigma][\delta]\rightsquigarrow^{\ast}w^{\prime}:\square(\Delta[\sigma]\vdash(T[\sigma]))

\displaystyle H_{4}:

\displaystyle\Phi;\Delta^{\prime}\mathrel{\raisebox{0.01pt}{\rule{0.45pt}{6.88889pt}}{\hskip 1.00006pt\vDash}}_{1}^{1}{{}^{\textsf{Nf}}w}\approx w^{\prime}:\square(\Delta[\sigma]\vdash(T[\sigma]))

We then case analyze
H_{4}
:

Subcase

\frac{\Phi;\Delta[\sigma]\vdash_{0}t^{\prime\prime}:T[\sigma]\quad\forall~\Phi^{\prime};\Delta^{\prime\prime}\mathrel{\raisebox{0.01pt}{\rule{0.45pt}{6.88889pt}}{\hskip 1.00006pt\vDash}}_{1}^{0}{\delta^{\prime\prime}}\approx\delta^{\prime\prime\prime}:\Delta[\sigma]~.~\Phi^{\prime};\Delta^{\prime\prime}\mathrel{\raisebox{0.01pt}{\rule{0.45pt}{6.88889pt}}{\hskip 1.00006pt\vDash}}_{1}^{0}{t^{\prime\prime}[\delta^{\prime\prime}]}\approx t^{\prime\prime}[\delta^{\prime\prime\prime}]:T}{\Phi;\Delta^{\prime}\mathrel{\raisebox{0.01pt}{\rule{0.45pt}{6.88889pt}}{\hskip 1.00006pt\vDash}}_{1}^{1}{{}^{\textsf{Nf}}{\texttt{box}\ t^{\prime\prime}}}\approx\texttt{box}\ t^{\prime\prime}:\square(\Delta\vdash T)[\sigma]}

Then we have

(by definition)

\displaystyle H_{5}:

\displaystyle\Phi\vDash\sigma,t^{\prime\prime}/u:\Psi,u:(\Delta\vdash T)

(by
H_{1}
and
H_{5}
)

\displaystyle\Phi;\Delta^{\prime}\mathrel{\raisebox{0.01pt}{\rule{0.45pt}{6.88889pt}}{\hskip 1.00006pt\vDash}}_{1}^{1}{t[\sigma,t^{\prime\prime}/u][\delta]}\approx t^{\prime}[\sigma,t^{\prime\prime}/u][\delta^{\prime}]:T^{\prime}[p(\textsf{id})][\sigma,t^{\prime\prime}/u]

(by computation)

\displaystyle T^{\prime}[p(\textsf{id})][\sigma,t^{\prime\prime}/u]=T^{\prime}[\sigma]

\displaystyle\texttt{letbox}\ {u}\shortleftarrow s\ \texttt{in}\ t[\sigma][\delta]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\texttt{letbox}\ {u}\shortleftarrow s[\sigma][\delta]\ \texttt{in}\ (t[q(\sigma)][\delta[p(\textsf{id})]])

\displaystyle\rightsquigarrow^{\ast}

\displaystyle\texttt{letbox}\ {u}\shortleftarrow\texttt{box}\ t^{\prime\prime}\ \texttt{in}\ (t[q(\sigma)][\delta[p(\textsf{id})]])

\displaystyle\rightsquigarrow

\displaystyle t[q(\sigma)][\delta[p(\textsf{id})]][\textsf{id},t^{\prime\prime}/u]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle t[q(\sigma)\circ(\textsf{id},t^{\prime\prime}/u)][\delta[p(\textsf{id})][\textsf{id},t^{\prime\prime}/u]]

(by computation)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle t[\sigma,t^{\prime\prime}/u][\delta]

(similarly)

\displaystyle\texttt{letbox}\ {u}\shortleftarrow s^{\prime}\ \texttt{in}\ t^{\prime}[\sigma][\delta^{\prime}]\rightsquigarrow^{\ast}t^{\prime}[\sigma,t^{\prime\prime}/u][\delta^{\prime}]

\displaystyle\Phi;\Delta^{\prime}\mathrel{\raisebox{0.01pt}{\rule{0.45pt}{6.88889pt}}{\hskip 1.00006pt\vDash}}_{1}^{1}{\texttt{letbox}\ {u}\shortleftarrow s\ \texttt{in}\ t[\sigma][\delta]}\approx\texttt{letbox}\ {u}\shortleftarrow s^{\prime}\ \texttt{in}\ t^{\prime}[\sigma][\delta]:T^{\prime}[\sigma]

Subcase

\frac{\Phi;\Delta^{\prime}\vdash_{1}v\sim v^{\prime}:\square(\Delta\vdash T)[\sigma]}{\Phi;\Delta^{\prime}\mathrel{\raisebox{0.01pt}{\rule{0.45pt}{6.88889pt}}{\hskip 1.00006pt\vDash}}_{1}^{1}{{}^{\textsf{Nf}}{v}}\approx v^{\prime}:\square(\Delta\vdash T)[\sigma]}

Then we have

(by monotonicity and
Lemma

3.13
)

\displaystyle H_{5}:

\displaystyle\Phi,u:(\Delta[\sigma]\vdash(T[\sigma]))\vDash\sigma[p(\textsf{id})],u^{\textsf{id}}/u:\Psi,u:(\Delta\vdash T)

(by monotonicity)

\displaystyle H_{6}:

\displaystyle\Phi,u:(\Delta[\sigma]\vdash(T[\sigma]));\Delta^{\prime}[p(\textsf{id})]\mathrel{\raisebox{0.01pt}{\rule{0.45pt}{6.88889pt}}{\hskip 1.00006pt\vDash}}_{1}^{1}{\delta[p(\textsf{id})]}\approx\delta^{\prime}[p(\textsf{id})]:\Gamma[\sigma][p(\textsf{id})]

(by computation)

\displaystyle\Gamma[p(\textsf{id})][\sigma[p(\textsf{id})],u^{\textsf{id}}/u]=\Gamma[\sigma[p(\textsf{id})]]=\Gamma[\sigma][p(\textsf{id})]

(by computation)

\displaystyle T^{\prime}[p(\textsf{id})][\sigma[p(\textsf{id})],u^{\textsf{id}}/u]=T^{\prime}[\sigma[p(\textsf{id})]]=T^{\prime}[\sigma][p(\textsf{id})]

(by
H_{1}
,
H_{5}
and
H_{6}
)

\displaystyle\Phi,u:(\Delta[\sigma]\vdash(T[\sigma]));\Delta^{\prime}[p(\textsf{id})]\mathrel{\raisebox{0.01pt}{\rule{0.45pt}{6.88889pt}}{\hskip 1.00006pt\vDash}}_{1}^{1}{t[\sigma[p(\textsf{id})],u^{\textsf{id}}/u][\delta[p(\textsf{id})]]}\approx t^{\prime}[\sigma[p(\textsf{id})],u^{\textsf{id}}/u][\delta^{\prime}[p(\textsf{id})]]:T^{\prime}[\sigma][p(\textsf{id})]

(by
Corollary

3.11
)

\displaystyle\Phi,u:(\Delta[\sigma]\vdash(T[\sigma]));\Delta^{\prime}[p(\textsf{id})]\vdash_{1}t[\sigma[p(\textsf{id})],u^{\textsf{id}}/u][\delta[p(\textsf{id})]]\simeq t^{\prime}[\sigma[p(\textsf{id})],u^{\textsf{id}}/u][\delta^{\prime}[p(\textsf{id})]]:T^{\prime}[\sigma][p(\textsf{id})]

\displaystyle\texttt{letbox}\ {u}\shortleftarrow s\ \texttt{in}\ t[\sigma][\delta]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\texttt{letbox}\ {u}\shortleftarrow s[\sigma][\delta]\ \texttt{in}\ (t[q(\sigma)][\delta[p(\textsf{id})]])

(by computation)

\displaystyle\rightsquigarrow^{\ast}

\displaystyle\texttt{letbox}\ {u}\shortleftarrow v\ \texttt{in}\ (t[q(\sigma)][\delta[p(\textsf{id})]])

(similarly)

\displaystyle\texttt{letbox}\ {u}\shortleftarrow s^{\prime}\ \texttt{in}\ t^{\prime}[\sigma][\delta^{\prime}]\rightsquigarrow^{\ast}\texttt{letbox}\ {u}\shortleftarrow v^{\prime}\ \texttt{in}\ (t^{\prime}[q(\sigma)][\delta^{\prime}[p(\textsf{id})]])

(neutral terms)

\displaystyle\Phi;\Delta^{\prime}\vdash_{1}\texttt{letbox}\ {u}\shortleftarrow v\ \texttt{in}\ (t[q(\sigma)][\delta[p(\textsf{id})]])\sim\texttt{letbox}\ {u}\shortleftarrow v^{\prime}\ \texttt{in}\ (t^{\prime}[q(\sigma)][\delta^{\prime}[p(\textsf{id})]]):T[\sigma]

(by
Lemma

3.13
)

\displaystyle\Phi;\Delta^{\prime}\mathrel{\raisebox{0.01pt}{\rule{0.45pt}{6.88889pt}}{\hskip 1.00006pt\vDash}}_{1}^{1}{\texttt{letbox}\ {u}\shortleftarrow v\ \texttt{in}\ (t[q(\sigma)][\delta[p(\textsf{id})]])}\approx\texttt{letbox}\ {u}\shortleftarrow v^{\prime}\ \texttt{in}\ (t^{\prime}[q(\sigma)][\delta^{\prime}[p(\textsf{id})]]):T[\sigma]

\displaystyle\Phi;\Delta^{\prime}\mathrel{\raisebox{0.01pt}{\rule{0.45pt}{6.88889pt}}{\hskip 1.00006pt\vDash}}_{1}^{1}{\texttt{letbox}\ {u}\shortleftarrow s\ \texttt{in}\ t[\sigma][\delta]}\approx\texttt{letbox}\ {u}\shortleftarrow s^{\prime}\ \texttt{in}\ t^{\prime}[\sigma][\delta]:T^{\prime}[\sigma]

Case

\frac{\Psi\vdash_{1}\Gamma\quad\Psi,g:\textsf{Ctx};\Gamma[p(\textsf{id})]\vdash_{1}t\approx t^{\prime}:T}{\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{1}\Lambda g.t\approx\Lambda g.t^{\prime}:(g:\textsf{Ctx})\Rightarrow T}

(by IH)

\displaystyle H_{0}:

\displaystyle\Psi,g:\textsf{Ctx};\Gamma[p(\textsf{id})]\Vdash_{1}t\approx t^{\prime}:T

(by assumption)

\displaystyle H_{1}:

\displaystyle\Phi\vDash\sigma:\Psi

(by assumption)

\displaystyle H_{2}:

\displaystyle\gamma;\tau:\Phi^{\prime};\Delta^{\prime\prime}\Longrightarrow\Phi;\Delta^{\prime}

\displaystyle\Phi^{\prime};\Delta^{\prime\prime}\mathrel{\raisebox{0.01pt}{\rule{0.45pt}{6.88889pt}}{\hskip 1.00006pt\vDash}}_{1}^{1}{\delta[\gamma;\tau]}\approx\delta^{\prime}[\gamma;\tau]:\Gamma[\sigma][\gamma]

(by

\displaystyle\Phi^{\prime};\Delta^{\prime\prime}\mathrel{\raisebox{0.01pt}{\rule{0.45pt}{6.88889pt}}{\hskip 1.00006pt\vDash}}_{1}^{1}{t[\sigma[\gamma],\Gamma^{\prime}/g][\delta[\gamma;\tau]]}\approx t^{\prime}[\sigma[\gamma],\Gamma^{\prime}/g][\delta^{\prime}[\gamma;\tau]]:T[\sigma[\gamma],\Gamma^{\prime}/g]

\displaystyle\Lambda g.t[\sigma][\delta][\gamma;\tau]~\$~\Gamma^{\prime}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Lambda g.(t[q(\sigma[\gamma])][\delta[\gamma;\tau][p(\textsf{id})]])~\$~\Gamma^{\prime}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle t[\sigma[\gamma],\Gamma^{\prime}/g][\delta[\gamma;\tau]]

(similarly)

\displaystyle\Lambda g.t^{\prime}[\sigma][\delta^{\prime}][\gamma;\tau]~\$~\Gamma^{\prime}\rightsquigarrow t^{\prime}[\sigma[\gamma],\Gamma^{\prime}/g][\delta^{\prime}[\gamma;\tau]]

\displaystyle\Phi^{\prime};\Delta^{\prime\prime}\mathrel{\raisebox{0.01pt}{\rule{0.45pt}{6.88889pt}}{\hskip 1.00006pt\vDash}}_{1}^{1}{\Lambda g.t[\sigma][\delta][\gamma;\tau]~\$~\Gamma^{\prime}}\approx\Lambda g.t^{\prime}[\sigma][\delta^{\prime}][\gamma;\tau]~\$~\Gamma^{\prime}:T[\sigma[\gamma],\Gamma^{\prime}/g]

\displaystyle\Phi;\Delta^{\prime}\mathrel{\raisebox{0.01pt}{\rule{0.45pt}{6.88889pt}}{\hskip 1.00006pt\vDash}}_{1}^{1}{\Lambda g.t[\sigma][\delta]}\approx\Lambda g.t^{\prime}[\sigma][\delta^{\prime}]:(g:\textsf{Ctx})\Rightarrow T[\sigma]

Case

\frac{\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{1}t\approx t^{\prime}:(g:\textsf{Ctx})\Rightarrow T\quad\Psi\vdash_{0}\Delta}{\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{1}t~\$~\Delta\approx t^{\prime}~\$~\Delta:T[\textsf{id}_{\Psi},\Delta/g]}

(by IH)

\displaystyle H_{0}:

\displaystyle\Psi;\Gamma\Vdash_{1}t\approx t^{\prime}:(g:\textsf{Ctx})\Rightarrow T

(by assumption)

\displaystyle H_{1}:

\displaystyle\Phi\vDash\sigma:\Psi

(by assumption)

\displaystyle H_{2}:

\displaystyle\Phi;\Delta^{\prime}\mathrel{\raisebox{0.01pt}{\rule{0.45pt}{6.88889pt}}{\hskip 1.00006pt\vDash}}_{1}^{i}{\delta}\approx\delta^{\prime}:\Gamma[\sigma]

\displaystyle\Phi;\Delta^{\prime}\mathrel{\raisebox{0.01pt}{\rule{0.45pt}{6.88889pt}}{\hskip 1.00006pt\vDash}}_{1}^{1}{t[\sigma][\delta]}\approx t^{\prime}[\sigma][\delta^{\prime}]:(g:\textsf{Ctx})\Rightarrow T[\sigma]

\displaystyle\Phi\vdash_{0}\Delta[\sigma]

\displaystyle\Phi;\Delta^{\prime}\mathrel{\raisebox{0.01pt}{\rule{0.45pt}{6.88889pt}}{\hskip 1.00006pt\vDash}}_{1}^{1}{t[\sigma][\delta]~\$~\Delta[\sigma]}\approx t^{\prime}[\sigma][\delta^{\prime}]~\$~\Delta[\sigma]:T[\sigma][\textsf{id},\Delta[\sigma]/g]

(by computation)

\displaystyle T[\sigma][\textsf{id},\Delta[\sigma]/g]=T[\textsf{id},\Delta/g][\sigma]

The goal is then concluded.

Case

\frac{\Psi\vdash_{i}\Gamma\quad\text{$\Gamma$ ends with $\cdot$}\quad|\Gamma|=m}{\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{i}\cdot^{m}\approx\cdot^{m}:\cdot}

(by assumption)

\displaystyle\Phi\vDash\sigma:\Psi

(by assumption)

\displaystyle\Phi;\Delta^{\prime}\mathrel{\raisebox{0.01pt}{\rule{0.45pt}{6.88889pt}}{\hskip 1.00006pt\vDash}}_{1}^{i}{\delta}\approx\delta^{\prime}:\Gamma[\sigma]

We must compare
\cdot^{m}[\sigma]\circ\delta=\cdot_{\widecheck{\delta}}^{\widehat{\delta}}
and
\cdot^{m}[\sigma]\circ\delta^{\prime}=\cdot_{\widecheck{\delta^{\prime}}}^{\widehat{\delta^{\prime}}}
.
But they are immediately equal, as we can show that

\Phi;\Delta^{\prime}\mathrel{\raisebox{0.01pt}{\rule{0.45pt}{6.88889pt}}{\hskip 1.00006pt\vDash}}_{1}^{i}{\delta}\approx\delta^{\prime}:\Gamma[\sigma]
implies

\widecheck{\delta}=\widecheck{\delta^{\prime}}
and
\widehat{\delta}=\widehat{\delta^{\prime}}
.

Case

\frac{\Psi\vdash_{i}\Gamma\quad g:\textsf{Ctx}\in\Psi\quad\text{$\Gamma$ ends with $g$}\quad|\Gamma|=m}{\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{i}\cdot_{g}^{m}\approx\cdot_{g}^{m}:\cdot}

(by assumption)

\displaystyle\Phi\vDash\sigma:\Psi

(by assumption)

\displaystyle\Phi;\Delta^{\prime}\mathrel{\raisebox{0.01pt}{\rule{0.45pt}{6.88889pt}}{\hskip 1.00006pt\vDash}}_{1}^{i}{\delta}\approx\delta^{\prime}:\Gamma[\sigma]

We look up
\sigma(g)
and consider what it ends with.

Subcase

\sigma(g)
ends with
\cdot
. Then we must compare

\cdot_{g}^{m}[\sigma]\circ\delta=\cdot_{\widecheck{\delta}}^{\widehat{\delta}}
and

\cdot_{g}^{m}[\sigma]\circ\delta^{\prime}=\cdot_{\widecheck{\delta^{\prime}}}^{\widehat{\delta^{\prime}}}
,
which we know are equal.

Subcase

\sigma(g)
ends with some
g^{\prime}
. Then we must compare

\cdot_{g}^{m}[\sigma]\circ\delta=\cdot_{g^{\prime}}^{\widehat{\delta}}
and

\cdot_{g}^{m}[\sigma]\circ\delta^{\prime}=\cdot_{g^{\prime}}^{\widehat{\delta^{\prime}}}
,
which we know are also equal.

Case

\frac{\Psi\vdash_{i}\Gamma\quad g:\textsf{Ctx}\in\Psi\quad\text{$\Gamma$ ends with $g$}\quad|\Gamma|=m}{\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{i}\textsf{wk}_{g}^{m}\approx\textsf{wk}_{g}^{m}:g}

(by assumption)

\displaystyle\Phi\vDash\sigma:\Psi

(by assumption)

\displaystyle H_{0}:

\displaystyle\Phi;\Delta^{\prime}\mathrel{\raisebox{0.01pt}{\rule{0.45pt}{6.88889pt}}{\hskip 1.00006pt\vDash}}_{1}^{i}{\delta}\approx\delta^{\prime}:\Gamma[\sigma]

Then we have

\textsf{wk}_{g}^{m}[\sigma]\circ\delta=\textsf{id}_{\sigma(g)}[id;p^{m}(\textsf{id})]\circ\delta=\textsf{wk}^{m}_{\sigma(g)}\circ\delta

and

\textsf{wk}_{g}^{m}[\sigma]\circ\delta^{\prime}=\textsf{id}_{\sigma(g)}[id;p^{m}(\textsf{id})]\circ\delta^{\prime}=\textsf{wk}^{m}_{\sigma(g)}\circ\delta^{\prime}

We show
\Phi;\Delta^{\prime}\mathrel{\raisebox{0.01pt}{\rule{0.45pt}{6.88889pt}}{\hskip 1.00006pt\vDash}}_{1}^{i}{\textsf{wk}^{m}_{\sigma(g)}\circ\delta}\approx\textsf{wk}^{m}_{\sigma(g)}\circ\delta^{\prime}:\sigma(g)

by unraveling
H_{0}

m
times.

∎

As a corollary of the fundamental theorems, we can prove the completeness theorem:

Proof of

3.24
.

Notice that the reducibility predicates are just special cases of the validity
judgments.
∎

3.4.
Convertibility Checking

In this section, we will write down the converibility checking rules and instantiate
the generic equivalence with it, proving that equivalence terms can be checked. We define three judgments:
\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{1}t\ \hat{\Longleftrightarrow}\ t^{\prime}:T
checks the convertibility of two terms

t
and
t^{\prime}
.
\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{1}w\Longleftrightarrow w^{\prime}:T
checks the convertibility of two normal forms
w
and
w^{\prime}
. This operation is directed by types.
\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{1}v\longleftrightarrow v^{\prime}:T
checks the convertibility of two neutral forms
v
and
v^{\prime}
. This operation is structural on the neutral forms. We give all the rules below:

\frac{\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{1}t\rightsquigarrow^{\ast}w:T\quad\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{1}t^{\prime}\rightsquigarrow^{\ast}w^{\prime}:T\quad\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{1}w\Longleftrightarrow w^{\prime}:T}{\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{1}t\ \hat{\Longleftrightarrow}\ t^{\prime}:T}\par\frac{\Psi\vdash_{1}\Gamma}{\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{1}\textsf{zero}\Longleftrightarrow\textsf{zero}:\texttt{Nat}}\par\frac{\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{1}t\ \hat{\Longleftrightarrow}\ t^{\prime}:\texttt{Nat}}{\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{1}\textsf{succ}~t\Longleftrightarrow\textsf{succ}~t^{\prime}:\texttt{Nat}}\par\frac{\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{1}v\longleftrightarrow v^{\prime}:\texttt{Nat}}{\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{1}v\Longleftrightarrow v^{\prime}:\texttt{Nat}}\par\frac{\Psi;\Gamma,x:S\vdash_{1}w[\textsf{id};p(\textsf{id})]~x\ \hat{\Longleftrightarrow}\ w^{\prime}[\textsf{id};p(\textsf{id})]~x:T}{\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{1}w\Longleftrightarrow w^{\prime}:S\longrightarrow T}\par\frac{\Psi\vdash_{1}\Gamma\quad\Psi;\Delta\vdash_{0}t:T}{\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{1}\texttt{box}\ t\Longleftrightarrow\texttt{box}\ t:\square(\Delta\vdash T)}\par\frac{\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{1}v\longleftrightarrow v^{\prime}:\square(\Delta\vdash T)}{\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{1}v\Longleftrightarrow v^{\prime}:\square(\Delta\vdash T)}\par\frac{\Psi,g:\textsf{Ctx};\Gamma\vdash_{1}w[p(\textsf{id})]~\$~g\ \hat{\Longleftrightarrow}\ w^{\prime}[p(\textsf{id})]~\$~g:T}{\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{1}w\Longleftrightarrow w^{\prime}:(g:\textsf{Ctx})\Rightarrow T}\par\frac{\Psi\vdash_{1}\Gamma\quad x:T\in\Gamma}{\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{1}x\longleftrightarrow x:T}\par\frac{\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{1}\delta\Longleftrightarrow\delta^{\prime}:\Delta\quad x:(\Delta\vdash T)\in\Psi}{\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{1}u^{\delta}\longleftrightarrow u^{\delta^{\prime}}:T}\par\frac{\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{1}v\longleftrightarrow v^{\prime}:S\longrightarrow T\quad\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{1}t\ \hat{\Longleftrightarrow}\ t^{\prime}:S}{\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{1}v~t\longleftrightarrow v^{\prime}~t^{\prime}:T}\par\frac{\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{1}v\longleftrightarrow v^{\prime}:\square(\Delta\vdash T)\quad\Psi\vdash_{1}T^{\prime}\quad\quad\Psi,u:(\Delta\vdash T);\Gamma[p(\textsf{id})]\vdash_{1}t\ \hat{\Longleftrightarrow}\ t^{\prime}:T^{\prime}[p(\textsf{id})]}{\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{1}\texttt{letbox}\ {u}\shortleftarrow v\ \texttt{in}\ t\longleftrightarrow\texttt{letbox}\ {u}\shortleftarrow v^{\prime}\ \texttt{in}\ t^{\prime}:T^{\prime}}\par\frac{\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{1}v\longleftrightarrow v^{\prime}:(g:\textsf{Ctx})\Rightarrow T\quad\Psi\vdash_{0}\Delta}{\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{1}v~\$~\Delta\longleftrightarrow v^{\prime}~\$~\Delta:T[\textsf{id},\Delta/g]}

We then instantiate the generic equivalence. We instantiate
\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{1}t\simeq t^{\prime}:T
with

\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{1}t\ \hat{\Longleftrightarrow}\ t^{\prime}:T
and
\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{1}t\sim t^{\prime}:T
with
\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{1}t\longleftrightarrow t^{\prime}:T
.

Most laws are immediate. We discuss a few of them.

Lemma 3.31 (PERs).

All three relations above are PERs.

Proof.

When we prove transitivity of
\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{1}t\ \hat{\Longleftrightarrow}\ t^{\prime}:T
, we use the uniqueness of
multi-step weak head reduction.
∎

Lemma 3.32 (Congruence of
box
).

If
\Psi\vdash_{1}\Gamma
,
\Psi;\Delta\vdash_{0}t:T
and
\Psi;\Delta\vdash_{1}t\ \hat{\Longleftrightarrow}\ t:T
,
then
\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{1}\texttt{box}\ t\ \hat{\Longleftrightarrow}\ \texttt{box}\ t:T
.

Proof.

Notice that we are almost there, except that we must prove

\Psi;\Delta\vdash_{1}t\ \hat{\Longleftrightarrow}\ t:T
for
t
at layer
0
. This premise is met due to our layered model, where we instantiate layer
0
and
layer
1
separately, so that the fundamental theorem of layer
0
gives

\Psi;\Delta\vdash_{1}t\ \hat{\Longleftrightarrow}\ t:T
.
∎

A successful instantiation gives us the following desired completeness theorem for
converibility checking:

Theorem 3.33 (Completeness).

•

If
\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{1}t\approx t^{\prime}:T
, then
\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{1}t\ \hat{\Longleftrightarrow}\ t^{\prime}:T
.

•

If
\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{1}\delta\approx\delta^{\prime}:\Delta
, then
\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{1}\delta\ \hat{\Longleftrightarrow}\ \delta^{\prime}:\Delta
.

Soundness is easy by a simple induction:

Theorem 3.34 (Soundness).

•

If
\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{1}t\ \hat{\Longleftrightarrow}\ t^{\prime}:T
, then
\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{1}t\approx t^{\prime}:T
.

•

If
\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{1}w\Longleftrightarrow w^{\prime}:T
, then
\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{1}w\approx w^{\prime}:T
.

•

If
\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{1}v\longleftrightarrow v^{\prime}:T
, then
\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{1}v\approx v^{\prime}:T
.

•

If
\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{1}\delta\ \hat{\Longleftrightarrow}\ \delta^{\prime}:\Delta
, then
\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{1}\delta\approx\delta^{\prime}:\Delta
.

This concludes our discussion about context variables.

4.
Dependent Layered Modal Type Theory

In this section, we combine the work by
Hu and Pientka (2024a)
and what we have built
up in the previous sections and scale all the way up to dependent types. We present
DeLaM
,
De
pendent
La
yered
M
odal Type
Theory. With dependent types, we can not only analyze the syntax of programs, but also that of
types. This ability, therefore, gives us the power to write tactics that could potentially
fill in proof obligations in a proof environment. In particular, this type theory addresses a number of problems that we often see in
proof assistants like Coq, Lean, and Agda. In Coq, tactics are written in a separate language, Ltac or Ltac2, where the
advantages of dependent types in Gallina, the core language, are lost. Stratifying the tactic language and the core language into two also cause
duplications: there are multiple notions for natural numbers, functions, etc.. On the other hand, in Lean and Agda, we use reflection to convert a Lean or an Agda
term into an AST and then use the core language to manipulate the AST. An instrumentation in the kernel is responsible for converting this AST back to a
valid term, if type-checked. This mechanism superficially provides a uniform way to tactics, but reflection
generally fails to guarantee the well-formedness of ASTs, making type malformedness
run-time errors and necessitating exception mechanisms exclusively for macro executions.

We believe that this type theory provides an example to address all aforementioned
problems. Starting this section, let us dive into dependent types.

4.1.
Highlights

On a high level, we would continue to apply the layering principle in
DeLaM
to
guide us in the design of this type theory. In particular, we would want the layer for static code to be subsumed by layer for
computing programs. Moreover, with context variables, we are now able to formulate a recursive
elimination principle for code, which was not possible in simple types. However, these two requirements combining together causes some high-level technical
effects to the design of the type theory, which are worth mentioning before presenting the
type theory itself.

4.1.1.
Code Promotion

Since we are going to introduce elimination principle for terms with dependent types,
we must also consider how equivalences are handled for code. For example, if we know a given piece of code has type
(\lambda x.x)~\texttt{Nat}
, should
we regard this type the “same” as
Nat
? Intuitively, the answer should be yes. After all, we only want to capture the syntax of the term, not its type. Effectively, for code of type
\square(\Gamma\vdash T)
, it should also be regarded as an inhabitant
of another type
\square(\Gamma\vdash T^{\prime})
, as long as
T\approx T^{\prime}
in
\Gamma
.

In the context of dependent types, however, that causes some problems in the presence
of function applications. Consider a function
f:\Pi(x:S).T
and an argument
t:S
. Then in general, the type of
f~t
is
T[t/x]
. Now, let us construct this term as code. Even though
t
is constructed as part of the code, the type of the overall code

\square(\Gamma\vdash(T[t/x]))
contains
t
and therefore part of the dynamics of
t
is in fact
promoted to the type. For a more concrete example, if
f:\Pi(x:\texttt{Ty}_{0}).x
and let the argument be

(\lambda x.x)~\texttt{Nat}
, then
\texttt{box}\ (f~((\lambda x.x)~\texttt{Nat}))
has type

\square(\Gamma\vdash(\lambda x.x)~\texttt{Nat})
, which we agree is just
\square(\Gamma\vdash\texttt{Nat})
. Clearly, the argument computes and is not purely static code as in simple types. We cannot avoid this phenomenon because of dependent types, so we must handle it with
care. This phenomenon is call a
code promotion
.

4.1.2.
Non-cumulativity

Due to code promotions, we must permit non-trivial equivalences in types of code. This causes particular problems when we want an elimination principle for code with
intensional analyses. When we split code into cases in the elimination form, we must specify in each case
how do we construct the original code from its components. Therefore, it is the most convenient, when each term has a unique type, leading to a
conclusion of preferring non-cumulative universes. Whereas with cumulative universes, types live in higher universes for free. Cumulativity forms a pre-order of types which cannot be captured solely by equivalence
rules and makes the typing rule for the elimination principle extremely difficult to express, if
not completely impossible.

4.1.3.
Universe Polymorphism

Though it is often omitted in other work, universe levels and universe polymorphism
are important ingredients in dependent type theory. They are typically considered as “details” and are not very much paid attention
to. However, in this work, we must be explicit about universe polymorphism. Consider some code for function application
t~s
. We in general do not know the type of
s
, let alone its universe level (though it
must be uniquely determined due to non-cumulativity discussed above). Therefore, the elimination principle for code must work for
all
universe
levels, leading to a formalism of universe polymorphism.

4.1.4.
Tarski-style Universes

Another ingredient to consider when approaching an elimination principle for code with
dependent types and intensional analyses is the separation between types and terms. Consider Russell-style universes where types and terms are not distinguished
naturally. It would probably suffice to say that
\square(\Gamma\vdash T)
can represent code for both types and
terms. In particular, code for some types just has type
\square(\Gamma\vdash\texttt{Ty}_{0})
, for example. Unfortunately, this thought is too naive. When we consider
\square(\Gamma\vdash T)
as the type of code, we are considering this type with two
indices, the (local) context
\Gamma
and the type of the code body
T
. But what is the type of
T
? Well, it is
\square(\Gamma\vdash\texttt{Ty}_{l})
for some
l
, which is just a special case of
\square(\Gamma\vdash T)
! A type clearly should not be indexed by a special case of itself. The intertwine between types and terms in Russell-style universes seems to even
prevent a proper statement of indices of types for code. However, Tarski-style universes, where types and terms are clearly distinguished, introduce
mutually inductive relations between types and terms, and safely bail us out of this
problem, as we will see very soon.

4.2.
Syntax

Let us start with the syntax of
DeLaM
. Since we employ Tarski-style universes, the syntax of terms and types are separate. Due to non-cumulativity, certain constructs must remember universe levels. Due to the elimination principle of code, some constructs must include additional
sub-structures so that the elimination eventually checks out. Let us begin with the subset that is basically just Martin-Löf type theory (MLTT).

(Local variables)

\displaystyle x,y

(Universe variables)

\displaystyle\ell

(Universe levels)

\displaystyle l

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\ \ell\;|\;0\;|\;1+l\;|\;l\sqcup l^{\prime}\;|\;\omega

(Types,
Typ
)

\displaystyle M,S,T

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\ \texttt{Nat}\;|\;\Pi^{l,l^{\prime}}(x:S).T\;|\;\texttt{Ty}_{l}\;|\;\overrightarrow{\ell}\Rightarrow^{l}T\;|\;\texttt{El}^{l}~t

(Terms,
Exp
)

\displaystyle s,t

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\ x

(encoding of types)

\displaystyle\;|\;\texttt{Nat}\;|\;\Pi^{l,l^{\prime}}(x:s).t\;|\;\texttt{Ty}_{l}

(natural numbers
N
)

\displaystyle\;|\;\textsf{zero}\;|\;\textsf{succ}~t\;|\;\textsf{elim}_{\texttt{Nat}}^{l}~(x.M)~s~(x,y.s^{\prime})~t

(dependent functions)

\displaystyle\;|\;\lambda^{l,l^{\prime}}(x:S).t\;|\;(t:\Pi^{l,l^{\prime}}(x:S).T)~s

(universe polymorphic functions)

\displaystyle\;|\;\Lambda^{l}~{\overrightarrow{\ell}}.t\;|\;t~\$~\overrightarrow{l}

(Local contexts,
Ctx
)

\displaystyle\Gamma,\Delta

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\ \cdot\;|\;g\;|\;\Gamma,x:T\scalebox{0.75}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}$\,@~l$}

(Universe contexts)

\displaystyle L

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\cdot\;|\;L,\ell

Due to three kinds of contexts and the scale of the system, we omit the discussion of
weakenings, which we diligently kept track of in the previous sections. We take various weakenings for granted. Nevertheless, they will appear in the semantics. Weakenings in general are pretty obvious, as we permit arbitrary lookups for all
variables. Since we now must deal with universe levels, we use
\ell
to range over variables for
universe levels. The syntax for universe levels follows Agda’s conventions. Universe levels form an idempotent commutative monoid, the laws of which we will show
in the next subsection. Here we use
\sqcup
to denote taking the max of two universe levels. The ability to take maximum between two levels induces a partial order:

\displaystyle l\leq l^{\prime}:=l^{\prime}\approx l\sqcup l^{\prime}

where we use
\approx
to express the equivalence between universe levels. Thus, with
\leq
, universe levels form a bounded inf-lattice. A strict order is given by requiring the pre-order to hold for the successor of
l
:

\displaystyle l<l^{\prime}:=l^{\prime}\approx(1+l)\sqcup l^{\prime}

This strict order, as to be shown later, is well-founded, based on which we will give
semantics to universes. Due to universe polymorphism, we must also include an
\omega
level, which will be
used to represent the universe level of a universe-polymorphic function. The
\omega
level must not appear in any program, does not participate in the bounded
inf-lattice specified above, is only used in type-checking, and therefore can be
ignored most of the time. The formalization of universe polymorphism here follows
Bezem
et al. (2023)

tightly. We use
\overrightarrow{\ell}\Rightarrow^{l}T
to denote the type of a universe-polymorphic function. It introduce a non-empty list of universe variables at once, and lives at
\texttt{Ty}_{\omega}
. The type
T
lives at
\texttt{Ty}_{l}
, where
l
may refer to all variables from
\overrightarrow{\ell}
. Since
l
cannot be
\omega
, we must have all universe variables introduced in one
go. The introduction form is
\Lambda^{l}~{\overrightarrow{\ell}}.t
, which also introduces a non-empty list of
universe variables first and then the function body as expected. The elimination form
t~\$~\overrightarrow{l}
symmetrically eliminates a universe-polymorphic
function with the same number of universe level expressions.

The rest of the expressions are pretty much standard from MLTT. We have natural numbers (
Nat
), their introduction forms and a recursion principle. We always use
M
to exclusively represent the motives of a recursion principle. For regular dependent functions
\Pi^{l,l^{\prime}}(x:S).T
, we must specify the universe levels
of
S
and
T
, following
Pujet and
Tabareau (2023)
. We might omit the universe levels if they are not important in the discussion. The function abstraction
\lambda^{l,l^{\prime}}(x:S).t
is standard; we might omit
l
and
S
if they
are not important or can be inferred from the context. The function application
(t:\Pi^{l,l^{\prime}}(x:S).T)~s
is arguably more complex. We explicitly specify the type of the function to prepare for a better formulation of
the elimination principle for code later in the section. By requiring explicit type annotations in elimination forms, types that are usually
hidden in the core syntax become
sub-structures in the elimination form for code. This verbosity has no negative impact for programmers: after all, we are discussing a core theory, and
we can let a type-inferring front-end to fill in these types for the users if they
choose so. Following conventions, we may simply write
s~t
if the types are not important.

Since we are employing Tarski-style universes, as we have specified in the syntax,
types and terms are separated. As terms, we use
encodings
of types, i.e. the overloaded
Nat
,

\Pi^{l,l^{\prime}}(x:s).t
and
\texttt{Ty}_{l}
, which are members of some universes. They are
decoded
into actual types through
\texttt{El}^{l}~t
, converting the encodings
to actual corresponding types. This part is basically identical to
Palmgren (1998)
. For simplicity, we have omitted the type lifter, which is responsible for raising the
universe levels explicitly, similar to
Lift
in Agda’s standard library. According
Palmgren (1998)
, the type lifter bears additional
equivalences and therefore we omit them here for conciseness, as lifting of the levels
is an orthogonal issue here.

4.3.
Universe Levels

In the syntax, we deliberately group all the universe variables into a separate
context. This is beneficial as both local and global contexts (to be discussed later) will need to refer
to universe variables. It is also helpful for the future work of extending
DeLaM
to more
layers, by simply inserting more contexts after
L
. In this section, we state the well-formedness and equivalence judgments for universe
levels. Note that all syntactically valid universe contexts are already well-formed as they
only contain universe variables.

\frac{\ell\in L}{L\vdash\ell:\textsf{Level}}\par\frac{}{L\vdash 0:\textsf{Level}}\par\frac{L\vdash l:\textsf{Level}}{L\vdash 1+l:\textsf{Level}}\par\frac{L\vdash l:\textsf{Level}\quad L\vdash l^{\prime}:\textsf{Level}}{L\vdash l\sqcup l^{\prime}:\textsf{Level}}

Notice that
\omega
is not well-formed. Indeed, the judgment
L\vdash l:\textsf{Level}
only captures the well-formed universe levels
that can be written by a programmer. The level
\omega
, on the other hand, only appears during type-checking to denote the
type of universe-polymorphic functions.

As discussed above, universe levels themselves form an idempotent commutative
monoid. Hence they have the following equivalence rules:

\frac{L\vdash l:\textsf{Level}}{L\vdash l\approx l:\textsf{Level}}\par\frac{L\vdash l\approx l^{\prime}:\textsf{Level}}{L\vdash l^{\prime}\approx l:\textsf{Level}}\par\frac{L\vdash l\approx l^{\prime}:\textsf{Level}\quad L\vdash l^{\prime}\approx l^{\prime\prime}:\textsf{Level}}{L\vdash l\approx l^{\prime\prime}:\textsf{Level}}

First we specify the basic PER rules. Then we have congruence rules:

\frac{L\vdash l\approx l^{\prime}:\textsf{Level}}{L\vdash 1+l\approx 1+{l^{\prime}}:\textsf{Level}}\par\frac{L\vdash l\approx l^{\prime}:\textsf{Level}\quad L\vdash l^{\prime\prime}\approx l^{\prime\prime\prime}:\textsf{Level}}{L\vdash l\sqcup l^{\prime\prime}\approx l^{\prime}\sqcup l^{\prime\prime\prime}:\textsf{Level}}

Finally we have the algebraic rules:

\frac{L\vdash l:\textsf{Level}}{L\vdash l\sqcup 0\approx l:\textsf{Level}}\par\frac{L\vdash l:\textsf{Level}\quad L\vdash l^{\prime}:\textsf{Level}\quad L\vdash l^{\prime\prime}:\textsf{Level}}{L\vdash(l\sqcup l^{\prime})\sqcup l^{\prime\prime}\approx l\sqcup(l^{\prime}\sqcup l^{\prime\prime}):\textsf{Level}}\par\frac{L\vdash l:\textsf{Level}\quad L\vdash l^{\prime}:\textsf{Level}}{L\vdash l\sqcup l^{\prime}\approx l^{\prime}\sqcup l:\textsf{Level}}\par\frac{L\vdash l:\textsf{Level}}{L\vdash l\sqcup l\approx l:\textsf{Level}}\par\frac{L\vdash l:\textsf{Level}\quad L\vdash l^{\prime}:\textsf{Level}}{L\vdash 1+(l\sqcup l^{\prime})\approx 1+l\sqcup 1+{l^{\prime}}:\textsf{Level}}\par\frac{\ell\in L}{L\vdash\ell\sqcup(1+\ell)\approx 1+\ell:\textsf{Level}}

The second last rule is distributivity of
succ
over
\sqcup
. The last rule is absorption of
succ
over
\sqcup
. The equivalence judgment confirms the well-formedness of both components:

Lemma 4.1 (Presupposition).

If
L\vdash l\approx l^{\prime}:\textsf{Level}
, then
L\vdash l:\textsf{Level}
and
L\vdash l^{\prime}:\textsf{Level}
.

Proof.

Induction.
∎

We can prove a more general absorption rule by doing a few inductions.

Lemma 4.2 (Absorption).

If
L\vdash l:\textsf{Level}
, then
L\vdash l\sqcup(1+l)\approx 1+l:\textsf{Level}
.

Proof.

Induction. Only the following case is interesting:

\frac{L\vdash l:\textsf{Level}\quad L\vdash l^{\prime}:\textsf{Level}}{L\vdash l\sqcup l^{\prime}:\textsf{Level}}

We reason as follows:

\displaystyle l\sqcup l^{\prime}\sqcup(1+{l\sqcup l^{\prime}})

\displaystyle\approx(l\sqcup(1+l))\sqcup(l^{\prime}\sqcup(1+{l^{\prime}}))

(by IH)

\displaystyle\approx(1+l)\sqcup(1+{l^{\prime}})

\displaystyle\approx 1+{(l\sqcup l^{\prime})}

Hence the proof is complete.
∎

Then we generalize further:

Lemma 4.3 (Absorption).

If
L\vdash l:\textsf{Level}
, then for any
n
,
L\vdash l\sqcup(n+{l})\approx n+{l}:\textsf{Level}
.

Proof.

We proceed by induction on
n
. The cases for
n=0
and
n=1
are simple. We consider the step case, where

n=1+n^{\prime}
and we know

L\vdash l\sqcup(n^{\prime}+{l})\approx n^{\prime}+{l}:\textsf{Level}
. We reason as follows:

(by IH)

\displaystyle l\sqcup(1+(n^{\prime}+l))

\displaystyle\approx l\sqcup(1+{(l\sqcup(n^{\prime}+{l}))})

\displaystyle\approx l\sqcup(1+l)\sqcup(1+n^{\prime}+{l})

(by absorption)

\displaystyle\approx(1+l)\sqcup(1+n^{\prime}+{l})

\displaystyle\approx(1+l)\sqcup(n^{\prime}+(1+l))

(by IH)

\displaystyle\approx n^{\prime}+{(1+l)}

\displaystyle\approx n+l

The proof is complete.
∎

As readers might have noticed, the theory for universe levels are self-contained and
their equivalence is decidable, as per implemented by Agda’s type-checker. For this reason, in the remainder of the discussion, we undermine the importance of
well-formedness and equivalence of universe levels, unless it is essential in the
surrounding context.

Next, we define substitutions for universe levels:

(Substitutions for universe levels)

\displaystyle\phi:=\cdot\;|\;\phi,l/\ell

\frac{}{L\vdash\cdot:\cdot}\par\frac{L\vdash\phi:L^{\prime}\quad L\vdash l:\textsf{Level}}{L\vdash\phi,l/\ell:L^{\prime},\ell}

Applying substitutions is intuitive:

\displaystyle\ell[\phi]

\displaystyle:=\phi(\ell)

\displaystyle 0[\phi]

\displaystyle:=0

\displaystyle 1+l[\phi]

\displaystyle:=1+(l[\phi])

\displaystyle l\sqcup l^{\prime}[\phi]

\displaystyle:=(l[\phi])\sqcup(l^{\prime}[\phi])

\displaystyle\omega[\phi]

\displaystyle:=\omega

We then have the following lemmas:

Lemma 4.4.

If
L\vdash l:\textsf{Level}
and
L^{\prime}\vdash\phi:L
, then
L^{\prime}\vdash l[\phi]:\textsf{Level}
.

Proof.

Induction.
∎

Lemma 4.5.

If
L\vdash l\approx l^{\prime}:\textsf{Level}
and
L^{\prime}\vdash\phi:L
, then
L^{\prime}\vdash l[\phi]\approx l^{\prime}[\phi]:\textsf{Level}
.

Proof.

Induction.
∎

The well-foundedness of the strict order
<
is intuitive. The only bottom element is
0
. We simply keep removing
succ
from all components of
\sqcup
, and we must
eventually stop. Thus, the simplest way to argue the well-foundedness of
<
is to define a measure
based on the number of
succ
’s that can be removed from an
l
. This number is defined over all
L\vdash l:\textsf{Level}
recursively as follows:

\displaystyle\textsf{count}(\ell)

\displaystyle:=\{\ell\mapsto 0,0\mapsto 0\}

\displaystyle\textsf{count}(0)

\displaystyle:=\{0\mapsto 0\}

\displaystyle\textsf{count}(1+l)

\displaystyle:=\textsf{merge}(\{\ell\mapsto 1+n\;|\;\ell\mapsto n\in\textsf{count}(l)\},\{0\mapsto 1+\textsf{count}(l)(0)\})

\displaystyle\textsf{count}(l\sqcup l^{\prime})

\displaystyle:=\textsf{merge}(\textsf{count}(l),\textsf{count}(l^{\prime}))

Here
count
returns a map that counts the number of
succ
’s over all
universe variables and
0
. The function
merge
merges two maps and takes the maximum in a conflict. The following definition makes sure equivalent universe levels to have the same
representation as maps:

\displaystyle\textsf{adjust}(m):=\begin{cases}\{\ell\mapsto n\;|\;m(\ell)\mapsto n\}&\text{if $m(0)\leq\max_{\ell\mapsto n\in m}n$}\\
m&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

In the first branch, we check if there is a variable which has a higher universe level
than the constant. If so, we drop the constant completely. For example, in
2\sqcup(2+\ell)
,
2
is
redundant, as we know
2+\ell
is at least as large as
2
. On the other hand, in
2\sqcup(1+\ell)
, it is possible for
1+\ell

to be smaller than
2
when we take
\ell
as
0
. Therefore, in this case, we must keep
2
. Thus, the finiteness of
decreasing steps of the universe levels can be seen as taking some finite steps by
removing all
1+
from maps returned by
\textsf{adjust}(\textsf{count}(l))
. Then we can just take away variables until we can no longer descend. In fact,
\textsf{adjust}(\textsf{count}(l))
should be considered a normalization algorithm for
universe levels. We can simply compare equality between maps computed as such decide whether two
universe levels are equivalent. The correctness is as follows:

Lemma 4.6.

If
L\vdash l\approx l^{\prime}:\textsf{Level}
, then
\textsf{adjust}(\textsf{count}(l))=\textsf{adjust}(\textsf{count}(l^{\prime}))
.

Proof.

Induction. Take advantage of the idempotent commutative monoidal nature of maximum.
∎

This lemma ensures that the procedure respects equivalence between universe levels. The other direction is seen by providing a “decoding” function, which converts a map
to a universe level. We give one possible function that converts a map to a
Level
.

\displaystyle\textsf{flatten}_{L}(m):=\begin{cases}\bigsqcup_{\ell\mapsto n\in m}n+\ell&\text{if $0$ is not in $m$}\\
m(0)\sqcup(\bigsqcup_{\ell\mapsto n\in m}n+\ell)&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

where the order of
\ell
’s respects their order in
L
and all
\sqcup
are right
associative. These requirements give a syntactically unique flattening of a map. Then we prove

Lemma 4.7.

If
L\vdash l:\textsf{Level}
, then
\Gamma\vdash l\approx\textsf{flatten}_{L}(\textsf{adjust}(\textsf{count}(l))):\textsf{Level}
.

Proof.

We proceed by induction. It is rather immediate. For the
succ
case, we use its distributivity to
propagate it inwards. For the
\sqcup
case, we use commutativity to rearrange levels
within and absorption to eliminate small levels whenever necessary.
∎

4.4.
Typing and Equivalence Judgments

In this section, we introduce the typing and equivalence judgments, only for the MLTT
part. We will consider the modal part next altogether. The typing and equivalence judgments are defined mutually as usual. All the related judgments are:

•

L\vdash\Psi
denotes the well-formedness of the global context
\Psi
under

L
.

•

L\;|\;\Psi\vdash_{i}\Gamma
denotes the well-formedness of
\Gamma
given the universe
context
L
and the global context
\Psi
at layer
i
. In this section, we are not very concerned about layers yet as most parts about
meta-programming and intensional analysis come in a later section
(
Sec.

4.5
).

•

L\;|\;\Psi\vdash_{i}\Gamma\approx\Delta
denotes the equivalence between two local contexts

\Gamma
and
\Delta
.

•

L\;|\;\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{i}T\scalebox{0.75}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}$\,@~l$}
denotes the well-formedness of the type
T
living in the
universe level
l
at layer
i
in the given contexts.

•

L\;|\;\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{i}t:T\scalebox{0.75}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}$\,@~l$}
denotes the well-typedness of
t
of type
T
, which is in
the universe level
l
at layer
i
.
In the special occasion of
T
being some
Ty
, we might write
L\;|\;\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{i}T:\texttt{Ty}_{l}\;(\scalebox{0.75}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}$\,@~1+l$})
to simultaneously denote two judgments at the same time to save space.

•

L\;|\;\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{i}T\approx T^{\prime}\scalebox{0.75}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}$\,@~l$}
denotes the equivalence between types
T
and
T^{\prime}
living in the
universe level
l
at layer
i
in the given contexts.

•

L\;|\;\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{i}t\approx t^{\prime}:T\scalebox{0.75}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}$\,@~l$}
denotes the equivalence between
t
and
t^{\prime}
of type

T
, which is in the universe level
l
at layer
i
. The shorthand
L\;|\;\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{i}T\approx T^{\prime}:\texttt{Ty}_{l}\;(\scalebox{0.75}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}$\,@~1+l$})
has a meaning similar to above.

•

L\;|\;\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{i}\delta:\Delta
denotes the well-formedness of a local substitution

\delta
which substitute all local variables in
\Delta
into terms referring to

\Gamma
. We will introduce this judgment and the next when we discuss the modal part.

•

L\;|\;\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{i}\delta\approx\delta^{\prime}:\Delta
denotes the equivalence between local
substitutions
\delta
and
\delta^{\prime}
.

The judgments for MLTT are rather routine. Many are just generalization of the judgments in
Sec.

2
.
Let us first consider the well-formedness and equivalence of local contexts:

\frac{L\vdash\Psi}{L\;|\;\Psi\vdash_{i}\cdot\\
L\;|\;\Psi\vdash_{i}\cdot\approx\cdot}\par\frac{L\vdash\Psi\quad g:\textsf{Ctx}\in\Psi}{L\;|\;\Psi\vdash_{i}g\\
L\;|\;\Psi\vdash_{i}g\approx g}\par\frac{L\;|\;\Psi\vdash_{i}\Gamma\quad\quad L\;|\;\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{i}T\scalebox{0.75}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}$\,@~l$}\quad L\vdash l:\textsf{Level}}{L\;|\;\Psi\vdash_{i}\Gamma,x:T\scalebox{0.75}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}$\,@~l$}}\par\frac{L\;|\;\Psi\vdash_{i}\Gamma\approx\Delta\quad L\;|\;\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{i}T\approx T^{\prime}\scalebox{0.75}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}$\,@~l$}\quad L\;|\;\Psi;\Delta\vdash_{i}T\approx T^{\prime}\scalebox{0.75}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}$\,@~l$}\quad L\;|\;\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{i}T\scalebox{0.75}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}$\,@~l$}\quad L\;|\;\Psi;\Delta\vdash_{i}T^{\prime}\scalebox{0.75}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}$\,@~l^{\prime}$}\quad L\vdash l\approx l^{\prime}:\textsf{Level}}{L\;|\;\Psi\vdash_{i}\Gamma,x:T\scalebox{0.75}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}$\,@~l$}\approx\Delta,x:T^{\prime}\scalebox{0.75}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}$\,@~l^{\prime}$}}

The well-formedness of types are also immediate, following
Pujet and
Tabareau (2023)
. When we encounter
El
, we resort that to the typing judgment of terms. Overlapping rules for well-typedness of encoding as terms are also listed:

\frac{L\;|\;\Psi\vdash_{\Uparrow(i)}\Gamma}{L\;|\;\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{i}\texttt{Nat}:\texttt{Ty}_{0}\;(\scalebox{0.75}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}$\,@~1$})}\par\frac{L\vdash l:\textsf{Level}\quad L\vdash l^{\prime}:\textsf{Level}\quad\quad L\;|\;\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{i}S\scalebox{0.75}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}$\,@~l$}\quad L\;|\;\Psi;\Gamma,x:S\scalebox{0.75}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}$\,@~l$}\vdash_{i}T\scalebox{0.75}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}$\,@~l^{\prime}$}}{L\;|\;\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{i}\Pi^{l,l^{\prime}}(x:S).T\scalebox{0.75}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}$\,@~l\sqcup l^{\prime}$}}\par\frac{L\vdash l:\textsf{Level}\quad L\vdash l^{\prime}:\textsf{Level}\quad\quad L\;|\;\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{i}s:\texttt{Ty}_{l}\scalebox{0.75}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}$\,@~1+l$}\quad L\;|\;\Psi;\Gamma,x:\texttt{El}^{l}~s\scalebox{0.75}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}$\,@~l$}\vdash_{i}t:\texttt{Ty}_{l^{\prime}}\scalebox{0.75}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}$\,@~1+l^{\prime}$}}{L\;|\;\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{i}\Pi^{l,l^{\prime}}(x:s).t:\texttt{Ty}_{l\sqcup l^{\prime}}\scalebox{0.75}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}$\,@~1+{(l\sqcup l^{\prime})}$}}\par\frac{L\vdash l:\textsf{Level}\quad\quad L\;|\;\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{i}t:\texttt{Ty}_{l}\scalebox{0.75}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}$\,@~1+l$}}{L\;|\;\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{i}\texttt{El}^{l}~t\scalebox{0.75}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}$\,@~l$}}\par\frac{L\;|\;\Psi\vdash_{\Uparrow(i)}\Gamma\quad L\vdash l:\textsf{Level}}{L\;|\;\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{i}\texttt{Ty}_{l}:\texttt{Ty}_{1+l}\;(\scalebox{0.75}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}$\,@~2+l$})}\par\frac{L\;|\;\Psi\vdash_{\textsc{m}}\Gamma\quad L,\overrightarrow{\ell}\;|\;\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{1}T\scalebox{0.75}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}$\,@~l$}\quad\quad|\overrightarrow{\ell}|>0\quad L,\overrightarrow{\ell}\vdash l:\textsf{Level}}{L\;|\;\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{\textsc{m}}\overrightarrow{\ell}\Rightarrow^{l}T\scalebox{0.75}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}$\,@~\omega$}}\par\frac{L\;|\;\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{i}T\scalebox{0.75}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}$\,@~l^{\prime}$}\quad\quad L\vdash l\approx l^{\prime}:\textsf{Level}}{L\;|\;\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{i}T\scalebox{0.75}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}$\,@~l$}}

Note that all type constructors with explicitly specified universe levels must not
refer to
\omega
. Indeed,
\omega
level only appears when we validate a universe-polymorphic function
and nowhere else. Nor can we pass around a universe-polymorphic function. Moreover, universe-polymorphic functions are only available at the highest layer, which is the
layer with capability to do meta-programming and recursive intensional analysis. This is because that universe variables must also be visible by the bindings in global
contexts. In the well-formedness rule for
Nat
, we use a function
\Uparrow(i)
which alters the
layer, in which local contexts live. This treatment is necessary to accommodate code promotions and permit computation in the
local contexts and on the type level. We give the actual definition of
\Uparrow(i)
in
Sec.

4.5
.

The equivalence between types is composed of three parts. The first part is the PER rules.

\frac{L\;|\;\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{i}T\approx T^{\prime}\scalebox{0.75}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}$\,@~l$}}{L\;|\;\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{i}T^{\prime}\approx T\scalebox{0.75}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}$\,@~l$}}\par\frac{L\;|\;\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{i}T\approx T^{\prime}\scalebox{0.75}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}$\,@~l$}\quad L\;|\;\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{i}T^{\prime}\approx T^{\prime\prime}\scalebox{0.75}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}$\,@~l$}}{L\;|\;\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{i}T\approx T^{\prime\prime}\scalebox{0.75}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}$\,@~l$}}

Then we have the congruence rules, which simply straightforwardly propagate
equivalence downwards:

\frac{L\;|\;\Psi\vdash_{\Uparrow(i)}\Gamma}{L\;|\;\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{i}\texttt{Nat}\approx\texttt{Nat}:\texttt{Ty}_{0}\;(\scalebox{0.75}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}$\,@~1$})}\par\frac{L\vdash l_{1}\approx l_{3}:\textsf{Level}\quad L\vdash l_{2}\approx l_{4}:\textsf{Level}\quad\quad L\;|\;\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{i}S\approx S^{\prime}\scalebox{0.75}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}$\,@~l_{1}$}\quad L\;|\;\Psi;\Gamma,x:S\scalebox{0.75}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}$\,@~l_{1}$}\vdash_{i}T\approx T^{\prime}\scalebox{0.75}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}$\,@~l_{2}$}}{L\;|\;\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{i}\Pi^{l_{1},l_{2}}(x:S).T\approx\Pi^{l_{3},l_{4}}(x:S^{\prime}).T^{\prime}\scalebox{0.75}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}$\,@~l_{1}\sqcup l_{2}$}}\par\frac{L\vdash l_{1}\approx l_{3}:\textsf{Level}\quad L\vdash l_{2}\approx l_{4}:\textsf{Level}\quad\quad L\;|\;\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{i}s\approx s^{\prime}:\texttt{Ty}_{l_{1}}\scalebox{0.75}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}$\,@~1+l_{1}$}\quad L\;|\;\Psi;\Gamma,x:\texttt{El}^{l_{1}}~s\scalebox{0.75}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}$\,@~l_{1}$}\vdash_{i}t\approx t^{\prime}:\texttt{Ty}_{l_{2}}\scalebox{0.75}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}$\,@~1+l_{2}$}}{L\;|\;\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{i}\Pi^{l_{1},l_{2}}(x:s).t\approx\Pi^{l_{3},l_{4}}(x:s^{\prime}).t^{\prime}:\texttt{Ty}_{l_{1}\sqcup l_{2}}\scalebox{0.75}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}$\,@~1+{(l_{1}\sqcup l_{2})}$}}\par\frac{L\vdash l\approx l^{\prime}:\textsf{Level}\quad L\;|\;\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{i}t\approx t^{\prime}:\texttt{Ty}_{l}\scalebox{0.75}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}$\,@~1+l$}}{L\;|\;\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{i}\texttt{El}^{l}~t\approx\texttt{El}^{l^{\prime}}~t^{\prime}\scalebox{0.75}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}$\,@~l$}}\par\frac{L\;|\;\Psi\vdash_{\Uparrow(i)}\Gamma\quad L\vdash l\approx l^{\prime}:\textsf{Level}}{L\;|\;\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{i}\texttt{Ty}_{l}\approx\texttt{Ty}_{l^{\prime}}:\texttt{Ty}_{1+l}\;(\scalebox{0.75}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}$\,@~2+l$})}\par\frac{L,\overrightarrow{\ell}\;|\;\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{\textsc{m}}T\scalebox{0.75}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}$\,@~l$}\quad L,\overrightarrow{\ell}\;|\;\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{\textsc{m}}T\approx T^{\prime}\scalebox{0.75}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}$\,@~l$}\quad\quad|\overrightarrow{\ell}|>0\quad L,\overrightarrow{\ell}\vdash l\approx l^{\prime}:\textsf{Level}}{L\;|\;\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{\textsc{m}}\overrightarrow{\ell}\Rightarrow^{l}T\approx\overrightarrow{\ell}\Rightarrow^{l^{\prime}}T^{\prime}\scalebox{0.75}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}$\,@~\omega$}}\par\frac{L\;|\;\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{i}T\approx T^{\prime}\scalebox{0.75}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}$\,@~l^{\prime}$}\quad L\vdash l\approx l^{\prime}:\textsf{Level}}{L\;|\;\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{i}T\approx T^{\prime}\scalebox{0.75}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}$\,@~l$}}

Finally, we have a number of computation rules that decode terms into types:

\frac{L\;|\;\Psi\vdash_{\Uparrow(i)}\Gamma}{L\;|\;\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{i}\texttt{Nat}\approx\texttt{El}^{0}~\texttt{Nat}\scalebox{0.75}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}$\,@~0$}}\par\frac{L\vdash l:\textsf{Level}\quad L\;|\;\Psi\vdash_{\Uparrow(i)}\Gamma}{L\;|\;\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{i}\texttt{Ty}_{l}\approx\texttt{El}^{1+l}~\texttt{Ty}_{l}\scalebox{0.75}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}$\,@~1+l$}}\par\frac{L\vdash l:\textsf{Level}\quad L\vdash l^{\prime}:\textsf{Level}\quad L\;|\;\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{i}s:\texttt{Ty}_{l}\scalebox{0.75}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}$\,@~1+l$}\quad L\;|\;\Psi;\Gamma,x:\texttt{El}^{l}~s\scalebox{0.75}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}$\,@~l$}\vdash_{i}t:\texttt{Ty}_{l^{\prime}}\scalebox{0.75}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}$\,@~1+l^{\prime}$}}{L\;|\;\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{i}\Pi^{l,l^{\prime}}(x:\texttt{El}^{l}~s).\texttt{El}^{l^{\prime}}~t\approx\texttt{El}^{l\sqcup l^{\prime}}~\Pi^{l,l^{\prime}}(x:s).t\scalebox{0.75}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}$\,@~l\sqcup l^{\prime}$}}

We do not have an encoding for universe-polymorphic functions, so there is not a
decoding rule for them.

Then we move on to defining the typing rules for terms. They are pretty much straightforward:

The equivalence rules for terms are also composed of three parts. The PER rules are immediate:

\frac{L\;|\;\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{i}t\approx t^{\prime}:T\scalebox{0.75}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}$\,@~l$}}{L\;|\;\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{i}t^{\prime}\approx t:T\scalebox{0.75}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}$\,@~l$}}\par\frac{L\;|\;\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{i}t\approx t^{\prime}:T\scalebox{0.75}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}$\,@~l$}\quad L\;|\;\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{i}t^{\prime}\approx t^{\prime\prime}:T\scalebox{0.75}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}$\,@~l$}}{L\;|\;\Psi;\Gamma\vdash_{i}t\approx t^{\prime\prime}:T\scalebox{0.75}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}$\,@~l$}}

The congruence rules are naturally induced by the typing rules:

4.5.
Meta-programming Modalities

In this part, we introduce the modalities for meta-programming and intensional
analysis. We use the
\square
modality to represent the type of
code and we use layers to control the computational behaviors of the type theory. However, there are two points that we need to pay attention to:

(1)

Previously, we have discussed code promotions. Code promotions imply that we must permit computation of code on the type level. This further implies that we must introduce an intermediate layer between layer for code and
that for meta-programs, which restricts the language to still MLTT but permits computation.

(2)

Due to Tarski-style universes, we must introduce two kinds of contextual types,
one for types and one for terms. As seen in the syntax of types and terms, they are mutually defined, so the
recursive principles for code of types and terms must also be mutual.

c

d

m

Language

MLTT

MLTT

MLTT extended with meta-programming

Computation

No

No

Yes

Yes

Meta-programming

No

No

No

Yes

Layer of types

Features at each layer
